A Higher-Order Lambda Calculus:
PowerEpsilon
Ming-Yuan Zhu
CoreTek Systems, Inc.
1107B CEC Building
6 South Zhongguancun Street
Beijing 100086
People’s Republic of China
E-Mail: zhumy@mail.coretek.com.cn

Abstract
In recent years, a number of formulations of intuitionistic type theory have been
considered as a basis for studying machine-assisted formal proof development
and as a theoretical foundation for the study of programming languages. In this
book, a higher-order lambda calculus – PowerEpsilon is presented. PowerEpsilon is both a strongly-typed polymorphic functional programming language
based on Martin-Löf’s Type Theory and Calculus of Constructions (CC) by
Coquand and Huet, and a proof development system much similar to other mechanical proof development systems such as LCF and Nuprl. PowerEpsilon
may be viewed as a higher-order λ-calculus associated, via the propositionsas-types principle, with natural deduction proofs in an extension of Church’s
higher-order logic. The system has been proved both proof-theoretically and
model-theoretically consistent, and the type checking problem has been proved
decidable. The new contribution of this work is that we have introduced the
concept of limit of type universe hierarchies (Kind) and a scheme for inductive
(recursive) defined types.

“It is by logic that we prove, but by intuition that we invent.”
— H. Poincare

“Thoughts without content are empty, intuitions without concepts
are blind.”
— I. Kant

Chapter 1

Introduction
In mathematical logic, formal languages in which we can express substantial
parts of mathematics have long history, going back to Frege’s formulation in
1872 of the predicate logic. So, if we want a programming language in which
it should be possible not just to write down programs but also to reason about
them, an obvious attempt is to see whether any of the formalization used for
mathematics could also be used for programming.

1.1

What is PowerEpsilon

PowerEpsilon is a strongly typed functional programming language based on
higher-order type theory. The basic ideas of PowerEpsilon are taken from
Martin-Löf ’s Type Theory and Calculus of Constructions (CC) invented
by Coquand and Huet.
PowerEpsilon is a proof development system. It has a very rich type
system which can be used as a mathematical language to represent higher-order
predicate calculus, and therefore to formulate theories of mathematics.
PowerEpsilon is a formal specification language which can be used for
knowledge representations.

1.2

Martin-Löf ’s Type Theory

Martin-Löf’s type theory [Mar 75, Mar 82, Mar 84] has been developed with
the aim of being a formalization of constructive mathematics. Its rules are
formulated in the style of Gentzen’s natural deduction system for predicate
logic; a formal system which Gentzen set up in 1936 with the intention that
it should be as close as possible to actual reasoning. Martin-Löf has suggested
that type theory also could be viewed as a programming language. As such it is
a typed functional language without assignments or other imperative features.
1

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION

2

Compared with other programming languages, it has a very rich type structure
in that a type of a program can be used to describe what the program should
do without describing how the program performs its task. The language differs
from other programming languages in that all programs terminate. General
recursion is not available, but primitive recursion together with functions of
higher-order makes it possible to express all provably total recursive functions.
In order to express the task of the program in the type system, it is necessary to be able to express properties of programs. Properties can be expressed
as propositions (formulae), using predicate logic. In recent years, a number
of new type theories has been proposed such as the Calculus of Constructions
(CC) by Coquand and Huet [CoqH 88]. These theories make it possible for us
to formally treat some “higher-order” concepts such as “proposition”, “axiom”,
“rule”, “transformation”, “problem”, and “proof strategy” etc in one uniform
framework. Normally, a software development environment only describe a special kind of “proposition” or “rule”, but can not treat them in a more abstract
way. Based on these theories, a number of new approaches for software development have been proposed [Const 86].
In this report we describe the core of a strongly-typed polymorphic functional programming language called PowerEpsilon. Our original motivation
came from the problem of finding a strongly-typed language suitable for use as
a metalanguage for manipulating programs, proofs, and other similar symbolic
data. PowerEpsilon can be used as both a programming language with a very
rich set of data structures and a system for formalizing constructive mathematics.

1.3

Why is System Called PowerEpsilon

One of the hardest questions to answer is that why the system is called PowerEpsilon. We have two choice to answer the question. Either we may view
PowerEpsilon just as a name standing for something which has no meaning at
all, so we call the system PowerEpsilon just because it is a new name which
is never used by other systems before; or we may give an interpretation to the
PowerEpsilon with the following two reasons
• A classical representation of natural numbers is the power set representation. The order of the logic systems described by PowerEpsilon can be
represented as the power set over kinds starting from Prophierarchy.
• ²0 is minimal ordinals which satisfies the property
ω

²0 = sup{ω, ω ω , ω ω ω , ω ω ω , . . .}
which is corresponding to Kind in PowerEpsilon.

Chapter 2

λ-Calculus
The λ-calculus and its variable-free equivalent, combinatory logic, were initiated
around 1930 by Church, Schonfinkel and Curry [Chur 41, CurF 58, CurHS 72]
respectively. The intention of the founders of the subject was to study computation rules; in other words to study the notion of “function” in the sense of its
evaluation. The study was based on a type free structure, where the objects of
study are at same time functions and arguments. In particular, a function can
be applied to itself (recursive function). For the usual conception of a function
in mathematics (in Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory) this is impossible (because of
the axiom of foundation).
The λ-calculus represents a class of (partial) functions (λ-definable functions)
on the integers which turns out to be the class of (partial) recursive functions.
The equivalence between the Turing computable functions and the general recursive functions was originally proved via the λ-calculus: the general recursive
functions are exactly the λ-definable functions as are the Turing computable
functions.
Historically the first undecidable problem was constructed by Church as a
problem about terms of the λ-calculus (whether they have a normal form). The
first definition, due to Church and Kleene, of the recursive originals went via
the λ-calculus. The fixed point theorem of the λ-calculus inspired Kleene to the
recursion theorem [Klee 52]. Thus the λ-calculus played a central role in the
early investigations of the theory of recursive functions.
Church originally designed the λ-calculus as part of a general system of
functions intended to be a foundation of mathematics. The paradox of Kleene
and Rosser showed that this system was inconsistent. The present theory was
extracted as a consistent subtheory.
Due to the type free approach it was not clear how to construct models
of the theory. One would want a set X in which its function X → X can be
embedded. This difficulty was overcome by Scott in his D∞ model constructions
[Scot 70, Scot 75, Scot 76], by restricting X → X to the continuous functions
3

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

4

on X (provided with a proper topology).
For more detailed description on λ-calculus, please refer to [Bar 84].

2.1

Abstract Syntax of λ-Calculus

From an intuitive standpoint, λ-terms (expressions) can be viewed as notations
for functions in the form of rule governing their input/output behaviour. Functions with an arbitrary finite number of arguments can be reduced to singleargument functions if we allow functions as values. So a sufficient expressive
formalism is obtained by introducing two primitive operations: function abstraction and application.
The set Λ of terms is defined by the following abstract syntactic rules:
M ::= x | λx.M | (M N );
Where M , N range over elements of Λ and x is an unspecified element of a
denumerable set of variables. λx.M is a constructor, called λ-abstraction, to
denote new functions. A λ-abstraction is a particular sort of expression which
denotes a function. The λ says “here comes a function”, and is immediately
followed by a variable, then the body of the function. The variable is called
formal parameter, and we say that λ binds it. The function application is
denoted by simple juxtaposition (M N ).

2.1.1

Function Application and Currying

Following the standard conventions, we assume that application associates to
the left, so a term of the form
M1 M2 M3 . . . Mn
is an abbreviation for
((. . . ((M1 M2 )M3 ) . . .)Mn ).
This allows us to think of all functions as having a single argument only. This
technique is called currying.
On the other hand, λ-abstraction associates to the right so
(λx1 .(λx2 .(. . . (λxn .M ) . . .)))
can be written as
λx1 .λx2 . . . . λxn .M.

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

2.2

Basic Concepts of λ-Calculus

2.2.1

Evaluation Theory of λ-Calculus

5

The primary purpose of the λ-calculus was to study evaluation or execution;
it is a paradigm for evaluation, in the same way that the predicate calculus is
a paradigm for deduction. More recently, and largely due to Scott, the model
theory of the λ-calculus has grown and has been harmonized with its evaluation
theory.
Definition 2.1 (Free-Variables) The concept of free-variables is defined as follow:
1. x occurs free in x (but not in any other variables).
2. x occurs free in (E1 E2), if it occurs free in E1 or E2.
3. x occurs free in λy.E if x and y are different variables and x occurs free in E.
4. x occurs free in (E) if it occurs free in E.
We denote by FV(M ) the set of free variables in term M .
Definition 2.2 (Bounded-Variables) The concept of bounded-variables is defined
as follow:
1. No variable occurs bound in an expression consisting just a single variable.
2. x occurs bound in (E1 E2), if it occurs bound in E1 or E2.
3. x occurs bound in λy.E if x and y are the same variables or if x occurs bound
in E.
4. x occurs bound in (E) if it occurs bound in E.

Definition 2.3 (Substitution) Let x be a variable and E1 and E2 expressions. Then E2[E1/x] is the expression E 0 defined as follows:
1. E2 is a variable:
(a) If E2 = x then E 0 = E1.
(b) If E2 6= x then E 0 = E2.
2. E2 is an application P (Q):
(a) E 0 = (P [E1/x] Q[E1/x]).
3. E2 is an abstraction λy.E.
(a) If y = x then E 0 = E2.
(b) If y 6= x then
i. If x does not occur free in E or if y does not occur free in E1
then E 0 = λx.E[E1/x]
ii. If x does occur free in E and y does occur in E1 then E 0 =
λz.E[z/y][E1/x], where z is the first variable in the sequence of
variables such that z does not occur free in E1 or E.

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

6

performing transformations on λ-expressions.

We write ⇐⇒, where ⇐⇒ = ⇐⇒ ∪ ⇐⇒ ∪ ⇐⇒, to indicate that either side may
be replaced by the other whenever one of them occurs as an expression or as a
subexpression of a larger expression.
Reduction
In practice, we use conversion rules to evaluate λ-expressions, which means that
we try to eliminate as many abstractions as possible. α-rule does not help in
this, but the other two rules, when used in the left-right direction, both replace
an expression containing an abstraction with some other expression which may
well be simpler. For this reason, this kind of conversion is called a reduction and
the particular expression which is replaced is called a redex. So an expression
of the form (λx.M N ) is a β-redex, and λx.(M x) is an η-redex (if x is not
free in M ). Since the α-rule is only a renaming rule which can be performed
by identifying terms which differ only in the names of their bound variables, we
can assume, following [Bar 84], that this operation is carried out in the metalanguage, without explicit mention of it. When indicating a reduction we often
βη
βη
λ
use the symbol −→instead of ⇐⇒. We usually write −→
→as the reflexive and
βη
transitive closure of −→.
We have now developed three conversion rules which allow us to interconvert
expressions involving λ-abstractions. They are
• Name changing. α-conversion allows us to change the name of the formal
parameter of a λ-abstraction.
• Function application. β-reduction allows us to apply a λ-abstraction to
an argument, by making a new instance of the body of the abstraction,
substituting the argument for free occurrences of the formal parameter.
Special care needs to be taken when the argument contains free variables.
• Elimination redundant λ-abstractions. η-reduction can sometimes eliminate a λ-abstraction.

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

7

Normal Form
If the final result of the reduction of an expression contains no redexs, then such
expression is said to be in normal form.
Weak Head Normal Form
A λ-expression is in weak head normal form (WHNF) if and only if it is of the
form
(F E1 E2 . . . En )
where n ≥ 0; and either F is a variable or F is a λ-abstraction and (F E1 . . . Em )
is not a redex for any m ≤ n. An expression has no top-level redex if and only
if it is in weak head normal form.
Head Normal Form
A λ-expression is in head normal form (HNF) if and only if it is of the form
λx1 .λx2 . . . λxn .(vM1 M2 . . . Mm )
where n, m ≥ 0; v is a variable and (vM1 M2 . . . Mp ) is not a redex for any
p ≤ m.
Anything is WHNF is also in HNF, but not vice versa.
Normalizable
βη

M is normalizable if there exists an N in normal form with M −→
→N .
Strongly Normalizable
M is strongly normalizable if there is no infinite sequence of reductions
βη

βη

βη

M −→M1 −→M2 −→ . . .
starting at M .
For example, the term Ω ≡ (λx.(x x)λx.(x x)) is neither strongly normalizable nor normalizable because the only possible reduction sequence starting at
Ω is
βη
βη
βη
Ω−→Ω−→Ω−→ . . . ,
and the term (K I Ω), where K ≡ λx.λy.x and I ≡ λx.x is not strongly
normalizable but is normalizable with normal form I. The latter example shows
that there can be alternative reduction sequence starting at a term containing
more than one redex, and some of them may reach a normal form while others
do not. So one may ask: can two different reduction sequences lead to different
normal forms? Fortunately the answer is “no”, due to the following theorems.

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

λ

Theorem 2.1 (Church-Rosser I) If X ⇐⇒Y then there exists an expression
βη

βη

Z such that X −→Z and Y −→Z.
Corollary 2.2 No expression can be converted to two distinct normal forms.

2.2.2

Reduction Order

An expression may contain more than one redex, so reduction can proceed by
alternative routes. Furthermore, some reduction sequences may reach a normal
form while others do not.
Normal Order Reduction
The normal order reduction is the reduction in which at each stage reduces the
left-most redex in the expression.
In normal order no expression in the argument position of a β-redex is evaluated until the redex has itself been reduced, which might eliminate the argument
from the expression altogether. So this reduction is also called lazy evaluation.
With normal order reduction, the leftmost reduction is always performed
first. Normal order reduction is guaranteed to terminate with a normal form if
any order of evaluation does.
βη

Theorem 2.3 (Church and Rosser II) If A−→
→B, and B is a normal form,
then there exists a normal order reduction from A to B.
Applicative Order Reduction
In the applicative order reduction, the operator and operand (function and argument) of an application (β-redex) are separately evaluated to normal form
before the β-reduction is performed. So this reduction is also called eager evaluation.
The applicative order reduction consisting of always performing the leftmost
inner-most reduction first.
Although, by the first Church-Rosser Theorem, we know that whenever the
reduction terminates the applicative order reduction will give the same result
as normal order reduction. However, it is less powerful than normal order
reduction, since it may fail to terminate in some case where the normal order will
terminate. However, when it does terminate it is often faster than normal order.
This is because applicative order evaluates operands only once, before they are
substituted into the body of the operator, whereas normal order evaluates them
as many times as necessary, after the substitution. But this is also the secret
of normal order reduction’s extra power: for “as many times as necessary”
sometimes means “never”.

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

9

Let us consider what happens if there is a choice:
(λx.λy.y (λx.(x x) λx.(x x)) b)
By attacking the leftmost β-redex, we can reduce this to (λy.y b) and hence b.
On the other hand if we attack the other β-redex our attempts at evaluation
may never terminate.
The rules of normal and applicative order reduction (which partly resemble
the “call-by-name” and “call-by-value” rules of Algol 60) can form the basis
of mechanistic definitions of the semantics of the λ-calculus; the two versions
will define slightly different languages, reflecting the different power of the two
abstract computation engines.
Weak Head Normal Form Reduction
The weak head normal form reduction is to reduce the top-level redex (there
can only be one such) until weak head normal form is reached. The weak head
WH
WH
formal form reduction is denoted by −→ and −→
→ is the reflexive and transitive
WH
closure of −→.

2.3

Definition of Fixed Points

2.3.1

Fixed Point Combinator Y

A function can be defined recursively. A typical example is the factorial function
described as follows:
fact = λn. if n = 0 then 1 else n ∗ (fact (n − 1) fi;
This is an equation which we have to try to solve for fact, just as x = 5/x is
an equation to be solved for x. The existence of an equation, however, does

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

11

not by itself guarantee the existence of a solution; x = x + 3, for example, has
no solution in the real numbers. Let us see if this particular equation has a
solution.
Suppose we put
H ≡ λf.λn. if n = 0 then 1 else n ∗ (f (n − 1)) fi;
Then we are seeking an expression for factthat satisfies
fact = (H fact)
So factis to be a fixed point of H.
In general a fixed point of a function is a value in the function’s domain
which is mapped to itself by that function. Considering functions from integers
to integers we have, for example, that the fixed point of λx.27 is 27, and the
fixed point of λx.6 − x is 3. Not all such functions have exactly one fixed point,
for example, λx.x + 1 has none, while λx.x has infinitely many.
We can define a general form of fixed point combinator Y as follows:
Y ≡ λh.(λx.(h (x x))(λx.(h (x x))
which, applied to any expression, renders a fixed point of that expression.

2.3.2

Paradoxes of Y

There is a fundamental difficulty caused by the combinator Y involving the application of x to itself. The possibility of self-application can lead to paradoxes.
For example, suppose we define
U ≡ λy. if (y y) = a then b else a fi;
Then an attempt to evaluate (U U ) gives
(U U ) = if (U U ) = a then b else a fi;
which is a contradiction.

2.4

Domain Theory and Mathematical Model of
λ-Calculus

For the last two decade, the λ-calculus has been used by computer scientists for
defining the formal semantics of programming languages. However, for seeking
a successful semantic theory of the λ-calculus, we must find a way to restrict
the class of functions we are allowed to consider. Otherwise there will be simply

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

12

too many of them for us to find any non-trivial space with an isomormorphic
function space.
As we have mentioned above that a function may have more than one fixed
point, so the question arises of which fixed point Y produces. The answer is to
be found in domain theory, and the solution produced by (Y H) turns out to
be the unique least fixpoint of H, where ‘least’ is used in a technical domaintheoretical sense.
The domain theory has been developed by Scott [Scot 75, Scot 76], which
provides an elegant mathematical model for λ-calculus [Stoy 77].

2.5

Functional Programming Language

2.5.1

LISP

The programming language LISP is perhaps the first practical language based
on the λ-calculus model.
LISP contributed a great deal to our understanding of programming language theory. LISP is sufficiently simple to permit the development of relatively tractable mathematical model. McCarthy [McC 62] used this model as
a starting point for the development of a mathematical theory of computation.
He considered many of the basic theoretical programming language issues such
as mathematical semantics, proof of program correctness (including compiler
correctness) and proofs of program equivalence (by recursive induction) several
years before they were considered by anyone else.

2.6

Implementation Techniques for λ-Calculus

A central issue on implementation of functional programming languages is how
to implement them more efficient. For this purpose, many abstract machine
models have been found out in order to transform a functional program to a
program in a lower-level language, close to a conventional machine language.
Among them, three typical models are investigated. They are Landin’s SECD
machine model [Lan 63, Lan 65], Graph Reduction Machine model and Turner’s
combinator model [Tur 79].

2.6.1

Landin’s SECD Machine

The SECD machine invented by Landin is a mechanism for implementation of
functional programming languages.
SECD derives its name from the designation of its four principal registers.
S (Stack): Used to hold intermediate results when computing the values of
expression.

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS

13

E (Environment): Used to hold the values bound to variables during evaluation.
C (Control list): Used to hold the machine-language program being executed.
D (Dump): Used as a stack to save values of other registers on calling a new
function.
The entire state of the machine can be denoted by giving the content of its
registers. Thus, each instruction in the machine can be described by giving the
state of the machine before and after its execution. This is called a machine
transition.
With this mechanism, we can build a compiler for a given functional language
based on λ-calculus model. The compiler will translate an expression written
in that language to a sequence of SECD code. Then this program can be run
in the SECD machine efficiently.

2.6.2

Graph Reduction Machine Model

The technique of evaluating a functional language program by rewriting an
expression where the expression is represented in (directed, acyclic) graph form
is, not unnaturally, known as graph reduction. An impressive implementation
of a functional language that uses a compilation approach to graph reduction
is described in [Johnss 83]. Where a compiler for a functional language FC, a
subset of ML, operates by compiling each function, viewed as a rewrite rule, to
a sequence of instructions for an abstract graph reduction machine, called Gmachine. The G-machine code for a function manipulates the graph to reduce
a function application to its value. The G-code is then translated into sequences
of native code for VAX-11 that directly perform the required rewrites. The
resulting implementation is very fast indeed.

2.6.3

Turner’s Combinator Approach

Turner’s approach is to compile a functional program to machine code consisting entirely of combinators from combinatory logic. The compiled program then
consists entirely of an expression built from applications of a fixed set of combinators. Therefore the resulting form does not involve variables at all. There is
a set of rewrite rules for certain combinator configurations that is fixed across
all programs so evaluation consists simply of reducing the combinator expression using these rules. The simplicity of this idea has prompted a hardware
realization in the form of a sequential combinator reduction machine [Clar 80].
The simplest set of combinators can be defined as follows (so called SKI
machine):
S

≡

λx.λy.λz.(x z (y z))

CHAPTER 2. λ-CALCULUS
K
I

≡
≡

14
λx.λy.x
λx.x

Additional combinators may be added in to the set to obtain more powerful
combinator machine:
B
C

≡ λx.λy.λz.(x (y z))
≡ λx.λy.λz.((x y) z)

The appeal of the combinator approach is that it reduces the problem of evaluating a functional program to a small number of primitives. The disadvantage is that the combinators defined rather small transformations and combinator expressions for nontrivial user functions may become quite large and
thus require a correspondingly greater number of steps to interpret. Hughes
[Hugh 82] redefines the idea and uses an infinite (program dependent) set of
“super-combinators”. A program is translated into an expression containing
super-combinators and a set of super-combinator definitions.

Type Lambda-Calculus
3.1

Types in Computer Science

In the Webster’s New Collegiate Dictionary, the following sentences were found
concerning the word “type”:
. . . a: qualities common to a number of individuals that distinguish
them as an identifiable class: as (1): the morphological, physiological, or ecological characters by which relationship between organisms
may be recognized (2): the form common to all instances of a word
b: a typical and often superior specimen c: a member of an indicated class or variety of people <the quests were mostly urban -s Lucy Cook> d: a particular kind, class, or group: as (1): a texonomic category essentially equivalent to a division or phylum (2): a
group distinguishable on physiologic or zero logical bases (3): one of
a hierarchy of mutually exclusive classes in logic suggested to avoid
paradoxes e: something distinguishable as a variety. . . .
Type is one of most important concepts in the theory of programming languages. The research progress of the type theory has a profound impact on the
development of computer science during the past two decades.
Wegner [Weg 86] has given a detailed discussion on this question which is
quoted as follows:
This is a difficult question to answer because a complete answer must characterize aggregate properties of the type system as a whole as well as properties
of individual types. We were tempted to replace the question by “What is a
type system?”. Instead of such rephrasing we choose to interpret the original
question as an abbreviation for the longer question: “What properties must
types have both individually and collectively to constitute an acceptable type
system for an object-oriented language?”.

15

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

16

The following collection of partial answers illustrates the variety of levels at
which an answer may be formulated.
1. Applicative programmer’s view: Types partition values into equivalent classes with common attributes and operations. Polymorphism allows
type to have overlapping value sets so the partition (equivalence relation)
becomes a covering (compatibility relation) of the universal value set.
2. System evolution (object-oriented) view: Types are behaviour specifications (predicates) that may be composed and incrementally modified to
form new behaviour specifications. Inheritance is an important mechanism
for incrementally modifying behaviour that supports system evolution.
3. Type checking view: Types impose syntactic constraints on expressions
so that operators and operands of composite expressions are compatible. A
type system is a set of rules for associating a type with every semantically
meaningful subexpression of a programming language.
4. Verification view: Types determine behaviour invariants that instances
of the type are required to satisfy.
5. System programming and security view: Types are a suit of clothes
(armor) that protects raw information (bit strings) from unintended interpretations.
6. Implementer’s view: Types specify a storage mapping for values.

3.2

Intuitionistic Logic

In mathematical logic, formal languages in which we can express substantial
parts of mathematics have long history, going back to Frege’s formulation in
1872 of the predicate logic. So, if we want a programming language in which
it should be possible not just to write down programs but also to reason about
them, an obvious attempt is to see whether any of the formalization used for
mathematics could also be used for programming.

3.2.1

Nonconstructive Mathematics

Today, the standard formalization of classical, i.e. nonconstructive mathematics
is the axiomatization of set theory given by Zermelo in 1908 (and later extended
by Fraenkel). However, ZF is not intended to be a language in which we are
supposed to actually do mathematics in, but rather a language in which we
exhibit the basic principles of set theory. For instance, in ZF there are only two
nonlogical symbols, = and ∈, expressing equality and membership respectively.
So in ZF we can not write any program at all. Even if we extend ZF to a

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

17

syntactically richer language, there remains the problem of how to represent
programs; the notion of function can not be used because functions in classical
set theory are in general not computable.

3.2.2

Constructive Mathematics

Out of the foundational crisis of mathematics in the first decades of this century, constructive mathematics arose as an independent branch of mathematics,
mainly developed by Brouwer under the name intuitionism. Constructive mathematics did not get much support because of the general belief that important
parts of mathematics were impossible to develop constructively. By the work of
Bishop, however, this belief has been shown to be totally wrong. In the book
“Foundations of Constructive Analysis”, Bishop rebuilds constructively central
parts of classical analysis; and he does it in a way that demonstrates that constructive mathematics can be as simple and elegant as classical mathematics.
Bishop has also envisaged the possibility of using a formalization of constructive mathematics for programming, starting from Gödel’s theory of computable
functions of finite type. Constable has also used constructive mathematics as a
foundation of programming.

3.2.3

Deductions as Computations and Computations as
Deductions

The following are some general examples of “deductions as computations”:
• A first example of “deduction as computation” is backward chaining. An
algorithm for searching for a deduction of A from B1 , ..., Bn may start with
a desired conclusion A, and then apply all rules of deduction in reverse
systematically at intermediate stages to see if each statement could arise
as intermediate conclusion from intermediate premises. The algorithm
then terminates when A has been traced back through various stages to
the assumptions B1 , ..., Bn , and does not terminate if it never succeeds in
constructing a deduction of A from B1 , ..., Bn . A typical example of this
kind of systems is logic programming language Prolog.
• A second example of “deduction as computation” is forward chaining. An
algorithm for searching a deduction of A from B1 , . . . , Bn may start with
B1 , . . . , Bn as data, applying all rules of deduction systematically at intermediate stages to intermediate premises to obtain intermediate conclusions. The program then terminates when A is obtained as a conclusion,
and therefore when a deduction of A from B1 , . . . , Bn has been obtained.
An idealization of classical expert system language OPS5 is an example
of this kind of systems.
The following are some general examples of “computation as deduction”:

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

18

• Think of a computation as proceedings in stages, and as having a unique
state at each stage and a unique input history. Each successive state is one
of the possible successor of the previous state of the processor and input
history. The possible finite sequences of states compatible with machine
operation rules are the possible finite execution sequences. A sequential
machine is one with only one execution sequence. A translation from
execution sequences to deductions can be described as follows: given the
input history H and the current state S and one possible successor state
S 0 (there may be many), associate one rule of inference I(H, S, S 0 ) with
H and S as premises, and S 0 as conclusion. This is an operational view
of computations. There are many other view on computation, and each
admits a corresponding deduction system. From another point of view,
each execution sequence is a model of a theory, rather than a deduction
in the theory.
• Every system in which program correctness (i.e., that programs meet their
specification) can be proved can be constructed as a logical system. There
is an enormous quantity of ongoing work concerning sequential programs
on program specification, program development, and program verification.
From intuitionistic point of view, proposition and judgement are different
concepts. A proposition is a description of certain fact, we can use logical
operators such as ⇒, ∧, ∨, ¬, ∀ and ∃ to construct complex proposition from
simple ones. On other hand, when we say that a proposition hold, we have make
a judgement. For example, if A stands for “points x and y are on a line”, then
A is only a proposition. But if we say that A is true, that is, we have a proof
to show that points x and y are on a line, then we have made a judgement.
In classical logic, a proposition is a value of true or false. Intuitionistic
logic, however, a proposition is determined by the contents of its proof. If we
can give a proof of this proposition, then the proposition holds. Therefore, the
provability is the true value of the proposition.
The meaning of logical connectives and the intuitionistic interpretation of
propositions can be related as Table 3.1: That is, the proposition F alse has no

proof. The proof of proposition A ∧ B consists of a proof of A and a proof of

proof of proposition A ⇒ B is a method, given a proof of A, a proof of B can be
obtained. The proof of proposition ∀(x)B(x) is a method too, given any proof
a for A, a proof of B(x) can be obtained. The proof of proposition ∃(x)B(x)
consists of a proof a of A and a proof of B(a).

3.3

Natural Deduction Systems

3.3.1

Deduction System

A deduction system for the predicate calculus consists of a (possibly infinite)
recursive set of valid well-formed formulas (wffs), called axioms, and a finite set
of mappings, called rules of inferences; each rule of inference maps one or more
valid wffs into a valid wff. A wff B is said to be deducible (provable) in such
a system if there exists a finite sequence of wffs such that B is the last wff in
the sequence and each wff in the sequence is either an axiom or derived from
previous wffs in the sequence by one of the rules of inference. Such a sequence
of wffs is called a proof of B. Thus, since each axiom is a valid wff and each
rule of inference maps valid wffs into a valid wff, it follows that every deducible
wff is valid.

3.3.2

Natural Deduction System

A natural deduction system is a deduction system invented by Gentzen in which
the set of axioms and inference rules is rich enough to enable valid wffs to be
deduced in a very “natural” way. We now use the Greek letter Γ to indicate
any sequence of zero or more wffs and use notation Γ ` B standing for wff B
is the consequence of Γ.

3.3.3

3.4

System F

Although type-free λ-calculus is powerful in that it can represent all computable
functions, it has two defects as functional programming language.
• Our program can continually break the stipulations of our conceptual type
system, which permits only numbers to be added and so forth.

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

22

• Non-terminating computation is possible because not every term has a
normal form.
Both problems are due to the great amount of freedom in combining terms. In
a typed λ-calculus this combinatorial freedom is restricted by a number of type
inference rules. Now we will introduce a typed λ-calculus called F.
System F is a second-order typed λ-calculus formulated by Girard [Gir 72].
It is an extension of the simple typed lambda calculus by adding an operation
of abstraction on types. This operation is extremely powerful and in particular
all the usual data types such as natural numbers, lists and trees are definable.
Since the calculus is composed of only a few constructs, F is a useful tool for
studying and giving semantics to programming languages where types appear
explicitly as parameters. System F and its strong normalbility were first introduced and proved in the context of proof theory by Girard [Gir 72, GirLT 89].

3.4.1

Types and Terms

In F, each terms has a type, and types are written using higher-order symbols
(type-constructors), ⇒ and ∀.
The syntax of F may be separated into two parts. The first part is the set of
second-order lambda expressions or term. The second syntactic class contains
the type expressions.
The set T of types is defined inductively as follows:
σ ::= t | σ⇒τ | ∀t. σ
where σ, τ range over T and t is an unspecified element of a denumerable set V
of type variables. We denote by FTV(σ) the set of type variables which are free
in σ. We will identify the types which differ only in the names of bound type
variables.
The set Λ of terms is defined as follows:
M ::= x | (M N ) | (M τ ) | λx : σ. M | Λt. M
where M, N range over Λ, σ, τ range over T , t ranges over V, and x is an
unspecified element of a denumerable set X of (term) variables. We denote
by FV(M ) the set of free variables of M and by FTV(M ) the set of free type
variables of M . Again we identify the terms which differ only in the name of the
bound variables and bound type variables. As in Chapter 2 we can leave out
redundant parentheses by adopting the standard conventions that application
associates to the left, abstraction associates to the right, and the type forming
operation ⇒ associates to the right.

3.4.2

A context is a partial function Γ : X −→T with finite domain. Alternatively, we
will also regard contexts as finite sets of pairs x : σ such that no x occurs twice.

used to derive judgements. A term M is well-typed if Γ `M : σ for some Γ and

Now the notions such as ‘normal form’, ‘strong normalizable’ are defined with
λ

λ

We have seen that not every term in type free λ-calculus has a normal form, but
in polymorphic λ-calculus things are different because formation of well-typed
terms is restricted by the type inference rules. In fact, we have the following
theorem:
Theorem 3.2 Every well-typed term is strongly normalizable.
λ

Alternatively, this theorem can also be stated as “−→ is strongly normalizing”.

One may have observed from the above examples how ‘polymorphism’ works: a
type ∀t. σ is actually a type schema, its element Λt. M may have different types
when applied to different types. For example
n Bool : Bool⇒(Bool⇒Bool)⇒Bool
n Nat : Nat⇒(Nat⇒Nat)⇒Nat
One may also have seen that this language is revolutionary in that it merges data
structures and control structures. Here, a data structure is but an unfulfilled
control structure, waiting for more arguments to be able to “compute itself out”.
Thus to each data type corresponds naturally a control structure. For example,
Bool is just the notion of conditional, that is, if b : Bool, u : U and v : U , the
construction if b then u else v may be implemented as b U u v : U . For
Nat, the polymorphic natural n : Nat may be thought of as the construction
for i := 1 to n do.

3.5

Should Types be Modelled by Calculi or Algebra?

Wegner [Weg 86] has also discussed this question. His discussion can be summarized as the following:

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

29

• A calculus is a syntactic system of transformation rules. A “calculus” may be a formal system such as the predicate calculus where computation consists of applying rules of inference to prove theorems. It may
be a reduction system such as the lambda calculus where computation
consists of reducing expressions to a normal form in which no further reductions are applicable. The notion of calculus that emerges from these
examples is that of a syntactically defined set of computation or inference
rules are applicable (in reduction systems).
• An algebra is a semantic system whose behaviour can be realized
by a variety of syntactic calculi. An algebra is semantic system whose
behaviour can be realized by a variety of syntactic calculi. For example the
algebra of integers can be realized by decimal or binary number systems or
even by lambda calculus representation. An algebra may be thought of as
an equivalence class of calculi with common behaviour or as an abstraction
from a specific syntactic realization of a calculus to a semantic specification
of an underlying behaviour.
• Specifying a computation as an algebra or a calculus. In some
contexts a choice can be made on between specifying a computation as an
algebra or a calculus.
• Relation between calculi and algebras. Calculi are concrete (syntactic) algebras while algebras are abstract (semantic) calculi. Viewed as a
mathematical statement we can simply say that an algebra is model of a
calculus in the model theoretical sense, and conversely that a calculus is a
concrete realization of some behaviour it is trying to model. Moreover, a
given calculus generally has many semantic models and conversely a given
algebra can generally be realized by many calculi.
• Modelling types with calculi and algebra. Algebras can be used
to specify behaviour while calculi can used to specify rules for computation. Since individual types are forms of behaviour they are appropriately
specified by algebras. However, relationship among types are forms of
behaviour they are appropriately specified by algebras. Many-sorted algebras provide a basis for modelling properties of individual types while the
second-order lambda calculus provides a basis for modelling properties of
the type system as a whole, such as polymorphism.
As the theory of power domains has been used to study the semantics of concurrent system, the theory of super types can be used to study the formal logic
frameworks.

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

3.6

Philosophical View of Types

3.6.1

Realist View

30

The realist view of types is that we live in a world populated by values and that
types are an explanatory mechanism introduced for the purpose of classifying
and managing values. Thus the realist postulates that a global universe exists
prior to the things. So the objects of sense perception or sometimes of cognition
in general are real in their own right and exist independently of their being
known or related. The philosophical position is that “observability depends on
existence”.

3.6.2

Intuitionist View

The intuitionist view of types is that types are the basic conceptual entities
of the domain of discourse and that values exist only in so far as they are
constructable from some type. Thus the intuitionist requires existence to be
demonstrated by observability or constructibility and believes that natural phenomena as well as artificial mathematics and computational entities exist only
when they are observed, constructed, or interpreted. The philosophical position
is that “existence depends on observability”.
From the mathematical point of view, the following corresponding may be
observed in the proof theory:
Realism ←→ Existence P roof
Intuitionism ←→ Constructive P roof

3.7

Polymorphic Functional Languages

3.7.1

Edinburgh ML

A typical polymorphic functional programming language is perhaps the Edinburgh ML defined in LCF [GorMW 79] (Logic for Computable Functions)
system. ML is a polymorphic functional language. Its main features are:
• It is fully higher-order, i.e. functions are first-order values and may be
passed as arguments, returned as results, or embedded in data structures.
• It has a simple, but flexible, mechanism for raising and handling exceptions.
• ML has an extensible and completely secure polymorphic type discipline,
namely, implicit polymorphism [Mil 78]. ML may be written in an essential type-free way and their consistency with respect to typing is automatically checked.

CHAPTER 3. TYPE LAMBDA-CALCULUS

3.7.2

31

HOPE

HOPE [Bur 80] is a polymorphically typed higher order recursion equation
based functional language. It is a successor to an early first order recursion
equation based language NPL [Bur 77], that itself grew from the work of program transformation. The first implementation of HOPE was at Edinburgh
University. There are now implementations at Bell Laboratories and at Imperial College, London, where it was the initial language behind the design of the
parallel graph reduction machine, ALICE [DarlR 81].

3.7.3

SASL, KRC, Miranda

Turner [Tur 85] has been responsible for a series of higher order functional languages, culminating in Miranda which is a polymorphically typed, higher order,
recursion equation based functional language. The basic idea are taken from
the earlier languages SASL [Tur 76, Rich 84] and KRC [Tur 82], with the addition of a type discipline essentially same as that of ML. The Miranda has
been implemented on a variety of computers, running under the Unix operating
system.
• The Miranda programming language is purely functional – there are no
side-effects or imperative features of any kind. A program is a collection of equations defining various functions and data structures which are
interested in computing.
• An equation can have several alternative right-hand sides distinguished by
‘guards’ (a guard is a Boolean expression).
• Miranda is a higher-order language – functions are first class citizens and
can be both passed as parameters and returned as results.
• Miranda’s evaluation mechanism is ‘lazy’, in the sense that no subexpression is evaluated until its value is known to be required. One consequence of this is that it is possible to define functions which are non-strict
(meaning that they are capable of returning an answer even if one of their
arguments is undefined).
• The ZF-expressions (also called list comprehension) in Miranda give a
concise syntax for a rather general class of iterations over lists. The notation is adapted from Zermelo-Frankel set theory.
• Miranda is strongly typed. That is, every expression and every subexpression has a type, which can be deduced at compile-time, and any inconsistency in the type structure of a program results in compile-time error
message. Types in Miranda can be polymorphic as in ML. User can
define their own types using equations and the user-defined types can also
be polymorphic.

Chapter 4

Syntax of PowerEpsilon
4.1

Notations

For technical reasons the following notations are used in PowerEpsilon:
• ! : for the universal quantifier ∀.
• ? : for the existential quantifier ∃.
• \ : for the lambda abstraction quantifier λ.

4.2

Lexicon

Since the limited usage of computer character set (ASCII codes only), we can
use a variety of letters, both lowercase (a, b, . . . ), uppercase (A, B, . . . ), and
even digits (0, 1, 2, . . . ). However, subscript and superscript letters, and Greek
letters usually used in mathematics are not allowed.
Comments are enclosed in between % and %.

4.3

Syntax

A program written in PowerEpsilon is a theory with an optional query part:
program ::= theory {query}
theory ::=
theory identifier is
{import}
term decl list
end;
query ::=
32

CHAPTER 4. SYNTAX OF POWEREPSILON

33

query
import
term list
end;
A theory is a syntactic unit in PowerEpsilon, which has no semantic meaning
at all; the only role played is to syntactically wrap a set of closely related terms
(propositions and proofs) into one package. The purpose of introduction of
‘theory’ is to make it easy to manage the proof environment for PowerEpsilon.
For instance, Nat is a theory that wraps all the propositions and functions
for specifying properties of natural numbers. The query part describes the
expressions to be evaluated.
Terms in a theory or query may be dependent on other theories. This dependency is specified by a import clause.
import ::= import ident list
ident list ::= identifier {, identifier}∗
The main part of a theory is a sequence of term declarations. A term declaration is either a term specification or a term definition. A term specification
specifies the type of a term. The specification of a term is optional. In the absence of such a specification, the term definition acts as the specification. There
is a type inference system which can derive the specification of a term from its
definition. If both a specification and a definition for a term are given, the term
definition must conform to the term specification.
term decl list ::= term decl {; term decl}∗
term decl ::= term dec | term def
term dec ::= dec identifier : term
term def ::= def identifier = term
The basic expressions of PowerEpsilon, called terms, are defined as follows:

CHAPTER 4. SYNTAX OF POWEREPSILON

4.4

Currying and Higher-Order Terms

4.4.1

Currying and Uncurrying

34

PowerEpsilon is a higher-order language – functions types are first class citizens and can be both passed as parameters and return as results. Function
application is left-associative, so when we write @(f, x, y) it is passed as
@(@(f, x), y), meaning that the result of applying f to x is a function, which
is then applied to y. Functions of more than one argument which take them
“one at a time” like f are called currying.
The curring form of lambda expressions provides some flexibility which is
important for writing easily understood programs. For example,
\(x1 : A1 ) (\(x2 : A2 ) · · · (\(xn : An )M ) · · ·)
can be written
\(x1 : A1 ) \(x2 : A2 ) · · · \(xn : An )M,
or further simplified to
\(x1 : A1 , x2 : A2 , · · · , xn : An )M.
!-terms and ?-terms are treated similarly. In addition, the following terms
[A1 −> A2 −> · · · −> An−1 −> An ]
@(A1 , A2 , A3 , ; · · · , An )
< A1 , A2 , · · · , An−1 , An >
are respectively abbreviations for
[A1 −> [A2 −> · · · [An−1 −> An ] · · · ; ]]
@(· · · @(@(A1 , A2 ), A3 ), · · · , An )
< A1 , < A2 , · · · < An−1 , An > · · · >>
Unlike function application which is left-associative, all of other constructs are
right-associative.
The dec and def constructs are sugared lambda expressions which can be
decoded as an explicit on-line form of lambda expression. For instance, if a
sequence of def-constructs are given as follows:
def S =
\(T : Prop) \(x : [T -> T -> T], y : [T -> T], z : T)
@(x, z, @(y, z));
def K = \(T : Prop) \(x : T, y : T) x;
def I = \(T : Prop) \(x : T) x;
......

CHAPTER 4. SYNTAX OF POWEREPSILON

35

These lambda-expressions can be decoded as
\(S : \(T : Prop)
\(x : [T -> T -> T], y : [T -> T], z : T)
@(x, z, @(y, z)))
\(K : \(T : Prop) \(x : T, y : T) x)
\(I : \(T : Prop) \(x : T) x)
......
In PowerEpsilon, expressions and types have same forms of representation,
for example, the term \(x : T) E can be interpreted as either an expression or
a type depending on the role played by that this term in context.
Current version of PowerEpsilon has neither built-in functions nor built-in
types. Everything must be constructed from bottom. The recursive functions
can be defined using both dec and def constructs.
There are two kind of variables: identifiers and meta-variables. An identifier
is a string of letters or digits beginning with a letter. A meta-variable is an
identifier beginning with a character “*”. The meta-variables are specially used
for defining inductively defined types.

5.1

Type Inference Rules

We now describe the judgement form and the inference rules of PowerEpsilon.
Contexts
A context Γ is a list of bindings of the form x : A, where x is a variable and A
is a term. The empty context is denoted by <>. The set of free variables in a
context Γ ≡ x1 : A1 , · · · , xn : An , is defined as
[

Judgements
A judgement is a form
Γ `M : A
where Γ is a context, and M and A are terms. The intuitive meaning of the
judgement is that M has type A in context Γ. We write ` M : A for <> ` M :
A.
Inference Rules
Type theory has several kinds of rules:
• Formation rules build types from other types.
• Introduction rules build elements of types. By “propositions as types”,
they also introduce logical connectives.

• Elimination rules specify control structures, called selectors, for each type:

is assumed to be a valid context, where K and K 0 stand for arbitrary kinds, i, j
and k for natural numbers. There is a special treatment of the formation rule
for the strong sum types (the existential quantifier formula) ?(x : A) B for
preventing the logically inconsistency of in system, where the objects in Prop
hierarchy are lifted to the Type(0) hierarchy, the objects in Type(i) hierarchy
are lifted to the Type(i+1) hierarchy (i ∈ ω), and the types of form ?(x :
Kind) @(P, x) are not allowed. Since adding type-indexed strong sums directly
to the proposition level Prop of the system results in a logically inconsistent
system in which Giarad’s paradox can be derived. Furthermore, the type ?(x
: Kind) @(P, x) formally does not have any logical meaning, since Kind is
merely used for specifying generic type hierarchies in the system.

A derivation of a judgement J is a finite sequence of judgements J 1 , · · · , Jn
with Jn ≡ J such that, for all 1 ≤ i ≤ n, Ji is the consequence of some instance
of an inference rule whose premises are in {Jk | k < i}. A judgement J is
derivable if there is a derivation of J.
Γ-Terms, Γ-Types and Γ-Propositions
A term M is called a Γ-term (or well-typed term under Γ) if Γ `M : T for some
T . A term T is called a Γ-type if Γ `T : K for some kind K. A Γ-type T is
called a Γ-proposition if Γ `T 0 : Prop for some T 0 ≡ T and called a proper
Γ-type otherwise.

5.2

Type Conversion Rules

We now introduce another kind of judgement Γ `M ≡ N , whose intuitive meaning is that the terms M and N denote the same object. Here ≡ is the smallest
congruence over propositions and contexts containing β-conversion. Figure 5.3
summarizes the definition of type conversion rules.

5.3

Cumulativity Relation

The type inclusions between the universes induce the type cumulativity that is
syntactically characterized by the cumulativity relation ¿. The binary relations
¿i (i ∈ ω) over terms are inductively defined as follows:
1. A ¿0 B if and only if one of the following holds:
(a) A ≡ B;
(b) A ≡ Prop and B ≡ Type(i) for some i ∈ ω, or A ≡ Type(i) and
B ≡ Type(j) for some i < j;
(c) A ≡ Prop and B ≡ Kind, or A ≡ Type(i) and B ≡ Kind for some
i ∈ ω.
2. A ¿i+1 B if and only if one of the following holds:
(a) A ¿i B;
(b) A ≡ ! (x : A1)A2 and B ≡ ! (x : B1)B2 for some A1 ¿i B1 and
A2 ¿i B2;
(c) A ≡ \(x : A1)A2 and B ≡ \(x : B1)B2 for some A1 ¿i B1 and
A2 ¿i B2;
(d) A ≡ ?(x : A1)A2 and B ≡ ?(x : B1)B2 for some A1 ¿i B1 and
A2 ¿i B2;

Chapter 6

Inductive Defined Types
and Generic Type Universe
6.1

Seperation of Logic and Computation

PowerEpsilon, as we have claimed, can be used as both a programming language with a very rich set of data structures and a proof development system. PowerEpsilon can be classified into two categories: the core of the language – PowerEpsilon/Logic and a various of extension of PowerEpsilon
– PowerEpsilon/Computation. In PowerEpsilon/Logic, functions cannot
be defined with both dec and def constructs. In the other word, if a function is declared with dec construct, it cannot be defined with def construct
again. However, in PowerEpsilon/Computation, functions can be defined
with both dec and def constructs. It means that a restricted set of fixpoint
operators are definable within PowerEpsilon.
Recursive functions are definable using dec and def constructs in PowerEpsilon. For instance, the following is a definition of function LOOP:
dec LOOP is [Nat -> Nat];
def LOOP = \(x : Nat) @(LOOP, @(SS, x));
This non-terminating function is legal in PowerEpsilon/Computation, but
illegal in PowerEpsilon/Logic. However, as we will see later that in PowerEpsilon/Computation, the consistency preserved in PowerEpsilon/Logic
might be lost forever.

6.2

Structural Induction Principle

Definition 6.1 Partially Ordered Set A partailly ordered set (W, ≺) consists
43

Inductive Types and Generic Universe

44

of a nonempty set W and any binary relation ≺ on the elements of W which
satisfies the following properties:
1. For all a, b, c ∈ W , if a ≺ b and b ≺ c, then a ≺ c (transitivity).
2. For all a, b ∈ W , if a ≺ b, then b 6≺ a (asymmetry).
3. For all a ∈ W , a 6≺ a (irreflexivity).
As usual, we write a ≺ b or b Â a; a 6≺ b means “a does not procede b”. Note
that the ordering need not to be total, i.e., it is possible that for some
Definition 6.2 Well-Founded Set A partially ordered set W, ≺) which contains
no infinite decreasing sequences, a0 > a1 > a2 > . . ., of elements of W is called
a well-founded set.
Theorem 6.1 Structural Induction Let (S, ≺) be a well-founded set, and let phi be a
total predicate over S: if ∀(a ∈ S){[∀(b ∈ S such that b ≺ a)φ(b)] −> φ(a)}, then
∀(c ∈ S)φ(c).

Proof:
To prove the validity of this rule, we shall show that if the assumption is astisfied,
there can be no element in S for which φ is false. Consider the set A:
A = {a : a ∈ S ∧ φ(a) is false}
Let us assume that A is noempty. Then there is a least element a0 in A such
that a 6≺ a0 for any a ∈ A; otherwise there would be an infinite descending
sequence in S. Then, for any element b ∈ S such that b ≺ a0 , φ(b) is true; that
is, ∀(b ∈ S such that b ≺ a)φ(b) holds. But the assumption then implies that
φ(a0 ) is true, which contradicts the fact that a0 ∈ A. Therefore A must be
empty; that is, φ(c) is true for all elements c ∈ S. 2
Note that if there is no b ∈ S such that b 6≺ a, the statement ∀(b ∈
S such that b ≺ a)φ(b) holds vacuously. Therefore for such a’s we must show
φ(a) unconditionally to establish the hypothesis needed for the structural induction.
For type checking the induction principle and the predicate φ, the full range
of inductively defined type S must be checked instead of checking only the
individual elements of S.

6.3

An Inductive Scheme for PowerEpsilon

One of the fundamental concepts in logic is that of an inductively defined type
(IDT). One of the simplest inductively defined types is that of lists over a type
A. First, the empty list NIL is a list of A; this is the base case of the definition.

Inductive Types and Generic Universe

45

And if e is in A and if l is a list of A, then @(CONS, e, l) is a list of A. In
general, a type is specified inductively if we show how to build an element of
the type given other elements of it. Also in general there is a base case which
does not involve using other elements of the type to build new ones.
One of the important features of PowerEpsilon is that it provide a special
scheme for defining inductive types. Without this scheme, the inductive types
are impossible to be specified. As an example, let us assume that there is a
predefined type Nat : Prop with O : Nat and S : [Nat -> Nat], and we
want to define the following type:
dec Rec :
!(P : [Nat -> Prop])
[@(P, O) ->
[!(u : Nat) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(S, u))] ->
!(n : Nat) @(P, n)];
with the conversion rules
Γ ` P : [N at −> P rop]; Γ ` a : @(P, O); Γ ` f :!(u : N at)[@(P, u) −> @(P, @(S, O))]
Γ ` @(Rec, P, a, f, O) ≡ a
Γ`P : [N at−>P rop]; Γ`a : @(P, O); Γ`f :!(u : N at)[@(P, u)−>@(P, @(S, O))]; Γ`n : N at
Γ`@(Rec, P, a, f, @(S, n)) ≡ @(f, n, @(Rec, P, a, f, n))

Unfortunately, Rec cannot be proved within the framework of CC. It has to be
defined as a primitive. This shortcoming makes CC very inflexible for handling
inductive types.

6.3.1

Meta-Variables and Inductively Defined Types

To overcome the difficulty mentioned above, we will introduce a scheme for
proving a certain kind of inductive types.
Actually, We can prove a “principle of R-induction”:
∀(x : T )∀(y : T )(R(y, x) ∧ p[y/x] ⇒ p) ⇒ ∀(x : T )p
for any well-founded relation R (A binary relation R over a type T is wellfounded if any subset S of T has an R-minimal element) [Johnst 87], provided
that
1. R is a well-founded relation (WFR).
2. R is a transitive operation on a type T , i.e.
∀(y : T )∀(z : T )R(z, y) ∧ R(y, x) ⇒ R(z, x)
Let T C(x, R) be the smallest set of transitive closure of R over x of T :
T C(x, R) = {y : T |R(y, x)}

Inductive Types and Generic Universe

46

3. We know that the R-predecessors of any members of a given type
∀(y : T )∃(Z ⊆ T )∀(x : T )(x ∈ Z ⇔ R(x, y))
A relation R with this property is said to be local.
We called R which satisfies the properties (1)-(3) described above the wellordered relation (WOR).
Theorem 6.2 In the presents of axioms of Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory, assume
that R is a well-ordered relation on an inductively defined type T , the following
principle of R-induction hold:
∀(x : T )(∀(y : T )(R(y, x) ∧ p[y/x] ⇒ p) ⇒ ∀(x : T )p)
Proof:
Assume the hypothesis that p is R-inductive, i.e.
∀(x : T )(∀(y : T )(R(y, x) ∧ p[y/x]) ⇒ p)
Assume further ¬∀(x : T )p), i.e. ∃(x : T )¬p. Given a particular x for which ¬p,
consider the set
u = {y ∈ T C(x, R)|¬p[y/x]}
u is nonempty since e ∈ u, and so it has an R-minimal element y, say. But
then we have ¬p[y/x] since y ∈ u, and ∀(z : T )R(z, y) ∧ p[z/x] since all the
elements which are the predecessors of y are in T C(x, R) \ u. Hence we have
∃(x : T )∃(y : T )¬p ⇒ ¬R(y, x) ∨ ¬p[y/x]. This contradicts the R-inductiveness
of p. Hence (p is inductive) ⇒ ∀(x : T )p.2
We will introduce the concept of meta-variable for defining inductive types.
A meta-variable is an identifier beginning with a character “∗”. Together with
the meta-variable, two special type conversion rules is added.
Meta − Var − 1

Γ ` !(u : A)∗x:≡u

where R is a WOR, A is an ICT, M 0 of type A is a R-Minimal and M + is the
successor of M of type A under R.

Using this scheme together with the recursive definition scheme, Rec described above can be defined as follows.
def Rec =
\(P : [Nat -> Prop])
\(a : @(P, OO))
\(f : !(u : Nat) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(SS, u))])
\(*n : Nat)
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(P, *n),
@(IS_ZERO, *n),
a,
@(f, @(PP, *n), @(Rec, P, a, f, @(PP, *n))))
Rec has tte required type:
dec Rec :
!(P : [Nat -> Prop])
[@(P, OO) ->
!(u : Nat) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(SS, u))] ->
!(*n : Nat) @(P, *n)].
Where *n is an inductively defined variables and there is a naturally wellfounded relation < defined on Nat: 0 < 1 < 2 < . . ..
Semantically the :≡ in ∗x:≡e can be interpreted as the assignment which
assign term e into meta-variable ∗x.
The :≡ is stronger than ≡. We have ≡⊆ :≡, that is,
∀(e1, e2)e1 ≡ e2 ⇒ e1:≡e2
Intuitively, the inductive type definition scheme can be explained as follow:
1. Given a logic system L, any induction principles of L are impossible to
be derived within L as the theorems. However, these induction principles
may be derived as the consequences of the meta-system of L.
2. Two objects e and e0 , that are different in a logic system L, may be
identical in the meta-system of L.
The meta-variables satisfies the following properties:
Refl ∗x:≡∗x.
Symm ∗x:≡∗y ⇒ ∗y:≡∗x.
Tran ∗x:≡e1 ∧ e1 ≡ e2 ⇒ ∗x:≡e2 ; ∗x:≡∗y ∧ ∗y:≡e ⇒ ∗x:≡e.

6.3.2

48

Well-Ordered Relation R for Inductive Types

The crucial point for checking the correctness of inductive type Rec on an inductively defined types T is to find a well-ordered relation R over T. For example,
for type Nat of natural numbers, the binary relation <, read as “less than”, is
a well-ordered relation:
0 < 1 < 2 < ...
A easier way for finding a well-ordered relation R over an arbitrary inductively defined type T is to check the length of the elements of T using Nat and
“<” such that
1. The length of the base element a of T (R-Minimal) is 0.
2. Inductively, if the length of an element b of T is n, then the length of the
successor of b is n + 1.
This scheme is equivalent to introduce the type of natural number Nat with
0 : Nat and S : [Nat -> Nat], and the inductive type Rec on Nat as primitives.

6.4

On Type Universe Hierarchies

Another important feature of PowerEpsilon is the introduction of special constant: Kind, which is the limit of type universe hierarchies Type(0), Type(1),
....

6.4.1

Type Universe Hierarchies and Natural Numbers

The construction of universe hierarchies is analog to the constructive approach
for natural numbers proposed by von Neumann where each natural number is
the set of all smaller natural numbers [Ender 77].
0=∅
1 = {0} = {∅}
2 = {0, 1} = {∅, {∅}}
3 = {0, 1, 2} = {∅, {∅}, {∅, {∅}}}
...
More generally, if n is a natural number, then n ∪ {n} is a natural number
too, usually denoted as n+ = n ∪ {n}.
This construction of the natural numbers has the following properties:
0 ∈ 1 ∈ 2 ∈ 3 ∈ ...
and
0 ⊂ 1 ⊂ 2 ⊂ 3 ⊂ ...

In PowerEpsilon, we have
Prop ∈ Type(0) ∈ Type(1) ∈ . . .
and
Prop ⊂ Type(0) ⊂ Type(1) ⊂ . . .

6.4.2

Relationship between PowerEpsilon, CC and ECC

Let PowerEpsilon0 be the subsystem of PowerEpsilon with the typeuniverse
hierarchies Prop and Type(0) and PowerEpsilonω be the subsystem of PowerEpsilon with the type universe hierarchies Prop and Type(i) (i ∈ ω). We
have that PowerEpsilon0 is equivalent to CC with two universe hierarchies
Prop and Type(i). PowerEpsilonω is equivalent to ECC [Luo 91].
1. PowerEpsilon0 ≡ CC.
2. PowerEpsilonω ≡ ECC.
3. Prop closed under Π.
4. Type(i) closed under Π and Σ.
5. Kind closed under Π and Σ.

The order of a term in PowerEpsilon is determined by what kind of abstractions over types are allowed: we obtain the second-order polymorphic lambdacalculus F2 if we allow abstractions only over type variables of kind Prop; we

Chapter 7

Main Theoretic-Properties
of PowerEpsilon
7.1

Proof-Theoretic Properties of PowerEpsilon

There are some fundamental properties of PowerEpsilon which will be used
in the remainder of the paper. We state here only a few of them. The proofs
for these properties can be obtained by applying induction on the depth of
expressions and types.
Theorem 7.1 (Church-Rosser Property) All well-typed terms are ChurchRosser. That is, if term a is reduced to term b, and b is a normal form, then
there exists a normal order reduction from a to b.
Theorem 7.2 (Subject Reduction Property) If term t is reduced to term
t’ and the type of t is A, then the type of t’ is A too.
Theorem 7.3 (Weak Normalization Property) Any term can be reduced
to a normal form.
Theorem 7.4 (Strong Normalization Property) All terms of PowerEpsilon are strongly normalizable, and the normal form is unique.
This property means that in PowerEpsilon every potentially infinite sequence of reduction steps terminates in a normal form due to strong normalization, and the functions to be defined are all the total computable functions. So
the collection of functions definable by PowerEpsilon are much smaller than
untyped lambda-calculus representing all the computable functions.
The normalization theorem for PowerEpsilon2 (PowerEpsilon restricted
on second-order) implies the consistency of second-order arithmetic system P A2 .
51

CHAPTER 7. MAIN THEORETIC-PROPERTIES OF POWEREPSILON52
There is an important result in mathematical logic, namely, the Gödel’s second
incompleteness theorem, which says (assuming that it is not contradictory) that
the consistency of P A2 cannot be proved within P A2 . Consequently, since
consistency can be deduced from normalization within P A2 , the normalization
theorem cannot be proved within P A2 .
Theorem 7.5 (Unicity of Types) Each expression has an unique type and
each type has an unique kind.
Theorem 7.6 (Type Checking is Decidable) Given environment Γ, term
m, and type A, it is decidable whether Γ ` m : A.
Theorem 7.7 (Typing is Computable) Γ ` m : ? is computable. This
states that given environment Γ and term m, it can be decided whether there is a
A such that Γ ` m : A and moreover one such A can be computed from Γ and
m.
Theorem 7.8 (Γ ` ? : A is Undecidable) Given environment Γ and type
A, it is undecidable whether there is a term m such that gv m : A.
Theorem 7.9 (Representability) The function representable in PowerEpsilonn (PowerEpsilon restricted on nth-order) are exactly those which are
provably total in nth-order Peano arithmetics P An .
The calculus of PowerEpsilon/Computation does not have the normalization property, since in PowerEpsilon the Curry-Howard paradigm is lost
and all propositions become provable. For example, in PowerEpsilon/Logic,
the type Null = !(T : Prop) T is an unprovable proposition and therefore can
be used as the truth-value F alse. However, in PowerEpsilon/Computation,
we may write the following pieces of program which can be a proof of Null:
def Null = !(T : Prop) T;
def DD = \(f : Null) f;
dec CC : Null;
def CC = @(DD, CC).
We even can define a fixpoint operator Y by PowerEpsilon/Computation:
dec Y : !(T : Prop) [[T -> T] -> T];
def Y = \(T : Prop) \(f : [T -> T]) @(f, @(Y, T, f));
such that if A : Prop and F : [A -> A], then @(Y, A, F) = @(F, @(Y, A,
F)).
Therefore a great care must be paid if we want to extend PowerEpsilon/Logic by adding some inductive types into it and still want to preserve the
normalization property.

CHAPTER 7. MAIN THEORETIC-PROPERTIES OF POWEREPSILON53

7.2

Set-Theoretic Semantics of PowerEpsilon/Logic

The proof-theoretic interpretation we have just presented above is syntactic
in nature. It is consistent with the set-theoretic intuition of interpreting M :
P as M ∈ P. Still, the functional space [M -> N] are not interpreted as the
full function space, but only as sets of definable algorithms, closed under the
operations corresponding to the syntactic operators. However, we know from
Reynold’s work that a naive set-theoretic semantics cannot exist [Rey 84].
Fortunately, An intuitionistic set-theoretic semantics based on ω-Set model
proposed by Moggi [LonM 88, Hyl 87] can be employed to capture the intuitive
meaning of the constructs in the calculus and reflects its essential properties.
There is a translation from PowerEpsilon/Logic into ω-Set such that the
proper types can be interpreted as ω-sets and propositions as objects of a full
subcategory PROP of the category of ω-sets which is isomorphic to the category of partial equivalent relations. The lowest impredicative universe Prop
corresponds to the category PROP, the predicative universe Type(i) corresponds to large set universes, and the universe Kind corresponds to limit of
universes.
