7.3

Domain-Theoretic Semantics of PowerEpsilon/Computation

The domain-theoretic semantics for PowerEpsilon/Computation is also possible, where each object (proposition or proof) is mapped to an element of P ω.
This model is too strong and is not suitable for PowerEpsilon/Logic, since
it provide an interpretation for logically inconsistent systems and therefore fail
to capture the essential feature of the calculus. However, domain-theoretic semantics may be useful for PowerEpsilon/Computation, when we consider
PowerEpsilon/Computation as a calculus for specifying semantics of programming languages.

7.4

Model-Theoretic Semantics of PowerEpsilon/Logic

We can build a so called Boolean-valued universes model for PowerEpsilon
similar to the universe V B of “B-random sets” as the model for the ZermeloFraenkel axiomatic set theory [Man 77].

7.5

Logical Truths and Computational Truths

A constructive type theory such as Martin-Löf’s type theory, has an internal
logic based on the Curry-Howard principle of propositions-as-types. However,
as compared with traditional logical systems such as the predicate logic, the
internal logic of type theory and the associated notion of logical truth (as opposed to mathematical truth) are much less understood. This is partly because
that, based on a rigid interpretation of the propositions-as-types principle as
identifying propositions and types, as found in Martin-Löf’s type theory, to understand the logic is to understand the whole type theory, including the types
of mathematical structures.
For instance, in computational logic we may have the following definitions
usually represented in form of Boolean logic:
def Bool = !(T : Prop) [T -> T -> T];

The computational truth is defined as a Boolean value TT.
def TT = \(T : Prop, x : T, y : T) x;

The logical falsity is defined independently.
def FF = \(T : Prop, x : T, y : T) y;

The logic operators are then defined in terms of both TT and FF.

CHAPTER 7. MAIN THEORETIC-PROPERTIES OF POWEREPSILON56

def NOT = \(t : Bool) @(t, Bool, FF, TT);
def AND = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(t2, Bool, @(t1, Bool, TT, FF), FF);
def OR = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(t2, Bool, TT, @(t1, Bool, TT, FF));
def IMPLY = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(OR, @(NOT, t1), t2);

In constructive logic, however, what we have is the following definitions for the
logical truths where the definition of logical truth has to be given in terms of
logical falsity.
def Null = !(T : Prop) T;
def True = [Null -> Null];

We then will be able to define a various of logical operators:
def Not = \(A : Prop) [A -> Null];
def And = \(U : Prop, V : Prop) !(T : Prop) [[U -> V -> T] -> T];
def Or = \(U : Prop, V : Prop) !(T : Prop) [[U -> T] -> [V -> T] -> T];
def Imply = \(U : Prop, V : Prop) [U -> V];

In computational logic, there is a direct correspondence in between OR and IMPLY
as we have IMPLY defined in terms of NOT and OR.
IMPLY = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(OR, @(NOT, t1), t2)
However, in constructive logic framework, Not, And and Or are all defined in
terms of Imply.
The correlation between the internal logic of type theory and the computational logic is not well-understood.
It is clear that in type theory there should be a clear distinction between the
notion of a logical proposition and that of a type of mathematical structures,
and on the basis of this distinction, a further distinction between the notion of
(pure) logical truth and that of mathematical truth. Informally, this amounts
to an argument for the independence of logic in the sense that the province of
logic has a trait of being universal and hence independent of the existence of the
entities to be reasoned about by means of the logic. A conceptual identification
of logical propositions and types of mathematical structures could be misleading
and may make it more difficult to understand the internal logic of type theory.

Chapter 8

Applications
We have described the formal definition of PowerEpsilon. The language is
able to represent and manipulate a rich set of logic systems as we have shown
in the examples. In addition, the language can serve as its own metalanguage
providing that it can model reflection and reification in a very natural way.

8.1

Predicative and Nonpredicative Objects

An object is said to be predicative if it is defined by a quantification over a
type which does not contain the object itself. In this sense, PowerEpsilon
allows the formation of non-predicative objects. For instance, the polymorphic
identity function I = \(X : TYPE)\(x : X) x is not predicative since it can
be instantiated over its own type !(X : TYPE) [X -> X].

8.2

Curry-Howard Isomorphism

One of the basic ideas behind constructive type theory is the Curry-Howard
interpretation of propositions as types and proofs as programs. This view of
propositions is related both to Heyting’s explanation of intuitionistic logic and,
on a more formal level, to Kleene’s realizability interpretation of intuitionistic
arithmetic.
The Curry-Howard [How 69] isomorphism establishes the relationship between the calculus of deductions in the higher-order intuitionistic predicate logic
and the calculus of typed terms in the polymorphic lambda-calculus. The isomorphism maps
• Higher-order predicate logic to corresponding types.
• Deduction of predicates to terms of the corresponding type.

57

CHAPTER 8. APPLICATIONS

58

This correspondence between propositions and types is a powerful paradigm for
mechanical proof construction and computer programming.
The judgement or type assignment “a : A” in PowerEpsilon can be read
in at least the four following ways:
• a is an element of type A.
• a is a proof object for the proposition A.
• a is a program satisfying the specification A.
• a is a solution to the problem A.
In classical mathematics, a proposition is thought of as being true or false independently of whether we can prove or disprove it. On the other hand, a
proposition is constructively true only if we have a method to prove it. For
example, classically the law of excluded middle, A ∨ (¬A), is true, since the
proposition A is either true or false. Constructively, however, a disjunction is
true only if we can prove one of the disjuncts. Since we have no method of
proving or disproving an arbitrary proposition A, we have no proof of A ∨ (¬A)
and therefore the law of excluded middle is not constructively valid.
We may establish the following identifications between propositions and
types.
• A proof of type [A -> B] is a function (method, program) which to each
proof of A gives a proof of B.
• A proof of type @(Product, A, B) is a pair whose first component is a
proof A and whose second component is a proof of B.
• A proof of type @(Sum, A, B) is either a proof A or a proof of B together
with the information of which of A or B we have a proof. The elements
in @(Sum, A, B) are of the form @(INJ1, A, B, a) and @(INJ6, A, B,
b) where g : A and b : B.
• A proof of type !(x : V) @(B, x) is a function (method, program)
which to each element a of type A gives a proof of @(B, a). The type
corresponding to this is the Cartesian product of a family of types. The
elements of this type are functions which, when applied to an element a
in the type A gives an element in the type @(B, a).
• A proof of type ?(x : A) @(B, x) is a pair whose first component a is
an element of type A and whose second component is a proof of @(B, a).
The type corresponding to this is the disjoint union of a family of types.
The elements of this type are pairs <a, b> where a : R and b : @(B,
a).

CHAPTER 8. APPLICATIONS

59

From the points of view for computer programming, a proposition could be
interpreted as the specifications of the task of a program in the following way.
• @(Product, A, B) is a specification of programs which, when executed,
yield a pair @(PRODUCT, A, B, a, b), where a is a program for the task
A and b is a program for the task B.
• @(Sum, A, B) is a specification of programs which, when executed, either
yields @(INJ1, A, B, a) op @(INJ2, A, B, b), where a is a program
for A and b is a program for B.
• [A -> B] is a specification of programs which, when executed, yields \(x
: A) b, where b is a program for B under the assumption that x is a
program for A.
• !(x : A) @(B, x) is a specification of programs which, when executed,
yields \(x : A) b, where b is a program for @(B, x) under the assumption that x is an object of A. This means that when a program for the
problem !(x : A) @(B, x) is applied to an arbitrary object x of X, the
result will be the program for @(B, x).
• ?(x : A) @(B, x) is a specification of programs which, when executed,
yields <a, b>, where a is an object of A and b is a program for @(B, a).
So, to solve the task ?(x : A) @(B, x) it is necessary to find a method
which yields an object a in A and a program for @(B, a).
There are two ways to manipulate types, one way is to construct programs from
types and another is to deduce type structures from the corresponding programs.
The former is corresponding to program derivation from specification and the
later is corresponding to program verification.

8.2.1

From Types to Programs: The Constructive Mathematics

The extraction of programs from proofs or programming in constructive logic
is based on the idea that under some restrictions proofs can be considered as
programs. The general scheme of extracting programs from proofs is the following: at the beginning one writes a specification of the problem in some formal
language (for example, the PowerEpsilon). Then a formal proof of this specification is constructed and the program is extracted from this proof according
to one of the known methods. Because proof construction is not decidable,
strategic information has to be provided by users.

CHAPTER 8. APPLICATIONS

8.2.2

60

From Programs to Types: The Type Inferences

The type inference mechanism of PowerEpsilon can be used mathematically
as a proof editor to check whether a proof for a mathematical theorem is written
correctly.

8.3

Applications of PowerEpsilon

There are many different ways that PowerEpsilon can be used.
Standardization of Mathematics The system can be used as a constructive
logic to develop mathematical proofs. Usually, the papers submitted to
mathematical journals contain a large amount of mathematical theorems
and proofs. Those theorems and proofs often have two problems. First,
since the space limitation, the proofs are incomplete presented. Secondly,
most proofs contain errors. To judge the correctness of those papers left a
heavy burden to the referees. The problems may be solved by making, in
certain degree, the standardization of mathematics and mechanization of
proof checking much similar to the standardization and mechanization of
programming languages processing we hade made in software engineering.
Functional Language The system can be seen as a functional language, with
a number of novel features, such as that every expression has a defined
value; every program terminates; the system of types is more expressive
than those in common use as it does dependent product and function
spaces; and the language is integrated with a logic which to reason about
the programs.
Formal Program Development System The system can be used as a tool
for formal program derivation where programs are extracted from constructive proofs.
Formal Program Verification System The system can be used as a tool
for program verification, where the programs are developed first and then
their correctness are proved. This shows that we have therefore a system
in which programming and verification are integrated.
Program Transformation System The system can also be used to support
program transformation. The essence of program transformation is to take
a program into another with same application behaviour, yet improved in
some aspect such as time efficiency or space usage. Two functions have
the same behaviours when they return the same the same results for all
arguments, when they are extensionally equal, in other words. In PowerEpsilon, program transformation will therefore involve the replacement
of an object by an extensionally equivalent one, through a series of simple

CHAPTER 8. APPLICATIONS

61

steps of the same kind. From the point of view of constructive logic, program transformation can be considered as the translation from one type
to another. Therefore, PowerEpsilon can be used as a meta-language to
study the general strategies and schemes of program transformations.
Decision Support System PowerEpsinon can be used as a decision support system based on the explanation that decision making can be viewed
as a theorem proving process, where for a given problem, we have to prove
whether there is a solution to this problem. A typical decision making process can be expressed at least as the following two propositions:
!(p : Problem) ?(s : Solution) @(Solve, s, p);

or for a specific SOLUTION of type Solution,
!(p : Problem) @(Solve, SOLUTION, p);

for the first formula we have to decide for every problem p whether we
can find a solution s which solves p and for the second formula we have
to decide for every problem p where a specific method SOLUTION solves p.
Knowledge Base System PowerEpsilon can be used as a knowledge base
system. A formalism for representing knowledge should at least be capable
of fulfilling the following five criteria:
1. Say that something has a certain property without saying what thing
has that property. For example, to say John has a sister without
saying that who she is. Formally speaking we have: ?(x : Person)
@(Sister, John, x).
2. Say that everything in a certain class has a certain property without
saying that what everything in that class is. For example, to say that
all dogs are mammals without listing all the dogs. Formally speaking
we have !(x : Animal) [@(Dog, x) -> @(Mammal, x)].
3. Say that at least one of two statements is true without saying which is
true. For example, to say that John teaches computer science 111 or
102 without specifying which of these courses he actually does teach.
Formally speaking we have @(Or, @(Teach, John, CSC101), @(Teach,
John, CSC102)).

4. Explicitly say that something is false. For example, John has no sister. Formally speaking, @(Not, ?(x : Person) @(Sister, John,
x)).

CHAPTER 8. APPLICATIONS

62

5. Either settle or leave open to doubt whether two non-identical expressions name the same object. For example, @(FATHER, John) and
Bill are two non-identical expressions. They may or may not denote
the same individual. If they do, we can explicitly say so by @(Equal,
Person, @(FATHER, John), Bill). If there is doubt about their
naming the same individual, we omit the above equality. This omission does not preclude their naming the same individual; it simply
leaves the knowledge base agnostic about this possibility. PowerEpsilon easily satisfies these five criteria and therefore is a good candidate for representing knowledge. Beside knowledge representation,
PowerEpsilon provides powerful mechanism for knowledge manipulation including natural deduction and consistency checking. The reflection mechanism of PowerEpsilon provide a flexible way to adjust
system itself to a specific application dynamically and autonomously.
Computer-Aided Education Computer-aided instruction has many advantages. When well programmed, a computer can relieve teachers of giving and correcting drill exercises, even of teaching many manipulative
techniques. Students who react negatively to a teacher can turn to the
computer; it instructs without emotional involvement and gives instant,
impartial feedback. It shows no impatience no matter how often a student repeats a mistake. The computer’s greatest asset is that it forces the
student to become an active participant. For most of students, mathematical theorem proving is a hard job. PowerEpsilon can provide a lot
of assistance in this area.
Tool for Studying Semantics of Programming Languages Since its powerful type system, PowerEpsilon can be used as a formal tool to study
semantics of programming languages.

Chapter 9

Representation of
Mathematical Structures in
PowerEpsilon
We have chosen a large number of known examples for testing the proof development system we have just constructed. We present here a few of them. Most
examples were taken from [Hue 89].
To define a type T, three kinds of operations are needed:
• Type formation. The type formation for a type T describe under which
conditions we may infer that T is a type and when two types T and S are
equal.
• Type introduction.
• Type elimination.

9.1

Basic Data Structures

A data structure is an abstraction of a memory facility for storing data. The
data may consist of many component items. How these components relate to
one another determines the innate structure of the data itself. There is no best
data structure for all problems; the appropriate data structure in each case is
dependent on the nature of the data and the computations performed on it.
In PowerEpsilon, one can explicitly define common data types and functions on them, such as Boolean, integers and lists. The following are a few of
examples.

63

Mathematical Structures

9.1.1

64

Boolean

In most programming languages, Boolean is a basic built-in data type for constructing more complicated data structures and functions. In PowerEpsilon,
however, Boolean can be defined as follows:
theory Bool is
%-- Definition of Bool --%
def Bool = !(T : Prop) [T -> T -> T];
%-- Function definitions for Bool --%
%-- Constructors --%
def TT = \(T : Prop, x : T, y : T) x;
def FF = \(T : Prop, x : T, y : T) y;
%-- Canonical functions --%
def NOT = \(t : Bool) @(t, Bool, FF, TT);
def AND = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(t2, Bool, @(t1, Bool, TT, FF), FF);
def OR = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(t2, Bool, TT, @(t1, Bool, TT, FF));
def IMPLY = \(t1 : Bool, t2 : Bool) @(OR, @(NOT, t1), t2);
def GIF_THEN_ELSE = \(U : Prop, t : Bool, u : U, v : U) @(t, U, u, v)
end;

where TT and FF are the functions for Bool introduction and GIF THEN ELSE
is the function for Bool elimination. The function @(GIF THEN ELSE, T, b,
UU, VV) is a conditional function like if-then-else construct in most conventional programming languages which will select one of the two subcomponents
UU and VV for execution, depending on the input Boolean value. For example,
we will have
@(GIF THEN ELSE, T, TT, UU, VV) −→ UU
and
@(GIF THEN ELSE, T, FF, UU, VV) −→ VV
Other enumeration data types can be specified in a very similar way.
The type Bool is built on the first type hierarchy Prop which is an impredicative type. In similar way, we may construct a predicative type Bool0 on
Type(0).
theory Bool0 is
import Bool
def Bool0 = !(T : Type(0)) [T -> T -> T];

Mathematical Structures

65

def TT0 = \(T : Type(0), x : T, y : T) x;
def FF0 = \(T : Type(0), x : T, y : T) y;
def NOT0 = \(t : Bool0) @(t, Bool, FF, TT);
def AND0 = \(t1 : Bool0, t2 : Bool0)
@(t2, Bool, @(t1, Bool, TT, FF), FF);
def OR0 = \(t1 : Bool0, t2 : Bool0)
@(t2, Bool, TT, @(t1, Bool, TT, FF));
def IMPLY0 = \(t1 : Bool0, t2 : Bool0) @(OR0, @(NOT0, t1), t2);
def GIF_THEN_ELSE0 =
\(T : Type(0), t : Bool0, u : T, v : T) @(t, T, u, v)
end;

Similarily, we may have a sequence of Bool1, Bool2, . . . , Booln and so on. This
sequence satisfies the following property:
Bool ¿ Bool0 ¿ Bool1 ¿ . . . ¿ Booln ¿ . . .

9.1.2

Natural Numbers

Let us present a definition for natural number and few functions over naturals.
theory Nat is
import Bool
def BIF_THEN_ELSE

= @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Bool);

%-- Definition of Nat --%
def Nat = !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [Ti -> Ti] -> Ti];
dec ERR : [Nat -> Nat];
dec PP
: [Nat -> Nat];
dec EQUAL : [Nat -> Nat -> Bool];
dec LESS : [Nat -> Nat -> Bool];
dec MINUS : [Nat -> Nat -> Nat];
def NIF_THEN_ELSE = @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Nat);
%-- Function definitions for Nat --%
%-- Constructors --%
def OO = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ti -> Ti]) x;

Mathematical Structures

def SS = \(t : Nat, Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ti -> Ti])
@(y, @(t, Ti, x, y));
%-- Canonical functions --%
def IS_ZERO = \(n : Nat) @(n, Bool, TT, \(x : Bool) FF);
def ADD = \(n1 : Nat, n2 : Nat) @(n2, Nat, n1, SS);
def TIMES = \(n1 : Nat, n2 : Nat) @(n2, Nat, OO, @(ADD, n1));
def EXP = \(n1 : Nat, n2 : Nat) @(n2, Nat, @(SS, OO), @(TIMES, n1));
def T = \(x : Nat, y : Nat) x; def F = \(x : Nat, y : Nat) y;
def PP = \(n : Nat)
@(n, [[Nat -> Nat -> Nat] -> Nat],
\(u : [Nat -> Nat -> Nat]) @(u, OO, OO),
\(p : [[Nat -> Nat -> Nat] -> Nat],
v : [Nat -> Nat -> Nat])
@(v, @(SS, @(p, T)), @(p, T)),
F);
def MINUS = \(n1 : Nat, n2 : Nat)
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(LESS, n1, n2),
@(ERR, n1),
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(IS_ZERO, n2),
n1,
@(MINUS, @(PP, n1), @(PP, n2))));
def EQUAL = \(n1 : Nat, n2 : Nat)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(IS_ZERO, n1),
@(IS_ZERO, n2),
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(IS_ZERO, n2),
FF,
@(EQUAL, @(PP, n1), @(PP, n2))));
def LESS = \(n1 : Nat, n2 : Nat)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(EQUAL, n1, n2),
FF,
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(IS_ZERO, n1),
TT,
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(IS_ZERO, n2),
FF,

66

Mathematical Structures

67

@(LESS, @(PP, n1), @(PP, n2)))));
dec REC : !(A : Prop) [Nat -> A -> [Nat -> A -> A] -> A];
def REC =
\(A : Prop)
\(n : Nat, a : A, f : [Nat -> A -> A])
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE,
A,
@(IS_ZERO, n),
a,
@(f, n, @(REC, A, @(PP, n), a, f)))
end;

where IS ZERO is a Boolean function to check whether a given natural number is zero or not. EQUAL is a Boolean function to check whether two given
natural numbers are equal or not. PP is a predecessor function for any given
natural numbers. We may see that natural numbers are polymorphic iterators.
The addition function ADD is obtained by iterating successor SS; the multiplication function TIMES is obtained by iterating ADD; similarly, the exponentiation
function EXP is obtained by iterating TIMES. The REC is a recursive operator.
Iterating a natural on a functional type may produce non-primitive recursive
functions; for example, we can get Ackermann’s function by the following definition:
def ACK =
\(n : Nat) @(n, [Nat -> Nat], SS, \(f : [Nat -> Nat]) \(m : Nat)
@(m, Nat, m, f));

It is interesting to see the type Nat as expression the proposition “there are
natural numbers”, which has the constructive proofs OO, @(SS, OO), @(SS,
@(SS, OO)), . . . .
Indeed, most (total) recursive functions are definable as proofs in PowerEpsilon.
Theorem 9.1 ([GirLT 89]) Every recursive function provably total in secondorder arithmetic system is definable as a proof of type [Nat -> Nat] in the
polymorphic λ-calculus.
Any recursive function is possible to be defined using fix-point operator we
have just defined at previous section. Suppose, for instance, we put
def H =
\(f : [Nat -> Nat]) \(n : Nat)
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(IS_ZERO, n),
@(SS, OO),
@(TIMES, n, @(f, @(PP, n)))).

Mathematical Structures

68

We can obtain a factorial function by applying the fixpoint operator Y to H.
def FACT = @(Y, [Nat -> Nat], H).

The basic way of proving that a proposition holds for all natural numbers is by
mathematical induction: from @(P, OO) and that @(P, n) implies @(P, @(SS,
n)) we may conclude that @(P, n) holds for all natural numbers n. In order to
be able to prove properties by induction on natural numbers in type theory, we
introduce the formation Rec. From a computational point of view, Rec makes
it possible to make definitions by primitive recursion. An inductive type over
Nat can be defined as follows:
theory Recnat is
import Bool, Nat
dec Rec :
!(P : [Nat -> Prop])
[@(P, OO) ->
!(u : Nat) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(SS, u))] ->
!(n : Nat) @(P, n)];
def Rec =
\(P : [Nat -> Prop])
\(a : @(P, OO))
\(f : !(u : Nat) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(SS, u))])
\(*n : Nat)
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(P, *n),
@(IS_ZERO, *n),
a,
@(f, @(PP, *n), @(Rec, P, a, f, @(PP, *n))));
end;

9.1.3

Integers

Now we extend our type Nat of natural numbers to a type Int of integers (both
positive and negative). Here “extension” is to be loosely interpreted, since Nat
will not actually be a subset of Int. But Int will include an “isomorphic copy”
of Nat.
1. A negative integer can be named by using two natural numbers and a
subtraction symbol: 2 − 3, 5 − 10, etc. For instance, we could try to take
the integer −1 to be the pair h2, 3i of natural numbers.
2. We can define an equivalence relation sim such that h2, 3i ∼ h0, 1i. Then
for the type Int of all integers, we can take the set of all equivalence classes:
Int = (Nat × Nat)/ ∼

Mathematical Structures

69

To call a pair of natural numbers a difference, then an integer will be
an equivalence class of differences. Consequently we can formulate the
following definition:
theory Int is
import Nat
def Int = !(Ti : Prop) [[Nat -> Nat -> Ti] -> Ti];
%-- Function definitions for Int --%
%-- Constructors --%
def INT =
\(u : Nat, v : Nat) \(Ti : Prop, z : [Nat -> Nat -> Ti])
@(z, u, v);
%-- Canonical functions --%
def PRO1 = \(t : Int) @(t, Nat, \(x : Nat, y : Nat) x);
def PRO2 = \(t : Int) @(t, Nat, \(x : Nat, y : Nat) y);
def IEQ = \(i1 : Int, i2 : Int)
@(EQUAL,
@(ADD, @(PRO1, i1), @(PRO2, i2)),
@(ADD, @(PRO1, i2), @(PRO2, i1)));
def ILS = \(i1 : Int, i2 : Int)
@(LESS,
@(ADD, @(PRO1, i1), @(PRO2, i2)),
@(ADD, @(PRO1, i2), @(PRO2, i1)));
def IADD = \(i1 : Int, i2 : Int)
@(INT,
@(ADD, @(PRO1, i1), @(PRO1, i2)),
@(ADD, @(PRO2, i1), @(PRO2, i2)));
def NEG = \(i : Int)
@(INT, @(PRO2, i), @(PRO1, i));
def ITIMES =
\(i1 : Int, i2 : Int)
@(INT,
@(ADD, @(TIMES, @(PRO1, i1), @(PRO1, i2)),
@(TIMES, @(PRO2, i1), @(PRO2, i2))),
@(ADD, @(TIMES, @(PRO1, i1), @(PRO1, i2)),
@(TIMES, @(PRO2, i1), @(PRO1, i2))));
def EE = \(n : Nat) @(INT, n, OO)

Mathematical Structures

70

end;

where IEQ is a Boolean function to check whether two given integers are equal
or not. Given two integers i1 and i2, ILS is a Boolean function to check
whether i1 is less than i2 or not. IADD is an addition function, ITIMES is
a multiplication function and NEG is a negative function for integers. IEE is
isomorphic embedding function which embeds Nat into Int.

9.1.4

Rationales

We can extend our type Int of integers to the type Rat of rational numbers in
much the same way as we extended Nat to Int. In fact, the extension from Int
to Rat is to multiplication what the extension from Nat to Int is to addition.
1. We can name a rational number by using two integers and a symbol for
division: 1/2, −3/4, etc.
2. By a fraction we mean an ordered pair of integers, the second component of
which (called the denominator) is nonzero. We want a suitable equivalence
relation ∼ for which h1, 2i ∼ h6, 12i. Since a/b = c/d iff a × d = c × b, we
choose to define as follows. Let Int’ be the set Int − {0} of nonzero
integers. Then Int × Int0 is the set of all fractions. The binary relation
∼ on Int × Int0 is defined as ha, bi ∼ hc, di iff a × d = c × b. The type Rat
of rational numbers is the set (Int × Int0 )/ ∼ of all equivalence classes of
fractions.
theory Rat is
import Int
def Rat = !(Ti : Prop) [[Int -> Int -> Ti] -> Ti];
%-- Function definitions for Rat --%
%-- Constructors --%
def RAT =
\(u : Int, v : Int) \(Ti : Prop, z : [Int -> Int -> Ti])
@(z, u, v);
%-- Canonical functions --%
def PR1 = \(t : Rat) @(t, Int, \(x : Int, y : Int) x);
def PR2 = \(t : Rat) @(t, Int, \(x : Int, y : Int) y);
dec ROO : Rat;
dec RMAX : [Rat -> Rat -> Rat];
dec RMIN : [Rat -> Rat -> Rat];

Mathematical Structures

71

def REQ = \(i1 : Rat, i2 : Rat)
@(IEQ,
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i1), @(PR2, i2)),
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i2), @(PR2, i1)));
def RLS = \(i1 : Rat, i2 : Rat)
@(ILS,
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i1), @(PR2, i2)),
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i2), @(PR2, i1)));
def RADD = \(i1 : Rat, i2 : Rat)
@(RAT, @(IADD,
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i1), @(PR2, i2)),
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i2), @(PR2, i1))),
@(ITIMES, @(PR2, i1), @(PR2, i2)));
def RNEG = \(i : Rat) @(RAT, @(NEG, @(PR1, i)), @(PR2, i));
def RTIMES = \(i1 : Rat, i2 : Rat)
@(RAT,
@(ITIMES, @(PR1, i1), @(PR1, i2)),
@(ITIMES, @(PR2, i1), @(PR2, i2)));
def REE = \(n : Int) @(RAT, n, @(INT, @(SS, OO), OO))
end;

where REQ is a Boolean function to check whether two given rationales are equal
or not. Given two rationales r1 and r2, RLS is a Boolean function to check
whether r1 is less than r2 or not. RADD is an addition function, RTIMES is a
multiplication function and REG is a negative function for rationales. REE is
isomorphic embedding function which embeds Int into Rat.

9.1.5

Real Numbers

We will continue to show how mathematics can be embedded in type theory by
giving a type-theoretic construction of the real numbers.
Basic to algebra and trigonometry, as well as to most advanced mathematics, is the system of real numbers. Nowhere is the difference between classical
and constructive mathematics more evident than in the treatment of the real
numbers and real analysis.
In our previous extensions of number systems, we relies on the facts that an
integer could be named by a pair of natural numbers, and a rational numbers
could be named by a pair of integers. But we can not hope to name a real number
by a pair of rationales, because, there are too many real numbers and not enough

Mathematical Structures

72

pairs of rationales. Hence we must look at new techniques in searching for a
way to name real numbers.
Actually there are several methods that can be used successfully to construct
the real numbers.
• One approach is to utilize decimal expansions, so that a real number is
determined by an integer and an infinite sequence of digits (a function
from ω into 10).
• An alternative construction of Real uses so called Dedekind cuts.
• Another common method of a suitable type Real is to treat the reals
as equivalence classes of convergent (or Cauchy) sequences of rationales,
choosing arbitrary representatives of equivalence classes when necessary.
The Cauchy sequence construction and the Dedekind cut construction each have
their own advantages. The Dedekind cut construction of Real has an initial
advantage of simplicity, in that it provides a simple definition of Real and its
ordering. But multiplication of Dedekind cuts is awkward, and verification of
the properties of multiplication is a tedious business. The Cauchy sequence
construction of Real also has the advantage of generality, since it can be used
with an arbitrary metric space in place of Rat.
Axiomatic Treatment of Real Numbers
The first method we will use to construct the type Real of real numbers is so
called Dedekind cuts. The idea behind Dedekind cuts is that a real number x
can be named by giving an infinite set of rationales, namely all the rationales
less than x. We will in effect define x to be the set of all rationales smaller than
x.
The two fundamental operations in the system of real numbers are addition
RRADD and multiplication RRTIMES. We shall list some of the most basic and
important properties of this system of real numbers for purposes of review.
The definition of RRADD is given as follows:
dec ADD_RSET : [Rat_Set -> Rat_Set -> Rat_Set];
dec RRADD_Axm :
!(x : Real, y : Real, r : Rat_Set)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
[@(Equal, Rat_Set, r, @(ADD_RSET, xx, yy)) ->
!(a : Rat)
[@(In, a, r) ->
?(b : Rat, c : Rat)
@(And,

Mathematical Structures

73

@(And, @(In, b, xx), @(In, c, yy)),
@(Equal, Rat, a, @(RADD, b, c)))]];
dec RRADD_Lem :
!(x : Real, y : Real)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
let s = @(ADD_RSET, xx, yy) in
@(Ded_Cut_Cond, s);
def RRADD =
\(x : Real, y : Real)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
let s = @(ADD_RSET, xx, yy) in
<s, @(RRADD_Lem, x, y)>;
There are seven properties which follow involving the operation of addition.
The first property can be directly derived from RRADD, which says that the set
of real numbers is closed under addition (Any two real numbers may be added,
and furthermore, the sum will always be a unique real number).
The proposition Com RRAdd says that addition is a commutative operation
(The order in which any two numbers are added makes no difference). The
proposition Ass RRAdd says that addition is an associative operation (The manner in which real numbers are grouped for adding makes no difference). The
proposition Zero RRAdd says that for the set of real numbers, the numbers RROO
is the additive identity (When RROO is added to any real number, the result
is that same real number). The proposition Neg RRAdd says that every real
number has one and only one additive inverse (A real number plus its additive
inverse always equals the additive identity). The propositions Cancl RRAdd,
Order RRAddL, Order RRAddR present the cancellation and ordering preservation properties of addition.
dec Com_RRAdd :
!(x : Real, y : Real)
@(KEqual, Type(3), Real, @(RRADD, x, y), @(RRADD, y, x));
dec Ass_RRAdd :
!(x : Real, y : Real, z : Real)
@(KEqual,
Type(3),
Real,
@(RRADD, @(RRADD, x, y), z),
@(RRADD, x, @(RRADD, y, z)));

Mathematical Structures

74

dec Zero_RRAdd :
!(x : Real)
@(KEqual, Type(3), Real, @(RRADD, x, RROO), x);
dec Neg_RRAdd :
!(x : Real)
@(KEqual, Type(3), Real, @(RRADD, x, @(RRNEG, x)), RROO);
dec Cancl_RRAdd :
!(x : Real, y : Real, z : Real)
[@(KEqual, Type(3), Real, @(RRADD, x, z), @(RRADD, y, z)) ->
@(KEqual, Type(3), Real, x, y)];
dec Order_RRAddL :
!(x : Real, y : Real, z : Real)
[@(RRLs, x, y) -> @(RRLs, @(RRADD, x, z), @(RRADD, y, z))];
dec Order_RRAddR :
!(x : Real, y : Real, z : Real)
[@(RRLs, @(RRADD, x, z), @(RRADD, y, z)) -> @(RRLs, x, y)];
The following is the whole definition of theory Real:
theory Real is
import
Rat_Prop,
Set3, Set4,
Product2, Product4, Product5, Product6,
Equal3, KEqual,
Sum3, Sum6
def Rat_Set = @(Set, Rat);
dec In : [Rat -> Rat_Set -> Prop];
dec Incl : [Rat_Set -> Rat_Set -> Prop];
def Not_Empty =
\(s : Rat_Set)
?(r : Rat) @(In, r, s);
def Not_Full =
\(s : Rat_Set)
?(r : Rat) @(Not, @(In, r, s));

Mathematical Structures

def Ded_Cut_Cond1 =
\(s : Rat_Set)
@(And0, @(Not_Empty, s), @(Not_Full, s));
def Ded_Cut_Cond2 =
\(x : Rat_Set)
!(q : Rat, r : Rat)
[@(And0, @(In, q, x), @(RLs, r, q)) -> @(In, r, x)];
def Ded_Cut_Cond3 =
\(x : Rat_Set)
!(q : Rat)
[@(In, q, x) -> ?(r : Rat) @(And0, @(In, r, x), @(RLs, q, r))];
def Ded_Cut_Cond =
\(s : Rat_Set)
@(And2,
@(Ded_Cut_Cond1, s),
@(And1, @(Ded_Cut_Cond2, s), @(Ded_Cut_Cond3, s)));
def Real = ?(s : Rat_Set) @(Ded_Cut_Cond, s);
%-- Ordering --%
def RRLs =
\(x : Real, y : Real)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
@(Incl, xx, yy);
dec RRLe : [Real -> Real -> Prop];
%-- Addition --%
dec ADD_RSET : [Rat_Set -> Rat_Set -> Rat_Set];
dec RRADD_Axm :
!(x : Real, y : Real, r : Rat_Set)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
[@(Equal, Rat_Set, r, @(ADD_RSET, xx, yy)) ->
!(a : Rat)
[@(In, a, r) ->
?(b : Rat, c : Rat)
@(And,
@(And, @(In, b, xx), @(In, c, yy)),
@(Equal, Rat, a, @(RADD, b, c)))]];

75

Mathematical Structures

dec RRADD_Lem :
!(x : Real, y : Real)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
let s = @(ADD_RSET, xx, yy) in
@(Ded_Cut_Cond, s);
def RRADD =
\(x : Real, y : Real)
let xx = @(FST, x),
yy = @(FST, y) in
let s = @(ADD_RSET, xx, yy) in
<s, @(RRADD_Lem, x, y)>;
%-- Zero --%
dec OO_RSET : Rat_Set;
dec RROO_Axm :
!(r : Rat_Set)
[@(Equal, Rat_Set, r, OO_RSET) ->
!(a : Rat) [@(In, a, r) -> @(RLs, a, ROO)]];
dec RROO_Lem : let s = OO_RSET in @(Ded_Cut_Cond, s);
def RROO = let s = OO_RSET in <s, RROO_Lem>;
%-- One --%
dec ONE_RSET : Rat_Set;

Constructive Treatment of Real Numbers
In a constructive setting, a common method of constructing Real is to utilize
the fact that a real number can be named by giving a sequence of rationales
(a function from Nat to Rat) converging to it. So one can take the set of all
convergent sequences and then divide out by an equivalence relation (where two
sequences are equivalent iff they converge to the same limit). But there is one

Mathematical Structures

83

hitch: The concepts of “convergent” and “equivalent” must be defined without
reference to the real number to which the sequence is converging. This can be
done by a technique named after Cauchy.
We define a Cauchy sequence to be a function s : [Nat -> Rat] such that
| @(s, n) − @(s, m) | is arbitrary small for all sufficiently large m and n.
The Cauchy sequence construction of Real has the advantage of generality,
since it can be used with an arbitrary metric space in place of Rat. The Cauchy
sequence construction may be found, among other places, in [HamL 61].
We define the Cauchy sequences r and s to be equivalent denoted as @(RREq,
r, s) iff | @(r, n) − @(s, n) | is arbitrary small for all sufficiently large n.
def Cau_Seq =
\(s : [Nat -> Rat])
?(m : [Rat -> Nat])
!(q : Rat, n : Nat)
[@(Rat_Less, ROO, q) ->
@(Nat_Less, @(m, q), n) ->
@(Rat_Less, @(RABS, @(RMINUS, @(s, n), @(s, @(m, q)))), q)];
def Real = ?(s : [Nat -> Rat]) @(Cau_Seq, s);

Elements of this type have the form <s, m, p>, where s is a sequence, m is a
modulus of continuity for s and p is a proof of this fact. In computing with reals,
this latter proof information will be computationally irrelevant, but nonetheless
has to be dealt with when we define new reals.
theory Real is
import Rat_Prop
dec RTWO : Rat;
dec RDIV : [Rat -> Rat -> Rat];
def Cau_Seq =
\(s : [Nat -> Rat])
?(m : [Rat -> Nat])
!(q : Rat, n : Nat)
[@(Rat_Less, ROO, q) ->
@(Nat_Less, @(m, q), n) ->
@(Rat_Less, @(RABS, @(RMINUS, @(s, n), @(s, @(m, q)))), q)];
def Real = ?(s : [Nat -> Rat]) @(Cau_Seq, s);
def RREq =
\(r : Real, s : Real)
!(e : Rat) ?(k : Nat) !(n : Nat)
[@(Rat_Less, ROO, e) ->

Mathematical Structures

84

@(Nat_Less, k, n) ->
@(Rat_Less, @(RABS, @(RMINUS, @(FST, r, n), @(FST, s, n))), e)];
%-- Addition --%
def ADD_SEQ =
\(xs : [Nat -> Rat], ys : [Nat -> Rat])
\(n : Nat)
@(RDIV, @(RADD, @(xs, n), @(ys, n)), RTWO);
dec RRADD_Lem :
!(x : Real, y : Real)
let xs = @(FST, x),
ys = @(FST, y) in
let zs = @(ADD_SEQ, xs, ys) in
@(Cau_Seq, zs);
def RRADD =
\(x : Real, y : Real)
let xs = @(FST, x),
ys = @(FST, y) in
let zs = @(ADD_SEQ, xs, ys) in
<zs, @(RRADD_Lem, x, y)>;
%-- Negation --%
def NEG_SEQ =
\(xs : [Nat -> Rat])
\(n : Nat)
@(RNEG, @(xs, n));
dec RRNEG_Lem :
!(x : Real)
let xs = @(FST, x) in
let zs = @(NEG_SEQ, xs) in
@(Cau_Seq, zs);
def RRNEG =
\(x : Real)
let xs = @(FST, x) in
let zs = @(NEG_SEQ, xs) in
<zs, @(RRNEG_Lem, x)>;
%-- Absolute Value --%

There is a slightly more streamlined approach[BishB 85]. We can take the sequences which have a fixed modules of continuity, the regular sequences as
def Seq = [Nat -> Rat];
def Reg =
\(s : Seq)
!(m : Nat, n : Nat)
let mm = @(EE, @(SS, m)),
nn = @(EE, @(SS, n)) in
let mr = @(RAT, IONE, mm),
nr = @(RAT, IONE, nn) in
@(Rat_Less,
@(RABS, @(RMINUS, @(s, n), @(s, m))),

Mathematical Structures

86

@(RADD, nr, mr));
def Real = ?(s : Seq) @(Reg, s);
Elements of this type will be pair <s, p> with s a sequence and p a proof that
s is regular.
theory Real is
import Rat_Prop
dec RTWO : Rat;
dec RDIV : [Rat -> Rat -> Rat];
def Seq = [Nat -> Rat];
def Reg =
\(s : Seq)
!(m : Nat, n : Nat)
let mm = @(EE, @(SS, m)),
nn = @(EE, @(SS, n)) in
let mr = @(RAT, IONE, mm),
nr = @(RAT, IONE, nn) in
@(Rat_Less,
@(RABS, @(RMINUS, @(s, n), @(s, m))),
@(RADD, nr, mr));
def Real = ?(s : Seq) @(Reg, s);
%-- Addition --%
def TWO = @(SS, ONE);
def ADD_SEQ =
\(xs : Seq, ys : Seq)
\(n : Nat)
let nn = @(SS, @(TIMES, TWO, n)) in
@(RADD, @(xs, nn), @(ys, nn));
dec RRADD_Lem :
!(x : Real, y : Real)
let xs = @(FST, x),
ys = @(FST, y) in
let zs = @(ADD_SEQ, xs, ys) in
@(Reg, zs);
def RRADD =

Mathematical Structures

87

\(x : Real, y : Real)
let xs = @(FST, x),
ys = @(FST, y) in
let zs = @(ADD_SEQ, xs, ys) in
<zs, @(RRADD_Lem, x, y)>
end;

Beside definition of addition RRADD for reals, we can develop other arithmetic
operations in a similar way.

9.1.6

Lists

For any type A, an important type built from it are lists of elements from A. The
type is denoted in terms of PowerEpsilon @(List, A). All nonempty lists are
built from the empty list which is denoted by NIL. If a is an element of A, then
@(CONS, a, NIL) is a nonempty list. In general given a list t and an element
h of A, @(CONS, h, t) is a list with h as its head and t as its tail.
One important kind of construction arise from the inductive nature of lists.
We can build an object incrementally from the elements of a list following the
same pattern used to build the list. That is , a list starts from the empty list
NIL. Suppose that corresponding to it we can specify some object, such as the
natural number OO. Given a list t, we extend it by adjoining an element h to form
@(CONS, h, t). In the same way, given a value associated with t, say its length
v, we can build a value associated with @(CONS, h, t) incrementally, say by
adding one to v, thereby associating @(SS, OO) to @(CONS, h, t). This process
of associating a value with a list is sometimes called primitive recursion on lists
or list recursion or list induction. The critical components in this definition are
expression a for the value in the base case and an expression b for the value in
the inductive base. We can use a linear format to present this definition:
theory List is
import Bool
dec Uu : Prop;
dec Aa : Prop;
def BIF_THEN_ELSE

= @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Bool);

dec EQUAL : [Uu -> Uu -> Bool];
%-- Definition of List --%
def List = !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [Uu -> Ti -> Ti] -> Ti];
dec ERR : Uu;
dec ITM : Uu;

Mathematical Structures

88

dec LST : List;
%-- Function declarations for List --%
dec LISTREC : [List -> List -> [Uu -> List -> List -> List] -> List];
dec LEQUAL : [List -> List -> Bool];
def LIF_THEN_ELSE = @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, List);
%-- Function definitions for List --%
def NIL = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Uu -> Ti -> Ti]) x;
def CONS =
\(w : Uu, t : List) \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Uu -> Ti -> Ti])
@(y, w, @(t, Ti, x, y));
def T = \(x : List, y : List) x;
def F = \(x : List, y : List) y;
def HEAD = \(s : List) @(s, Uu, ERR, \(u : Uu, v : Uu) u);
def TAIL =
\(n : List)
@(n, [[List -> List -> List] -> List],
\(u : [List -> List -> List]) @(u, NIL, NIL),
\(e : Uu,
p : [[List -> List -> List] -> List],
v : [List -> List -> List])
@(v, @(CONS, e, @(p, T)), @(p, T)),
F);
def IS_EMPTY = \(s : List) @(s, Bool, TT, \(u : Uu, v : Bool) FF);
def IS_IN_LIST =
\(e : Uu, s : List)
@(s,
Bool,
FF,
\(u : Uu, v : Bool) @(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(EQUAL, u, e), TT, v));
def EQUAL =
\(s1 : List, s2 : List)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(AND, @(IS_EMPTY, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, s2)),
TT,
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,

Mathematical Structures

89

@(OR, @(IS_EMPTY, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, s2)),
FF,
@(s1,
Bool,
TT,
\(u1 : Uu, v1 : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(IS_IN_LIST, u1, s2), v1, FF))));
def CONCAT =
\(s1 : List, s2 : List)
@(s2, List, s1, \(u : Uu, v : List) @(CONS, u, v));
def LISTREC =
\(l : List, a : List, b : [Uu -> List -> List -> List])
@(l,
List,
a,
\(u : Uu, v : List)
@(b, @(HEAD, l), @(TAIL, l), @(LISTREC, @(TAIL, l), a, b)));
dec Rec :
!(P : [List -> Prop])
[@(P, NIL) ->
!(a : Uu, u : List) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(CONS, a, u))] ->
!(*n : List) @(P, *n)];
def Rec =
\(P : [List -> Prop])
\(a : @(P, NIL))
\(f : !(x : Uu, u : List) [@(P, u) -> @(P, @(CONS, x, u))])
\(*n : List)
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(P, *n),
@(LEQUAL, *n, NIL),
a,
@(f, @(HEAD, *n), @(TAIL, *n), @(Rec, P, a, f, @(TAIL, *n))))
end;

where given a list l, HEAD and TAIL are two functions to take its first and second
components respectively; IS EMPTY is a Boolean function to check whether a
given list l is empty or not; IS IN LIST is a Boolean function to check whether
for a given list l and an element x, x is in l or not; CONCAT is a function
concatenate two lists l1 and l2 into one; LEQUAL is a Boolean function to check
whether two given lists l1 and l2 are equal or not. LISTREC is a recursive
function on List. Rec is an inductive type defined on Prop and Rec0 is an
inductive type defined on Type(0) for List. Similarly, we may define Rec1,

Mathematical Structures

90

Rec2, . . . , and so on.
The abstract data type List can be parameterized by taking the type of
elements as a parameter.
theory List is
import Bool
dec Aa : Prop;
def BIF_THEN_ELSE = @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Bool);
%-- Definition of List --%
def List = \(Uu : Prop) !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [Uu -> Ti -> Ti] -> Ti];
dec LERR : !(Uu : Prop) Uu;
dec UEQUAL : !(Uu : Prop) [Uu -> Uu -> Bool];
dec HEAD : !(Uu : Prop) [@(List, Uu) -> Uu];
dec TAIL : !(Uu : Prop) [@(List, Uu) -> @(List, Uu)];
dec LISTREC :
!(Uu : Prop)
[@(List, Uu) -> Aa -> [Uu -> @(List, Uu) -> Aa -> Aa] -> Aa];
%-- Function definitions for List --%
def NIL =
\(Uu : Prop) \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Uu -> Ti -> Ti]) x;
def CONS =
\(Uu : Prop)
\(w : Uu, t : @(List, Uu))
\(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Uu -> Ti -> Ti])
@(y, w, @(t, Ti, x, y));
def HEAD =
\(Uu : Prop, l : @(List, Uu))
@(l, Uu, @(LERR, Uu), \(u : Uu, v : Uu) u);
def IS_EMPTY =
\(Uu : Prop, s : @(List, Uu))
@(s, Bool, TT, \(u : Uu, v : Bool) FF);
def IS_IN_LIST =
\(Uu : Prop, e : Uu, s : @(List, Uu))

Mathematical Structures

91

@(s,
Bool,
FF,
\(u : Uu, v : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(UEQUAL, Uu, u, e), TT, v));
def UEQUAL =
\(Uu : Prop, s1 : @(List, Uu), s2 : @(List, Uu))
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(AND, @(IS_EMPTY, Uu, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, Uu, s2)),
TT,
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(OR, @(IS_EMPTY, Uu, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, Uu, s2)),
FF,
@(s1,
Bool,
TT,
\(u1 : Uu, v1 : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(IS_IN_LIST, Uu, u1, s2), v1, FF))));
def CONCAT =
\(Uu : Prop, s1 : @(List, Uu), s2 : @(List, Uu))
@(s2,
@(List, Uu),
s1,
\(u : Uu, v : @(List, Uu)) @(CONS, Uu, u, v));
def LISTREC =
\(Uu : Prop,
l : @(List, Uu),
a : Aa,
b : [Uu -> @(List, Uu) -> Aa -> Aa])
@(l,
Aa,
a,
\(u : Uu, v : Aa)
@(b,
@(HEAD, Uu, l),
@(TAIL, Uu, l),
@(LISTREC, Uu, @(TAIL, Uu, l), a, b)))
end;

9.1.7

Sets

A similar data structure which is frequently used in programming languages is
Set.

Mathematical Structures

theory Set is
import Bool
dec Ui : Prop;
dec UEQUAL : [Ui -> Ui -> Bool];
def BIF_THEN_ELSE = @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Bool);
def Set = !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [Ui -> Ti -> Ti] -> Ti];
def SIF_THEN_ELSE = \(t : Bool, u : Set, v : Set) @(t, Set, u, v);
%-- Function definitions for Set --%
def EMPTY_SET = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ui -> Ti -> Ti]) x;
def INSERT =
\(w : Ui, t : Set) \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ui -> Ti -> Ti])
@(y, w, @(t, Ti, x, y));
def IS_EMPTY = \(s : Set) @(s, Bool, TT, \(u : Ui, v : Bool) FF);
def IS_IN_SET =
\(e : Ui, s : Set)
@(s,
Bool,
FF,
\(u : Ui, v : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(UEQUAL, u, e), TT, v));
def EQUAL =
\(s1 : Set, s2 : Set)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(AND, @(IS_EMPTY, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, s2)),
TT,
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(OR, @(IS_EMPTY, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, s2)),
FF,
@(s1,
Bool,
TT,
\(u1 : Ui, v1 : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(IS_IN_SET, u1, s2), v1, FF))));
def UNION = \(s1 : Set, s2 : Set) @(s2, Set, s1, INSERT);

92

Mathematical Structures

93

def INTERSECT =
\(s1 : Set, s2 : Set)
@(s1,
Set,
EMPTY_SET,
\(u1 : Ui, v1 : Set)
@(SIF_THEN_ELSE, @(IS_IN_SET, u1, s2), @(INSERT, u1, v1), v1))
end;

where IS EMPTY is a Boolean function to check whether a given set s is empty or
not; IS IN SET is a Boolean function to check, for a given set s and an element x,
whether x is in s or not; EQUAL is a Boolean function to check whether two given
sets s1 and s2 are equal or not; UNION is the union function and INTERSECT is
the intersection function for sets.
As in most programming languages, the abstract data type Set can be parameterized by taking the type of elements as a parameter.
theory Set is
import Bool
dec UEQUAL : \(Ui : Prop) [Ui -> Ui -> Bool];
def BIF_THEN_ELSE = @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Bool);
def Set = \(Ui : Prop) !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [Ui -> Ti -> Ti] -> Ti];
def SIF_THEN_ELSE =
\(Ui : Prop) \(t : Bool, u : @(Set, Ui), v : @(Set, Ui))
@(t, @(Set, Ui), u, v);
%-- Function definitions for Set --%
def EMPTY_SET =
\(Ui : Prop) \(Ti : Prop) \(x : Ti, y : [Ui -> Ti -> Ti]) x;
def INSERT =
\(Ui : Prop) \(w : Ui, t : @(Set, Ui))
\(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ui -> Ti -> Ti])
@(y, w, @(t, Ti, x, y));
def IS_EMPTY =
\(Ui : Prop) \(s : @(Set, Ui))
@(s, Bool, TT, \(u : Ui, v : Bool) FF);
def IS_IN_SET =
\(Ui : Prop)
\(e : Ui, s : @(Set, Ui))

Mathematical Structures

94

@(s,
Bool,
FF,
\(u : Ui, v : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(UEQUAL, Ui, u, e), TT, v));
def EQUAL =
\(Ui : Prop, s1 : @(Set, Ui), s2 : @(Set, Ui))
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(AND, @(IS_EMPTY, Ui, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, Ui, s2)),
TT,
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(OR, @(IS_EMPTY, Ui, s1), @(IS_EMPTY, Ui, s2)),
FF,
@(s1,
Bool,
TT,
\(u1 : Ui, v1 : Bool)
@(BIF_THEN_ELSE, @(IS_IN_SET, Ui, u1, s2), v1, FF))));
def UNION =
\(Ui : Prop, s1 : @(Set, Ui), s2 : @(Set, Ui))
@(s2,
@(Set, Ui),
s1,
\(u : Ui, v : @(Set, Ui)) @(INSERT, Ui, u, v));
def INTERSECT =
\(Ui : Prop)
\(s1 : @(Set, Ui), s2 : @(Set, Ui))
@(s1,
@(Set, Ui),
@(EMPTY_SET, Ui),
\(u1 : Ui, v1 : @(Set, Ui))
@(SIF_THEN_ELSE,
Ui,
@(IS_IN_SET, Ui, u1, s2),
@(INSERT, Ui, u1, v1),
v1))
end;

9.1.8

Binary Trees

Trees, graphs and related mathematical structures play an important role in
computer science. They make some ideas easier to understand, their properties
allow the understanding of efficiency of many algorithms, and they are fundamental parts of many algorithms. The binary tree, for example, is an important
concept in several sorting algorithms, in some storage allocation algorithms, and

Mathematical Structures

95

in compilers. Thus, it is important to understand these basic structures.
A binary tree is characterized by the fact that any node can have at most
two branches, i.e., there is no node with degree greater than two.
theory Bintree is
import Bool
dec U : Prop;
dec ERR : U;
%-- Definition of Bintree --%
def Bintree = !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [U -> Ti -> Ti -> Ti] -> Ti];
def NIL = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [U -> Ti -> Ti -> Ti]) x;
def COUPLE =
\(u : U,
s : Bintree,
t : Bintree,
T : Prop,
x : T,
y : [U -> T -> T -> T])
@(y, u, @(s, T, x, y), @(t, T, x, y));
def T = \(x : Bintree, y : Bintree) x;
def F = \(x : Bintree, y : Bintree) y;
def NODE = \(t : Bintree) @(t, U, ERR, \(u : U, v : U, w : U) u);
def LEFT =
\(n : Bintree)
@(n, [[Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree] -> Bintree],
\(u : [Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree]) @(u, NIL, NIL),
\(e : U,
l : [[Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree] -> Bintree],
r : [[Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree] -> Bintree],
v : [Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree])
@(v, @(COUPLE, e, @(l, T), @(r, T)), @(l, T)),
F);
def RIGHT =
\(n : Bintree)
@(n, [[Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree] -> Bintree],
\(u : [Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree]) @(u, NIL, NIL),
\(e : U,

Mathematical Structures

96

l : [[Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree] -> Bintree],
r : [[Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree] -> Bintree],
v : [Bintree -> Bintree -> Bintree])
@(v, @(COUPLE, e, @(l, T), @(r, T)), @(r, T)),
F);
dec EQUAL : [Bintree -> Bintree -> Bool];
dec Rec :
!(P : [Bintree -> Prop])
[@(P, NIL) ->
!(x : U, u : Bintree, v : Bintree)
[@(P, u) -> @(P, v) -> @(P, @(COUPLE, x, u, v))] ->
!(*n : Bintree) @(P, *n)];
def Rec =
\(P : [Bintree -> Prop])
\(a : @(P, NIL))
\(f : !(x : U, u : Bintree, v : Bintree)
[@(P, u) -> @(P, v) -> @(P, @(COUPLE, x, u, v))])
\(*n : Bintree)
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(P, *n),
@(EQUAL, *n, NIL),
a,
@(f,
@(NODE, *n),
@(LEFT, *n),
@(RIGHT, *n),
@(Rec, P, a, f, @(LEFT, *n)),
@(Rec, P, a, f, @(RIGHT, *n))))
end;

where an inductive type Rec has been defined for Bintree.

9.2

Logical Operations

9.2.1

Null Type

The Null type can be defined in terms of PowerEpsilon as Null = !(A :
Prop) A. Since Null has no proof at all.
theory Null is
def Null = !(T : Prop) T
end;

Mathematical Structures

9.2.2

97

Trivial Type

Just as the false proposition ‘falsity’ (which should not be confused with the
object FF of type Bool) was represented by the type Null, so the true proposition
is represented by the one-element type Triv. Why one element? The intuition
is that the proposition is valid for obvious reasons, so there is only one trivial
proof TRIV of it.
theory Trivial is
def Triv = !(T : Prop) [T -> T];
def TRIV = \(T : Prop, t : T) t
end;

9.2.3

Π-Type

Universal quantification, or general product, or Π-type, can be defined by a
generalization of binary product Pi, where the Π-introduction, i.e. universal
generalization, is proved by abstraction GEN and the Π-elimination is proved by
instantiation INST, i.e. application.
theory Pi is
def Pi =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pp : [Ai -> Prop])
!(x : Ai) @(Pp, x);
def GEN =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pp : [Ai -> Prop], Bi : T)
\(f : !(x : Ai) [Bi -> @(Pp, x)]) \(y : Bi, x : Ai)
@(f, x, y);
def INST =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pp : [Ai -> Prop])
\(x : Ai, p : @(Pi, T, Ai, Pp)) @(p, x)
end;

9.2.4

Σ-Types

Existential quantification, or general sum, or Σ-type, can be defined by a generalization of the binary sum Sigma, where the Σ-introduction is proved by EXIST
and the Σ-elimination is proved by WITNESS.
theory Sigma is
def Sigma =

Mathematical Structures

98

\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
!(Bi : T) [!(x : Ai) [@(Pi, x) -> Bi] -> Bi];
def EXIST =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
\(x : Ai, y : @(Pi, x), Bi : T, q : !(x : Ai) [@(Pi, x) -> Bi])
@(q, x, y);
def WITNESS =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pi : [Ai -> Prop], p : @(Sigma, T, Ai, Pi))
@(p, Ai, \(x : Ai, y : @(Pi, x)) x)
end;

The Sigma type presented here is usually called weak sum. One of the major
difference between the weak sum and the strong sum ?(x : A) B is that the
elimination-rule for the weak sum is too weak and, in particular, there is no
way to prove that the first component of a weak pair of type @(Sigma, Ai,
Pi) satisfies the property Bi.

9.2.5

Product Types (×)

Product of Two Types P and Q
Given two types U and V, we can construct their Cartesian product – a type
of which is a pair whose first component being U and whose second component
being V.
theory Product2 is
def Product =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop) !(Ti : Prop) [[Ui -> Vi -> Ti] -> Ti];
def PRODUCT =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop)
\(u : Ui, v : Vi, Ti : Prop, z : [Ui -> Vi -> Ti]) @(z, u, v);
def PRO1 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, t : @(Product, Ui, Vi))
@(t, Ui, \(x : Ui, y : Vi) x);
def PRO2 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, t : @(Product, Ui, Vi))
@(t, Vi, \(x : Ui, y : Vi) y);
def SPLIT =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
\(t : @(Product, Ui, Vi), f : [Ui -> Vi -> Wi])

Mathematical Structures

99

@(f, @(PRO1, Ui, Vi, t), @(PRO2, Ui, Vi, t));
def And = Product
end;

@(Product, P, Q) is a method for proving any proposition A, provided one has
a proof that A follows from P and Q. Note that the proof of product-introduction
PRODUCT above is a pairing algorithm, the two projection functions PRO1 and
PRO2 being the proofs of product-elimination on the left and on the right.
Product of Three Types U, V and W
Similarly we can define the intuitionistic product of three propositions P, Q and
R which represent the conjunction of three propositions:
theory Product3 is
import Product2
def Product3 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
@(Product, Ui, @(Product, Vi, Wi));
def PRODUCT3 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
\(u : Ui, v : Vi, w : Wi)
@(PRODUCT, Ui, @(Product, Vi, Wi), u, @(PRODUCT, Vi, Wi, v, w));
def PROJ1 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
\(t : @(Product3, Ui, Vi, Wi))
@(PRO1, Ui, @(Product, Vi, Wi), t);
def PROJ2 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
\(t : @(Product3, Ui, Vi, Wi))
@(PRO1, Vi, Wi, @(PRO2, Ui, @(Product, Vi, Wi), t));
def PROJ3 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
\(t : @(Product3, Ui, Vi, Wi))
@(PRO2, Vi, Wi, @(PRO2, Ui, @(Product, Vi, Wi), t));
def SPLIT3 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop, Wi : Prop)
\(Yi : Type(0), t : @(Product3, Ui, Vi, Wi))
\(f : [Ui -> Vi -> Wi -> Yi])

Mathematical Structures

100

@(f,
@(PROJ1, Ui, Vi, Wi, t),
@(PROJ2, Ui, Vi, Wi, t),
@(PROJ3, Ui, Vi, Wi, t))
end;

Product of Infinite Type Sequence
Consider the Cartesian product of an infinite sequence of types defined as a
function A : [Nat -> Prop]. Now, the product type of A is @(GProduct, A),
and @(GPRODUCT, A, a) is one of its element, if a is of type A. If t is an element
of type @(GProduct, A), then @(GPROJ, A, n, t) is its nth component.
theory GProduct is
import Nat
def GProduct =
\(A : [Nat -> Prop]) !(T : Prop) [!(n : Nat) @(A, n) -> T] -> T];
def GPRODUCT =
\(A : [Nat -> Prop)
\(a : !(n : Nat) @(A, n))
\(T : Prop, z : [!(n : Nat) @(A, n) -> T]) @(z, a);
def GPROJ =
\(A : [Nat -> Prop])
\(n : Nat, t : @(GProduct, A))
@(t, @(A, n), \(a : !(n : Nat) @(A, n)) @(a, n))
end;

9.2.6

Sum Types (+)

Sum of Two Types U and V
We may similarly construct the intuitionistic sum of two propositions P and Q
which represents the disjunction of two propositions:
theory Sum2 is
def Sum2 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop)
!(Ti : Prop) [[Ui -> Ti] -> [Vi -> Ti] -> Ti];
def INJ1 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop) \(u : Ui) \(Ti : Prop)

Mathematical Structures

101

\(x : [Ui -> Ti], y : [Vi -> Ti]) @(x, u);
def INJ2 =
\(Ui : Prop, Vi : Prop) \(v : Vi) \(Ti : Prop)
\(x : [Ui -> Ti], y : [Vi -> Ti]) @(y, v);
def CASE =
\(U : Prop, V : Prop, Ti : Prop)
\(x : [U -> Ti], y : [V -> Ti], s : @(Sum2, U, V))
@(s, Ti, x, y);
def WHEN =
\(U : Prop, V : Prop, W : Prop)
\(a : @(Sum2, U, V), c : [U -> W], d : [V -> W])
@(a,
W,
\(x : U) @(CASE, U, V, W, c, d, a),
\(y : V) @(CASE, U, V, W, c, d, a));
def Or = Sum2
end;

where the sum-elimination is proved by the conditional expression CASE and the
two sum-introduction rules correspond to the two injection functions INJ1 and
INJ2.
Sum of Three Types U, V and W
Similarly we can define the intuitionistic sum of three propositions P, Q and R
which represent the disjunction of three propositions:

def INJC1 =
\(U : Prop, V : Prop, W : Prop)

\(u : U) \(Ti : Type(0))

\(U : Prop, V : Prop, W : Prop)
\(v : V) \(Ti : Type(0))

Sum of Infinite Type Sequence
Consider the sum of an infinite sequence of types defined as a function A : [Nat

@(GSum, A), and @(GINJ, A, a) is its element, if a is of type A.
theory GSum is
import Nat
def GSum =
\(A : [Nat -> Prop]) !(T : Prop) [?(n : Nat) @(A, n) -> T] -> T];
def GINJ =
\(A : [Nat -> Prop)
\(a : ?(n : Nat) @(A, n))
\(T : Prop, z : [?(n : Nat) @(A, n) -> T]) @(z, a);
def GCASE =
\(A : [Nat -> Prop])

Mathematical Structures

103

\(n : Nat, a : [?(m : Nat) @(A, m) -> @(A, n)], t : @(GSum, A))
@(t, @(A, n), a)
end;

9.2.7

Subset Types

We may also define the intuitionistic subset of a given type with the subsetintroduction rule INTRO and the subset-elimination rule ELIMIT:
theory Subset is
def Subset =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
!(Bi : T) [!(x : Ai) [@(Pi, x) -> Bi] -> Bi];
def INTRO =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
\(x : Ai, y : @(Pi, x))
\(Bi : T) \(q : !(x : Ai) [@(Pi, x) -> Bi])
@(q, x, y);
def ELIMIT =
\(T : Kind, Ai : T, Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
\(p : @(Subset, T, Ai, Pi))
@(p, Ai, \(x : Ai, y : @(Pi, x)) x)
end;

There is a little difference between Subset and Sigma which can be seen as
follows:
def Subset =
\(T : Kind) \(Ai : T) \(Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
!(Bi : T) [!(x : Ai) [@(Pi, x) -> Bi] -> Ai];
def Sigma = \(T : Kind) \(Ai : T) \(Pi : [Ai -> Prop])
!(Bi : T) [!(x : Ai) [@(Pi, x) -> Bi] -> Bi];

9.2.8

Leibniz’s Equality

Leibniz’s equality is definable in PowerEpsilon:
theory Equal is
def Equal =
\(A : Type(0), x : A, y : A)
!(P : [A -> Prop]) [@(P, x) -> @(P, y)]
end;

Mathematical Structures

104

The equality satisfies the reflexive, symmetric and transitive properties, which
can be verified as follows:
dec REFLEX :
!(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop], x : A)
@(R, x, x);
dec SYMM :
!(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop], x : A, y : A)
[@(R, x, y) -> @(R, y, x)];
dec TRANS :
!(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop], x : A, y : A, z : A)
[@(R, x, y) -> @(R, y, z) -> @(R, x, z)];
def LEMMA1 = @(REFLEX, Ti, @(Equal, Ti));
def LEMMA2 = @(SYMM, Ti, @(Equal, Ti));
def LEMMA3 = @(TRANS, Ti, @(Equal, Ti))

The following results are delivered by PowerEpsilon system:
LEMMA1 : !(x : Ti) @(Equal, Ti, x, x)
LEMMA2 : !(x : Ti) !(y : Ti)
[@(Equal, Ti, x, y) -> @(Equal, Ti, y, x)]
LEMMA3 : !(x : Ti) !(y : Ti) !(z : Ti)
[@(Equal, Ti, x, y) ->
@(Equal, Ti, y, z) ->
@(Equal, Ti, x, z)]

9.2.9

Classical First-Order Logic

Classical reasoning is reasoning by contradiction. The contradiction, or absurd
proposition, prove every proposition A by mere application Null. The following
is a typical definition of classical logic.
theory Classical_logic is
import Null
def Neg = \(Ai : Prop) [Ai -> Null];
def Cont = \(Ai : Prop, Bi : Prop) [Ai -> Bi -> Null];
def And = \(Ai : Prop, Bi : Prop) @(Neg, @(Cont, Ai, Bi));
def Or = \(Ai : Prop, Bi : Prop) @(Cont, @(Neg, Ai), @(Neg, Bi));

Mathematical Structures

105

def Impl = \(Ai : Prop, Bi : Prop) @(Cont, Ai, @(Neg, Bi));
def Eq =
\(Ai : Prop, Bi : Prop)
@(And, @(Impl, Ai, Bi), @(Impl, Bi, Ai));
def Clos = \(Ai : Prop) @(Neg, @(Neg, Ai));
def Clas = \(Ai : Prop) [@(Clos, Ai) -> Ai];
def DENY = \(Ai : Prop, p : Ai, q : @(Neg, Ai)) @(q, p);
def ID = \(Ai : Prop, x : Ai) x;
def EXCLUD_MID = \(Ai : Prop) @(ID, @(Clos, Ai))
end;

9.3

Mathematical Structures

9.3.1

Ordinals

All the types considered above are very simple. With more complex types, we
may define richer data structures. For instance, we may define ordinal notations,
as an extension of the natural number above. We just enrich Nat with a limit
operation, which associates an ordinal to a sequence of ordinals, represented as
a function of domain Nat.
Ordinals are mainly used for three purposes:
• Proofs using transfinite induction;
• Construction using transfinite recursion;
• Measuring cardinalities.
Transfinite Induction
Let C be a class of ordinals for which
1. ∅ ∈ C
2. If α ∈ C, then α + 1 ∈ C
3. If a set of ordinals {αi } is contained as a subset in C, then Bigcupαi ∈ C.
Then C contains all ordinals. In concrete applications, the verification of (1)
and (3) are often trivial and are omitted.

Mathematical Structures

106

Transfinite Recursion
Let G be a function of sets whose values are sets. Then there exists a unique
function F on the ordinals such that
F (α) = G(The set of values of F on the elements of alpha)
In fact, this equality uniquely determines
F (0) = G(∅)
F (1) = G({F (0)})
F (2) = G({F (0), F (1)}), and so on.
Thus, if we consider the class C of ordinals α for which we define F with
the required property on the initial segment of ordinal <α , then C satisfies the
transfinite induction principles of (1)-(3), and therefore contains all the ordinals.
theory Ordinal is
import Nat
%-- Definition of Ordinal --%
def Ordinal =
!(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> [Ti -> Ti] -> [[Nat -> Ti] -> Ti] -> Ti];
def OZERO =
\(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ti -> Ti])
\(li : [[Nat -> Ti] -> Ti]) x;
def OSUCC =
\(a : Ordinal, Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : [Ti -> Ti])
\(li : [[Nat -> Ti] -> Ti]) @(y, @(a, Ti, x, y, li));
def OLIMT =
\(o : [Nat -> Ordinal])
\(Ti : Prop)
\(x : Ti, y : [Ti -> Ti]) \(li : [[Nat -> Ti] -> Ti])
@(li, \(n : Nat) @(o, n, Ti, x, y, li));
def FINITE = \(n : Nat) @(n, Ordinal, OZERO, OSUCC);
def OMEGA = @(OLIMT, FINITE);
def OADD =
\(n1 : Ordinal, n2 : Ordinal)
@(n2, Ordinal, n1, OSUCC, OLIMT);

Mathematical Structures

107

def OTIMES =
\(n1 : Ordinal, n2 : Ordinal)
@(n2, Ordinal, OZERO, @(OADD, n1), OLIMT);
def OEXP =
\(n1 : Ordinal, n2 : Ordinal)
@(n2, Ordinal, @(OSUCC, OZERO), @(OTIMES, n1), OLIMT);
def EPS = @(OMEGA, Ordinal, OZERO, @(OEXP, OMEGA), OLIMT);
def INCR = \(f : [Nat -> Nat], n : Nat) @(SS, @(f, n));
def ITER = \(f : [Nat -> Nat], n : Nat) @(n, Nat, n, f);
def DIAG = \(o : [Nat -> Nat -> Nat], n : Nat) @(o, n, n);
def FAST = \(a : Ordinal) @(a, [Nat -> Nat], SS, ITER, DIAG);
def SLOW = \(a : Ordinal) @(a, [Nat -> Nat], SS, INCR, DIAG)
end;

where OADD, OTIMES and OEXP are the addition, multiplication and exponentiation functions for ordinals respectively. The ordinal EPS can be obtained
by iterating OEXP. FINITE is a function to coerce a natural number into the
corresponding ordinal; OMEGA is the first transfinite ordinal.
Still the transfinite induction rule can be defined with PowerEpsilon using
the induction scheme we have just introduced by adding one more condition for
the limit ordinals. However, loosely speaking, we may view the limit ordinals
as the special kinds of successor operators so the induction scheme defined so
far remains to be available.
For define the transfinite induction rule, we need the following auxiliary
types and functions:
dec OPRED : [Ordinal -> Ordinal];
def Trival = !(Ti : Prop) [Ti -> Ti -> Ti -> Ti];
def T1 = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : Ti, z : Ti) x;
def T2 = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : Ti, z : Ti) y;
def T3 = \(Ti : Prop, x : Ti, y : Ti, z : Ti) z;
def OCASE =
\(Ti : Prop, t : Trival) \(u : Ti, v : Ti, w : Ti)
@(t, Ti, u, v, w);
def OWHEN =
\(o : Ordinal)
@(o, Trival, T1, \(y : Trival) T2, \(l : [Nat -> Trival]) T3);

Mathematical Structures

108

def OFSET = \(o : Ordinal, l : Nat) @(l, Ordinal, o, OSUCC);

We now define the transfinite induction rule Rec of ordinals. The type of Rec
is as follows:
dec Rec :
!(P : [Ordinal -> Prop])
[@(P, OZERO) ->
!(u : Ordinal)
[@(P, u) -> @(P, @(OSUCC, u))] ->
!(f : [Nat -> Ordinal])
[!(n : Nat) @(P, @(f, n)) -> @(P, @(OLIMT, f))] ->
!(*n : Ordinal) @(P, *n)];

The inference rule of Rec is defined as follows:
def Rec =
\(P : [Ordinal -> Prop])
\(a : @(P, OZERO))
\(f : !(u : Ordinal)
[@(P, u) -> @(P, @(OSUCC, u))])
\(g : !(h : [Nat -> Ordinal])
[!(n : Nat) @(P, @(h, n)) -> @(P, @(OLIMT, h))])
\(*n : Ordinal)
@(OCASE,
@(P, *n),
@(OWHEN, *n),
a,
@(f,
@(OPRED, *n),
@(Rec,
P,
a,
f,
g,
@(OPRED, *n))),
@(g,
@(OFSET, *n),
\(m : Nat)
@(Rec, P, a, f, g, @(OFSET, *n, m))))

9.3.2

Semi-Group

Generally, an abstract mathematical theory can be viewed as an algebraic system which basically consists of a signature (a set of basic notions such as constants and function symbols), a set of hypotheses (axioms) and the proved
theorems (possibly together with their proofs).

Mathematical Structures

109

We can express the abstract mathematical theories by using either strongproduct types or strong-sum types. For instance, a semi-group SG might be
expressed as
def SG = !(s : Sig_SG) @(Ax_SG, s);
where Sig SG = !(X : Type(0)) [X -> X -> X]; or
def SG = ?(s : Sig_SG) @(Ax_SG, s);
where Sig SG = ?(X : Type(0)) [X -> X -> X].
For the former case, the proof of SG is a function p = \(s : Sig SG) t
such that @(p, a) ∈ @(Ax SG, a) provided that a ∈ Sig SG; for the later case,
the proof of SG is a pair of <a, b> such that a ∈ Sig SG and b ∈ @(Ax SG, a).
As we can see that the later approach is better than former one, since in the first
case we can only get one proof @(p, a) to show that for a given a ∈ Sig SG,
@(p, a) ∈ @(Ax SG, a). However, we cannot prove the existence of such a. So
we take the later approach to model any abstract mathematical theories.
Informally a semi-group is a tuple hA, +i consisting of a set A and a binary
operation + on A, such that the + is associative.
The following is the definition of a semi-group in terms of PowerEpsilon.
We also give a proof to show that the set of natural numbers is a semi-group:
theory Semi_group is
import Equal, Recnat
def Add = \(A : Type(0)) [A -> A -> A];
def Ass_ax =
\(A : Type(0), s : [A -> A -> A])
!(z : A, x : A, y : A)
@(Equal, A, @(s, x, @(s, y, z)), @(s, @(s, x, y), z));
def Ax_SG =
\(A : Type(0), s : [A -> A -> A]) @(Ass_ax, A, s);
def Semi_group =
?(A : Type(0), s : @(Add, A)) @(Ass_ax, A, s);
def SG_Gen =
\(A : Type(0), a : @(Add, A), p : @(Ax_SG, A, a)) <A, a, p>;
def Ass_law =
\(p : Nat)
!(m : Nat, n : Nat)
@(Equal, Nat, @(ADD, m, @(ADD, n, p)), @(ADD, @(ADD, m, n), p));

Mathematical Structures

110

dec Lemma0 :
!(x : Nat, y : Nat)
[@(Equal, Nat, x, y) -> @(Equal, Nat, @(SS, x), @(SS, y))];
dec Lemma1 :
!(x : Nat, y : Nat, u : Nat, v : Nat)
[@(Equal, Nat, @(SS, @(ADD, x, y)), @(SS, @(ADD, u, v))) ->
@(Equal, Nat, @(ADD, x, @(SS, y)), @(ADD, u, @(SS, v)))];
dec Lemma2 :
!(x : Nat, y : Nat, z : Nat, u : Nat)
[@(Equal, Nat, @(ADD, x, @(SS, @(ADD, y, z))), u) ->
@(Equal, Nat, @(ADD, x, @(ADD, y, @(SS, z))), u)];
def Lemma3 =
\(p : Nat)
\(f : !(m : Nat, n : Nat)
@(Equal,
Nat,
@(ADD, m, @(ADD, n, p)),
@(ADD, @(ADD, m, n), p)))
\(u : Nat, v : Nat)
@(Lemma0,
@(ADD, u, @(ADD, v, p)),
@(ADD, @(ADD, u, v), p), @(f, u, v));
def Lemma4 =
\(p : Nat)
\(f : !(m : Nat, n : Nat)
@(Equal,
Nat,
@(SS, @(ADD, m, @(ADD, n, p))),
@(SS, @(ADD, @(ADD, m, n), p))))
\(u : Nat, v : Nat)
@(Lemma1, u, @(ADD, v, p), @(ADD, u, v), p, @(f, u, v));
def Lemma5 =
\(p : Nat)
\(f : !(m : Nat, n : Nat)
@(Equal,
Nat,
@(ADD, m, @(SS, @(ADD, n, p))),
@(ADD, @(ADD, m, n), @(SS, p))))
\(u : Nat, v : Nat)
@(Lemma2, u, v, p, @(ADD, @(ADD, u, v), @(SS, p)), @(f, u, v));
dec Init : @(Ass_law, OO);

Mathematical Structures

111

dec Induct : !(u : Nat) [@(Ass_law, u) -> @(Ass_law, @(SS, u))];
def Induct =
\(u : Nat, f : @(Ass_law, u))
@(Lemma5, u, @(Lemma4, u, @(Lemma3, u, f)));
def Ass_pr = @(Rec, Ass_law, Init, Induct);
def Nat_SG = @(SG_Gen, Nat, ADD, Ass_pr)
end;

where Ass pr is a proof to show that Nat satisfies the associative property and
the Nat SG is a proof to show that Nat is a semi-group.

9.3.3

Group

Informally a group is a triple hA, +, 0i consisting of a set A, a binary operation
+ on A and an element 0 (zero) of A, such that the following conditions are
met:
• + is associative.
• 0 is an identity element, i.e., ∀(x : A)x + 0 = x.
• Inverses exist, i.e., ∀(x : A)∃(y : A)x + y = 0.
The following is the definition of a group in terms of PowerEpsilon:
theory Group is
import Logic, Semigroup
def O = \(A : Type(0)) A;
def Ide_ax =
\(A : Type(0))
\(o : A, s : [A -> A -> A])
!(x : A)
@(And,
@(Equal, A, @(s, x, o), x),
@(Equal, A, @(s, o, x), x));
def Inv_ax =
\(A : Type(0))
\(o : A, s : [A -> A -> A])
!(x : A) ?(y : A)
@(And,

Mathematical Structures

112

@(Equal, A, @(s, x, y), o),
@(Equal, A, @(s, y, x), o));
def Ax_G =
\(A : Type(0))
\(o : A, s : [A -> A -> A])
@(And,
@(Ide_ax, A, o, s),
@(And, @(Ass_ax, A, s), @(Inv_ax, A, o, s)));
def Group =
\(A : Type(0))
\(o : @(O, A), s : @(Add, A)) @(Ax_G, A, o, s);
def GR_Gen =
\(A : Type(0))
\(o : @(O, A), a : @(Add, A), p : @(Ax_G, A, o, a)) p
end;

9.3.4

Abelian-Group

An Abelian group is a triple hA, +, 0i consisting of a set A, a binary operation
+ on A and an element 0 (zero) of A, such that the following conditions are
met:
• + is associative and commutative.
• 0 is an identity element, i.e., ∀(x : A)x + 0 = x.
• Inverses exist, i.e., ∀(x : A)∃(y : A)x + y = 0.
The following is the definition of an Abelian group in terms of PowerEpsilon:
theory Abelgroup is
import Group
def Com_ax =
\(A : Type(0), s : [A -> A -> A]) !(x : A, y : A)
@(Equal, A, @(s, x, y), @(s, y, x));
def Ax_AG =
\(A : Type(0), o : @(O, A), s : @(Add, A))
@(And,
@(Ide_ax, A, o, s),
@(And,
@(Ass_ax, A, s),
@(And, @(Com_ax, A, s), @(Inv_ax, A, o, s))));

Mathematical Structures

113

def Abelian_group =
?(A : Type(0), o : @(O, A), s : @(Add, A)) @(Ax_AG, A, o, s);
def AG_Gen =
\(A : Type(0), o : @(O, A), a : @(Add, A), p : @(Ax_AG, A, o, a))
<A, o, a, p>
end;

9.3.5

Ring

A commutative ring is a quintuple hD, +, ∗, 0, 1i consisting of a set D, binary
operations + and ∗ on D, and distinguished elements 0 and 1 of D, such that
the following conditions are met:
• hD, +, 0i is an Abelian group.
• The operation ∗ is associative and commutative, and is distributive over
addition +.
theory Ring is
import Abelgroup, Monoi
def Dis_ax =
\(A : Type(0), m : [A -> A -> A], a : [A -> A -> A])
!(x : A, y : A, z : A)
@(And,
@(Equal, A, @(m, x, @(a, y, z)), @(a, @(m, x, y), @(m, x, z))),
@(Equal, A, @(m, @(a, x, y), z), @(a, @(m, x, z), @(m, y, z))));
def Ring =
?(A : Type(0))
?(o : @(O, A), i : @(O, A), a : @(Add, A), t : @(Add, A))
@(And,
@(Ax_AG, A, o, a),
@(And, @(Ax_MO, A, i, t), @(Dis_ax, A, a, t)));
def CRing =
?(A : Type(0))
?(o : @(O, A), i : @(O, A), a : @(Add, A), t : @(Add, A))
@(And, @(Ax_AG, A, o, a),
@(And, @(Ax_MO, A, i, t),
@(And, @(Com_ax, A, t),
@(And,
@(Dis_ax, A, a, t),
@(Neg, @(Equal, A, o, i))))));

Mathematical Structures

114

def RingGen =
\(A : Type(0), o : @(O, A), a : @(Add, A))
\(p : @(Ax_AG, A, o, a), i : @(O, A), t : @(Add, A))
\(q : @(Ax_MO, A, i, t), d : @(Dis_ax, A, t, a))
<A,
o,
i,
a,
t,
@(ANDS,
@(Ax_AG, A, o, a),
@(And, @(Ax_MO, A, i, t), @(Dis_ax, A, t, a)),
p,

end;

9.3.6

Integral-Domain

An integral domain is a commutative ring with identity with the additional
properties:
• 1 is a multiplicative identity (x ∗ 1 = x) and 0 6= 1.
• If x 6= 0 and y 6= 0, then also x ∗ y 6= 0.
theory Intdomain is
import CRing
def Nzr_ax =
\(A : Type(0), o : A, t : [A -> A -> A])

[@(And, @(Neg, @(Equal, A, x, o)), @(Neg, @(Equal, A, y, o))) ->
@(Neg, @(Equal, A, @(t, x, y), o))];

\(A : Type(0))

def Int_Domain =
?(A : Type(0))

9.3.7

Field

A field is a commutative ring with identity in which multiplicative inverse exist:
• 1 is a multiplicative identity (x ∗ 1 = x) and 0 6= 1.
• If x is a nonzero element of D, then ∃(y : D)x ∗ y = 1.
theory Field is
import CRing
def Nzr_ax =
\(A : Type(0), o : A, t : [A -> A -> A])

[@(And, @(Neg, @(Equal, A, x, o)), @(Neg, @(Equal, A, y, o))) ->
@(Neg, @(Equal, A, @(t, x, y), o))];

\(A : Type(0))

@(And, @(Ax_CRG, A, o, i, a, t), @(Nzr_ax, A, o, t));

?(A : Type(0))

\(A : Type(0))

9.3.8

Ordered-Field

An ordered field is a sextuple hD, +, ∗, 0, 1, <i such that the following conditions
are met:
• hD, +, ∗, 0, 1i is a field.
• < is a linear ordering on D.
• Order is preserved by addition and by multiplication by a positive element:
x<y ⇔x+x<y+z
If 0 < z, then
x<y ⇔x∗z <y∗z
theory Orderfield is
import Field
def Reflex =
\(A : Type(0)) \(R : [A -> A -> Prop]) !(x : A)
@(R, x, x);
def Symm =
\(A : Type(0)) \(R : [A -> A -> Prop]) !(x : A, y : A)
[@(R, x, y) -> @(R, y, x)];
def Trans =
\(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop]) !(x : A, y : A, z : A)
[@(R, x, y) -> @(R, y, z) -> @(R, x, z)];
def Trich =
\(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop])
!(x : A, y : A)
@(Or, @(R, x, y), @(Or, @(R, y, x), @(Equal, A, x, y)));
def Equiv =
\(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop], x : A, y : A, u : A, v : A)
@(And,
[@(R, x, y) -> @(R, u, v)],
[@(R, u, v) -> @(R, x, y)]);
def Lod_ax =
\(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop])

Mathematical Structures

117

@(And, @(Trans, A, R), @(Trich, A, R));
def Aod_ax =
\(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop], a : [A -> A -> A])
!(x : A, y : A, z : A)
@(Equiv, A, R, x, y, @(a, x, z), @(a, y, z));
def Tod_ax =
\(A : Type(0), R : [A -> A -> Prop], o : A, t : [A -> A -> A])
!(x : A, y : A, z : A)
[@(R, o, z) -> @(Equiv, A, R, x, y, @(t, x, z), @(t, y, z))];
def Ax_OFD =
\(A : Type(0))
\(o : @(O, A), i : @(O, A), a : @(Add, A), t : @(Add, A))
\(R : [A -> A -> Prop])
@(And,
@(Ax_FD, A, o, i, a, t),
@(And,
@(Lod_ax, A, R),
@(And, @(Aod_ax, A, R, a), @(Tod_ax, A, R, o, t))));
def OField =
?(A : Type(0))

\(A : Type(0))

\(p : @(Ax_OFD, A, o, i, a, t, R)) <A, o, i, a, t, R, p>
end;

9.3.10

Category

The concepts of morphism and universality occur for all types of algebraic systems. Their properties may be expressed in terms of the ”category” of all systems of a given type. The following is the definition of the concepts of categories
and functors.
theory Category is
import Logic, Equal
def Ide_ax =
\(A : Type(0))
\(Hom : [A -> A -> Prop])
\(Com : !(X : A, Y : A, Z : A)
[@(Hom, Y, Z) -> @(Hom, X, Y) -> @(Hom, X, Z)])
!(Y : A)
?(IY : @(Hom, Y, Y))
!(X : A, Z : A, f : @(Hom, X, Y), g : @(Hom, Y, Z))
@(And,
@(Equal, @(Hom, X, Y), @(Com, X, Y, Y, IY, f), f),
@(Equal, @(Hom, Y, Z), @(Com, Y, Y, Z, g, IY), g));
def Ass_ax =
\(A : Type(0))

Mathematical Structures

\(Hom : [A -> A -> Prop])
\(Com : !(X : A, Y : A, Z : A)
[@(Hom, Y, Z) -> @(Hom, X, Y) -> @(Hom, X, Z)])
!(W : A, X : A, Y : A, Z : A)
!(h : @(Hom, Z, W), g : @(Hom, Y, Z), f : @(Hom, X, Y))
@(Equal,
@(Hom, X, W),
@(Com, X, Z, W, h, @(Com, X, Y, Z, g, f)),
@(Com, X, Y, W, @(Com, Y, Z, W, h, g), f));
def Ax_Cat =
\(A : Type(0), Hom : [A -> A -> Prop])
\(Com : !(X : A, Y : A, Z : A)
[@(Hom, Y, Z) -> @(Hom, X, Y) -> @(Hom, X, Z)])
@(And, @(Ide_ax, A, Hom, Com), @(Ass_ax, A, Hom, Com));
def Category =
?(A : Type(0))
?(Hom : [A -> A -> Prop])
?(Com : !(X : A, Y : A, Z : A)
[@(Hom, Y, Z) -> @(Hom, X, Y) -> @(Hom, X, Z)])
@(Ax_Cat, A, Hom, Com);
def Idt_ax =
\(A : Category, B : Category)
\(OF : [@(FST, A) -> @(FST, B)])
\(MF : !(U : @(FST, A), V : @(FST, A))
[@(FST, @(SND, A), U, V) ->
@(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, U), @(OF, V))])
!(X : @(FST, A))
!(IA : @(FST, @(SND, A), X, X))
!(IB : @(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, X), @(OF, X)))
@(Equal,
@(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, X), @(OF, X)),
@(MF, X, X, IA),
IB);
def Mor_ax =
\(A : Category, B : Category)
\(OF : [@(FST, A) -> @(FST, B)])
\(MF : !(U : @(FST, A), V : @(FST, A))
[@(FST, @(SND, A), U, V) ->
@(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, U), @(OF, V))])
!(X : @(FST, A), Y : @(FST, A), Z : @(FST, A))
!(f : @(FST, @(SND, A), X, Y), g : @(FST, @(SND, A), Y, Z))
@(Equal,
@(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, X), @(OF, Z)),

119

Mathematical Structures

120

@(MF, X, Z, @(FST, @(SND, @(SND, A)), X, Y, Z, g, f)),
@(FST,
@(SND, @(SND, B)),
@(OF, X),
@(OF, Y),
@(OF, Z),
@(MF, Y, Z, g),
@(MF, X, Y, f)));
def Ax_Fct =
\(A : Category, B : Category)
\(OF : [@(FST, A) -> @(FST, B)])
\(MF : !(U : @(FST, A), V : @(FST, A))
[@(FST, @(SND, A), U, V) ->
@(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, U), @(OF, V))])
@(And, @(Idt_ax, A, B, OF, MF), @(Mor_ax, A, B, OF, MF));
def Functor =
?(A : Category, B : Category)
?(OF : [@(FST, A) -> @(FST, B)])
?(MF : !(U : @(FST, A), V : @(FST, A))
[@(FST, @(SND, A), U, V) ->
@(FST, @(SND, B), @(OF, U), @(OF, V))])
@(Ax_Fct, A, B, OF, MF)
end;

9.3.11

First-Order Peano Systems

We now give an axiomatization of natural numbers. The Peano axiomatic system are easily formalized as a first-order theory P. P consists of the following
basic predicates:
theory Peano_system is
import Nat, Equal, Logic
def NEqual = @(Equal, Nat);
dec ZERO : Nat;
dec SUCC : [Nat -> Nat];
dec PP3 :
!(x : Nat, y : Nat, z : Nat)
[@(And,
@(NEqual, z, @(SUCC, x)),
@(NEqual, z, @(SUCC, y))) ->

Mathematical Structures

121

@(NEqual, x, y)];
dec PP4 : !(x : Nat) @(Neg, @(NEqual, ZERO, @(SUCC, x)));
dec PP5 :
!(Fi : [Nat -> Prop])
[@(And,
@(Fi, ZERO),
!(x : Nat, y : Nat)
[@(And, @(NEqual, y, @(SUCC, x)), @(Fi, x)) -> @(Fi, y)]) ->
!(z : Nat) @(Fi, z)]
end;

9.3.12

Zermelo-Fraenkel Set Theory

In Section 9.1.7, an informal presentation of sets is described. In this section
we give an axiomatization of set theory known as Zermelo-Fraenkel Set Theory.
theory Zf_set is
import Classical_logic
dec Set : Type(0);
dec In
: [Set -> Set -> Prop];
dec Equal
: [Set -> Set -> Prop];
dec Include : [Set -> Set -> Prop];
def Negs = \(Ai : Type(0)) [Ai -> Null];
def Conts = \(Ai : Type(0), Bi : Type(0)) [Ai -> Bi -> Null];
def Ands =
\(Ai : Type(0), Bi : Type(0))
@(Negs, @(Conts, Ai, Bi));
dec NULL : Set;
dec UNION : [Set -> Set -> Set];
dec SUCC : [Set -> Set];
dec AXIOM1 :
!(a : Set, b : Set, x : Set)
[@(Eq, @(In, x, a), @(In, x, b)) -> @(Equal, a, b)];
dec AXIOM2 :
!(Pi : [Set -> Prop])
!(a : Set) ?(b : Set) !(x : Set)

Mathematical Structures

@(Eq, @(In, x, b), @(And, @(In, x, a), @(Pi, x)));
dec AXIOM3 :
!(a : Set, b : Set) ?(c : Set)
@(And, @(In, a, c), @(In, b, c));
dec AXIOM4 :
!(c : Set) ?(a : Set) !(x : Set)
[?(b : Set) @(And, @(In, x, b), @(In, b, c)) -> @(In, x, a)];
dec AXIOM5 :
!(a : Set) ?(b : Set) !(x : Set)
[@(Include, x, a) -> @(In, x, b)];
dec AXIOM6 :
?(a : Set)
@(Ands,
?(b : Set) @(And, @(Equal, b, NULL), @(In, b, a)),
!(x : Set)
[@(In, x, a) ->
?(y : Set)
@(And,
@(Equal, y, @(UNION, x, @(SUCC, x))),
@(In, y, a))])
end;
