Chapter 10

Computational Reflection
in PowerEpsilon
Reflective programming languages were introduced in order to study programs
that need knowledge of their own behavior. In artificial intelligence, this kind of
knowledge is needed, for example, in programs that must explain their behavior
to a user. In the study of programming languages, a similar phenomenon occurs
in extensible languages, that is, languages in which one can write programs that
change the language itself. Lisp is such a language: the user can change the
language itself by defining new special forms, which effectively cause new lines to
be added to the Lisp interpreter. The term reflection has first been introduced
in logic to denote a way of extending theories. Afterwards, it has been used
to express the relationships between the theory and meta-theory in logic-based
reasoning systems and more recently, it has been adopted as a general and
powerful programming language construct.
PowerEpsilon is highly reflexive language. In PowerEpsilon the thirdorder polymorphic lambda-calculus (P3 ) is powerful enough to represent programs written in the second-order polymorphic lambda-calculus (P2 ) and also
the functions reify and reflect. This allows the definition of EVAL for P2 in P3 .
The analog of the structure of the reflective tower emerges when we considers
the restriction of PowerEpsilon to types of order n (Pn ). Then Pn+1 is powerful enough to allow the definition of Pn for n ≥ 6. The following is an example
of definition of P2 in terms of P3 .

The following results can be obtained by running PowerEpsilon which prove

Chapter 11

Proof Development
11.1

Tarski’s Fixpoint Lemma

We now present a simple example of a higher-order proof. The goal is to prove
Tarski’s fixpoint lemma.
Lemma 11.1 (Tarski’s Lemma) A function monotonous over a complete partial
ordering admits a fixpoint.

Informally, let Ti be a set, Le (”≤”) a transitive relation over Ti which is
complete, in the sense that every subset of Ti has a least upper bound. Let FF
: [Ti -> Ti] be monotonously increasing. Then FF admits a fixpoint.
We must now formalize the notions of set, subset and fixpoint:
• A simple attempt at axiomatizing sets consists in assuming some type Ti
given with an equality relation Eq, and to represent sets in the ”universe”
Ti by their characteristic predicate, i.e. as elements of type Ti -> Prop.
• For fixpoint, we need to require that for some x, we have @(Le, @(FF,
x), x) and @(Le, x, @(FF, x)). That is, the only property of equality
that needed here is the fact that Le is anti-symmetric.
The proposition LETRANS says that Le is a transitive relation defined on Ti,
and the proposition LEANTISYM says that Le is anti-symmetric. The LUB is a
function which takes a set of Ti as the input and return its least upper bound.
The proposition UPPERB says that, for every Pi – the set of Ti, and every y of
type Ti, if y is a member of Pi, then y is less than @(LUB, Pi). The proposition
LEAST says that for every Pi – the set of Ti, and every y of type Ti, if, for every
z of type Ti, if z is member of Pi, then z is less than y, then @(LUB, Pi) is less
than y.

128

CHAPTER 11. PROOF DEVELOPMENT

129

The FF is arbitrarily given function defined on Ti. The proposition INCR
says that, for every x and y of type Ti, if x is less than y, then @(FF, x) is less
than @(FF, y).
We can then construct a set Po of Ti which contains all the u of type Ti
with the property of @(Le, u, @(FF, u)). Let XO be the least upper bound of
Po.
To prove the Tarski’s lemma, we need to prove the following lemmas: LEMMA1,
LEMMA2 and LEMMA3. The lemma LEMMA1 says that for every x of type Ti, if x
is less than @(FF, x), then XO is less than @(FF, XO). The lemma LEMMA2 says
that XO is less than @(FF, XO). The lemma LEMMA3 says that @(FF, XO) is less
than XO.
By combining LEMMA2 and LEMMA3, we can finally prove that XO is the fixpoint
for an arbitrarily given monotonous function FF over a complete partial ordering.
We therefore, obtain the following proof for Tarski’s lemma in terms of PowerEpsilon:
theory Tarski_lemma is
dec Ti : Type(0);
dec Eq : [Ti -> Ti -> Prop];
dec Le : [Ti -> Ti -> Prop];
dec LETRANS :
!(x : Ti, y : Ti, z : Ti)
[@(Le, x, y) -> @(Le, y, z) -> @(Le, x, z)];
dec LEANTISYM :
!(x : Ti, y : Ti)
[@(Le, x, y) -> @(Le, y, x) -> @(Eq, x, y)];
dec LUB : [[Ti -> Prop] -> Ti];
dec UPPERB :
!(Pi : [Ti -> Prop], y : Ti)
[@(Pi, y) -> @(Le, y, @(LUB, Pi))];
dec LEAST :
!(Pi : [Ti -> Prop], y : Ti)
[!(z : Ti) [@(Pi, z) -> @(Le, z, y)] -> @(Le, @(LUB, Pi), y)];
dec FF : [Ti -> Ti];
dec INCR :
!(x : Ti, y : Ti)
[@(Le, x, y) -> @(Le, @(FF, x), @(FF, y))];
def Po = \(u : Ti) @(Le, u, @(FF, u));

CHAPTER 11. PROOF DEVELOPMENT

130

def XO = @(LUB, Po);
def LEMMA1 =
\(x : Ti, assum : @(Le, x, @(FF, x)))
@(LETRANS,
x,
@(FF, x),
@(FF, XO),
assum,
@(INCR, x, XO, @(UPPERB, Po, x, assum)));
def LEMMA2 =
@(LEAST,
Po,
@(FF, XO),
\(z : Ti, assum : @(Po, z)) @(LEMMA1, z, assum));
def LEMMA3 =
@(UPPERB,
Po,
@(FF, XO),
@(INCR, XO, @(FF, XO), LEMMA2));
def THEOREM = @(LEANTISYM, @(FF, XO), XO, LEMMA3, LEMMA2)
end;

The following is the output produced by PowerEpsilon:
LEMMA1 : !(x : Ti) [@(Le, x, @(FF, x)) -> @(Le, x, @(FF, XO))]
LEMMA2 : @(Le, XO, @(FF, XO))
LEMMA3 : @(Le, @(FF, XO), XO)
THEOREM : @(Eq, @(FF, XO), XO)

11.2

Strongly Normalization of F

System F is a second-order typed λ-calculus formulated by Girard [Gir 72,
Gir 89]. It is an extension of the simple typed λ-calculus by adding an operation of abstraction on types. This operation is extremely powerful and in
particular all the usual data types such as natural numbers, lists and trees are
definable.
Since the calculus is composed of only a few constructs, F is a useful tool for
studying and giving semantics to programming languages where types appear
explicitly as parameters.
Strong normalization property states that all terms of F are strongly normalizable, and the normal form is unique. This property means that in F every

CHAPTER 11. PROOF DEVELOPMENT

131

potentially infinite sequence of reduction steps terminates in a normal form due
to strong normalization, and the functions to be defined are all the total computable functions. So the collection of functions definable by F are much smaller
than untyped λ-calculus representing all the computable functions.
A full version of proof of strongly normalization of F has been studied in
terms of PowerEpsilon. The whole proof contains about 1828 lines of PowerEpsilon statements.

Chapter 12

Program Development in
PowerEpsilon
One of the main reasons for using type theory for programming is that it is
a theory both for writing specifications and constructing programs. In type
theory, a specification is expressed by a type and an element of that type is a
program that satisfies the specification. The method corresponds to theorem
proving in mathematics: the specification plays the role of the proposition to
be proved and the program is obtained from the proof.
In this chapter, two programs are developed to show the use of the methodology on slightly larger and complicated problems. Each has a few further lessons
to offer on the subject of program development.

12.1

Program Specification

A problem is sometimes specified in a manner that leads itself to several interpretations. Hence, it is reasonable to spend some time making the specification
as clear and unambiguous as possible. Moreover, the form of the specification
can influence algorithmic development, so that striving for simplicity and elegance should be helpful. With some problems, the major difficulty is making the
specification simple and precise, and subsequent development of the program is
fairly straightforward.
Often, a specification may be in natural language or in some conventional
notation, that is at too “high a level” for program development, and it may
contain abbreviations dealing with the applications area with which the programmer is unfamiliar. The specification is written to convey what the program
is to do, and abstraction is often used simplify it. More detail may be required
to determine how to do it.
Program development in constructive type theoretical setting begins with
132

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

133

a specification, this is a representation of the relationship between the input
and output. A specification should be a description of the purpose or expected
behavior of the desired program. In general, the specification has the following form in terms of PowerEpsilon: !(x : A) ?(y : B) @(Spec, x, y).
Here, x denotes the input of desired program and y denotes its output. The
predicate @(Spec, x, y) describes the relationship between the input x and
the output y which is intended to satisfy. This specification is acting as a theorem, to derive a program from such a specification is equivalent to prove this
theorem. The proof of this theorem must be constructive, in the sense that,
in proving the existence of a satisfactory output y, it must tell us how to find
such an output. From this proof, a program to compute y can be extracted.
What we seek is, in fact, a function f which returns y corresponding to each x,
so we may modify the specification to ?(f : [A -> B]) !(x : A) @(Spec,
x, @(f, x)). What will a proof of this formula consist of? It will be a pair
<f, p> with f : [A -> B] and p a proof that for all x we have @(Spec, x,
@(f, x)). This pair consists precisely of the function required together with a
proof that has the property required of it.
However, there is a natural connection between this two kinds of specifications. We can apply the axiom of choice to the first to obtain the second; indeed
given this axiom the two specifications are logically equivalent. The axiom of
choice is the statement:
dec Choice_Axm :
[!(x : A) ?(y : B) @(Spec, x, y) ->
?(f : [A -> B]) !(x : A) @(Spec, x, @(f, x))]

It is not hard to show that is type is inhabited. Indeed, we can obtain a program
from a proof by proving the axiom of choice in the following form.
def Choice_Axm =
\(A : Prop, B : Prop, P : [A -> B -> Prop])
\(l : !(x : A) ?(y : B) @(P, x, y))
let f = \(x : A) @(FST, @(l, x)) in
let p = \(x : A, g : @(P,x,@(f,x))) g in
let q = \(x : A)
@(p, x, @(SND, @(l, x))) in
<f, q>;

In Zermelo-Fraenkel axiomatic set theory, there is no proof of the axiom of
choice, so it must be taken as axiom. In constructive type theory, however, a
detailed justification of the axiom of choice in form given above can be provided.
Therefore, there are two ways of proving the second form of specification
given above:
• We can prove the statement by giving a term f and proving it satisfies
the required property. This method corresponds to traditional program
development in a functional programming language: we first define the

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

134

function we think has the desired property and separately we prove that
it does have the property, an exercise in program verification.
• Alternatively, we can develop the proof from the top down, reducing the
existential statement of the second form to the first one. We then try to
prove this formula directly and from the proof we extract a function by
applying the axiom of choice.

12.2

Dutch Flag Problem

We now present an example of Dijkstra’s problem of the Dutch national flag
[Dij 76]: construct a program that given a sequence of objects, each having one
of the colors red, white or blue, rearranges the objects so that they appear in
the order of the Dutch flag.
We will derive a program for the Dutch flag problem using lists. We assume
that:
• Aa is a type;
• Red, White and Blue are type valued function (propositional functions) defined
on Aa;
• @(COLOR, x) is a proof-object of the type @(Sum3, @(Red, x), @(White, x),
@(Blue, x)).

In terms of PowerEpsilon, we introduce the following definitions:

!(a : Aa, u : Alist)

136

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

137

[@(P, u) -> @(P, @(ACONS, a, u))] ->
!(*n : Alist) @(P, *n)];
def Rec =
\(P : [Alist -> Type(0)])
\(a : @(P, ANIL))
\(f : !(x : Aa, u : Alist)
[@(P, u) -> @(P, @(ACONS, x, u))])
\(*n : Alist)
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE0,
@(P, *n),
@(LEQUAL, Aa, *n, ANIL),
a,
@(f,
@(HEAD, Aa, *n),
@(TAIL, Aa, *n),
@(Rec, P, a, f, @(TAIL, Aa, *n))));

The specification can be given by the proposition:
def Perm =
\(l1 : Alist, l2 : Alist)
!(x : Aa)
@(Equal, Nat, @(OCC_IN, x, l1), @(OCC_IN, x, l2));
def Part_perm =
\(l : Alist)
?(z : Prod)
@(Perm, l, @(APPD, @(PJ1, z), @(PJ2, z), @(PJ3, z)));
dec Spec : !(l : Alist) @(Part_perm, l);

A program that satisfies the specification will give a triple of lists as output. To
get a solution to Dijkstra’s formulation of the problem, these three lists should
be concatenated, therefore the function APPD is needed here.
Deriving a program that satisfies the specification is nothing but finding a
program which is a member of the type Spec, or, if we think of the specification
as a goal, to find a program that achieves the goal.
The intuitive idea behind the proof is the following: the list l of red, white
and blue objects will be partitioned by list recursion into s ∈ Prod:
• The base case, i.e. that l is equal to ANIL, is solved by simply putting the
partitioning equal to @(PROD, RNIL, WNIL, BNIL).
• For the induction step, assume that l is @(ACONS, x, y) and that we
have a partitioning z of y and then separate the problem into three cases:
1. x is red; s is then put equal to @(PROD, @(RCONS, x, @(PJ1, z)),
@(PJ2, z), @(PJ3, z));

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

138

2. x is white; s is then put equal to @(PROD, @(PJ1, z), @(WCONS,
x, @(PJ2, z)), @(PJ3, z));
3. x is blue; s is then put equal to @(PROD, @(PJ1, z), @(PJ2, z),
@(BCONS, x, @(PJ3, z))).
We now start the derivation by list induction over l. First, we assume that
l = ANIL, and have to show that @(PROD, RNIL, WNIL, BNIL) = @(FST, a),
where a ∈ @(Part perm, ANIL).
We have LEM0 and THM0 to achieve this goal:
dec LEM0 :
let pd = @(PROD, RNIL, WNIL, BNIL) in
@(Perm, ANIL, @(APPD, @(PJ1, pd), @(PJ2, pd), @(PJ3, pd)));
def THM0 = <@(PROD, RNIL, WNIL, BNIL), LEM0>;

Second, we come to the induction step. We have to prove the theorems THM1,
THM2 and THM3 respectively. We have
dec LEM1 :
!(x : Aa, l : Alist)
!(u : @(Red, x))
!(z1 : Rlist, z2 : Wlist, z3 : Blist)
let pd = @(PROD, @(RCONS, x, u, z1), z2, z3) in
[@(Perm, l, @(APPD, z1, z2, z3)) ->
@(Perm,
@(ACONS, x, l),
@(APPD, @(PJ1, pd), @(PJ2, pd), @(PJ3, pd)))];

!(x : Aa, l : Alist) !(u : @(Red, x)) !(p : @(Part_perm, l))
@(Part_perm, @(ACONS, x, l));
dec LEM2 :
!(x : Aa, l : Alist)
!(v : @(White, x))
!(z1 : Rlist, z2 : Wlist, z3 : Blist)
let pd = @(PROD, z1, @(WCONS, x, v, z2), z3) in
[@(Perm, l, @(APPD, z1, z2, z3)) ->
@(Perm,
@(ACONS, x, l),
@(APPD, @(PJ1, pd), @(PJ2, pd), @(PJ3, pd)))];
dec THM2 :
!(x : Aa, l : Alist) !(v : @(White, x)) !(p : @(Part_perm, l))
@(Part_perm, @(ACONS, x, l));
dec LEM3 :

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

139

!(x : Aa, l : Alist)
!(w : @(Blue, x))
!(z1 : Rlist, z2 : Wlist, z3 : Blist)

[@(Perm, l, @(APPD, z1, z2, z3)) ->
@(Perm,
@(ACONS, x, l),
@(APPD, @(PJ1, pd), @(PJ2, pd), @(PJ3, pd)))];

@(Part_perm, @(ACONS, x, l));

12.3

Quotient-Remainder Problem

Second example is the quotient-remainder problem 1 . The problem is to find
two functions QUOT and REMD such that @(QUOT, x1, x2) = z1 and @(REMD,
1 A revised version of Section 12.3 has been published in [ZhuW 92]

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

142

x1, x2) = z2. Here z1 is the quotient and z2 is the remainder of dividing x1
by x2. The specification specifies the behavior of desired program only for case
in which the divider x2 is not zero. The specification is given as follows:
def Nat_eq =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
?(z : Prod)
@(Equal, Nat, x1, @(ADD, @(TIMES, x2, @(PJ1, z)), @(PJ2, z)));
dec Spec :
!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) -> @(Nat_eq, x1, x2)];

We will derive a program for the quotient-remainder problem. In terms of
PowerEpsilon, we introduce the following definitions:
def And = Product;
dec QUOT : [Nat -> Nat -> Nat];
dec REMD : [Nat -> Nat -> Nat];
dec Less : [Nat
dec Greq : [Nat

-> Nat -> Prop];
-> Nat -> Prop];

def Prod = @(Product, Nat, Nat);
def PROD = @(PRODUCT, Nat, Nat);
def PJ1 = @(PRO1, Nat, Nat);
def PJ2 = @(PRO2, Nat, Nat);
def PIF_THEN_ELSE = @(GIF_THEN_ELSE, Prod);
dec Coerce : [Type(0) -> Prop];
dec NATREC : [Nat -> Nat -> Prod -> [Prod -> Prod] -> Prod];
def NATREC =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
\(a : Prod, b : [Prod -> Prod])
@(PIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(LESS, x1, x2),
a,
@(b, @(NATREC, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2, a, b)));

A program that satisfies the specification will give a pair of natural numbers as
output. Deriving a program that satisfies the specification is noting but finding
a program which is a member of the type Spec, or, if we think of the specification
as a goal, to find a program that achieves the goal.

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

143

The intuitive idea behind the proof is the following: we will divided the
problem into subproblems according to the input natural numbers x1 and x2
with the cases of x1 < x2 and x1 ≥ x2. The division will be done inductively
using a special kind of induction rule:
• The base case, i.e. that @(Less, x1, x2), is solved by simply putting
the result equal to @(PROD, x1, x1) which means that z1 = OO and z2
= x1.
• For the induction step, assume that @(Greq, x1, x2) and that we have
a proof p showing that @(Nat eq, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2), then we can
put the result equal to @(PROD, @(SS, @(PJ1, @(FST, p))), @(PJ2,
@(FST, p))).
A special kind of induction rule for the quotient-remainder problem has to be
defined on Nat which has the following form:
dec LS : !(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat) @(Less, x1, x2);
dec GE : !(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat) @(Greq, x1, x2);
dec Rec :
!(P : [Nat -> Nat -> Type(0)])
[!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) ->
@(Less, x1, x2) ->
@(P, x1, x2)] ->
!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) ->
@(Greq, x1, x2) ->
@(P, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2) ->
@(P, x1, x2)] ->
!(*x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) -> @(P, *x1, x2)]];
def Rec =
\(P : [Nat -> Nat -> Type(0)])
\(a : !(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) ->
@(Less, x1, x2) ->
@(P, x1, x2)])
\(b : !(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) ->
@(Greq, x1, x2) ->
@(P, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2) ->
@(P, x1, x2)])
\(*x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
\(s : @(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)))
@(GIF_THEN_ELSE0,

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

144

@(P, *x1, x2),
@(LESS, *x1, x2),
@(a, *x1, x2, s, @(LS, *x1, x2)),
@(b,
*x1,
x2,
s,
@(GE, *x1, x2),
@(Rec, P, a, b, @(MINUS, *x1, x2), x2, s)));

Here Rec is in some sense very similar to the “computation invariant” — an
important concept in program verification approach. To define an induction
rule is as much difficult as finding a “loop invariant” in program verification.
There is no general method of finding appropriate induction rules in order to
complete a proof.
We now start the derivation by induction over x1 and x2. First, we assume
that @(Less, x1, x2), and have to show that @(PROD, OO, x1) = @(FST,
a), where a ∈ @(Nat eq, x1, x1).
We have LEM1 and THM1 to achieve this goal:
dec LEM1 :
!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Less, x1, x2) ->
let pd = @(PROD, OO, x1) in
@(Equal,
Nat,
x1,
@(ADD, @(TIMES, x2, @(PJ1, pd)), @(PJ2, pd)))];
dec THM1 :
!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) ->
@(Less, x1, x2) ->
@(Nat_eq, x1, x2)];
def THM1 =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
\(s : @(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)))
\(u : @(Less, x1, x2))
<@(PROD, OO, x1), @(LEM1, x1, x2, u)>;

Second, we come to the induction step. We have to prove the theorem THM2.
We have
dec LEM2 :
!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
!(v : @(Greq, x1, x2))
!(z1 : Nat, z2 : Nat)

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

145

[@(Equal,
Nat,
@(MINUS, x1, x2),
@(ADD, @(TIMES, x2, z1), z2)) ->
let pd = @(PROD, @(SS, z1), z2) in
@(Equal,
Nat,
x1,
@(ADD, @(TIMES, x2, @(PJ1, pd)), @(PJ2, pd)))];
dec THM2 :
!(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
[@(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)) ->
@(Greq, x1, x2) ->
@(Nat_eq, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2) ->
@(Nat_eq, x1, x2)];
def THM2 =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
\(s : @(Not, @(Equal, Nat, x2, OO)))
\(v : @(Greq, x1, x2))
\(p : @(Nat_eq, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2))
let fp = @(FST, p) in
<@(PROD, @(SS, @(PJ1, fp)), @(PJ2, fp)),
@(LEM2, x1, x2, v, @(PJ1, fp), @(PJ2, fp), @(SND, p))>;

To combine THM1 and THM2, we then obtain theorems THM and extract the final
program PROG from THM3, where THM3 : Spec:
def THM3 =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat) @(Rec, Nat_eq, THM1, THM2, x1, x2);
def PROG =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
@(NATREC,
x1,
x2,
@(PROD, OO, x1),
\(z : Prod) @(PROD, @(SS, @(PJ1, z)), @(PJ2, z)));

The final program PROG can be divided into two program QUOT and REMD:
def QUOT =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(NOT, @(EQUAL, x2, OO)),
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(LESS, x1, x2),
OO,

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

146

@(SS, @(QUOT, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2))),
@(ERR, x2));
def REMD =
\(x1 : Nat, x2 : Nat)
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(NOT, @(EQUAL, x2, OO)),
@(NIF_THEN_ELSE,
@(LESS, x1, x2),
x1,
@(REMD, @(MINUS, x1, x2), x2)),
@(ERR, x2));

where the function ERR can be viewed as an exception handler.

12.4

Discussion

PowerEpsilon can be used as a powerful semantics-based programming tool
for development of provably correct software. It has created an entirely new
attitude towards the processes of program development, validation and adaptation. It provide a natural causal connections between software specifications
and computer programs that realize them where specifications are treated as
propositions (types) and programs are treated as proofs.

12.4.1

Advantages and Disadvantages of Using PowerEpsilon

Formal derivation of programs as it has been done in PowerEpsilon has many
advantages:
1. Programming in PowerEpsilon is like theorem proving in mathematics.
Since parts of the proofs are used in the actual program construction, the
proofs have to be more detailed and formal than they usually are in mathematics. The causal connections between specifications and their realizations are much stronger than they usually to be in most formal methods
such as algebraic specifications techniques and axiomatic approaches.
2. Programming in constructive type theory is more natural than verification approach. Mechanical program verification systems usually consist of
two main programs — a verification condition generator and a theorem
prover. The use of such a system involves two steps. (a) The verification
condition generator accepts as its input a source program to be verified, an
input/output specification for the program, and some inductive invariants;
the verification condition generator produces some formulas, called “verification conditions”, which imply that source program behaves as specified.

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

147

(b) The verification conditions are then submitted to the theorem-prover.
If the theorem-prover determines that the verification conditions are theorems, then the source program behaves as specified. Generally, the verification approach can be viewed as the program-driven approach in which
programs are developed first, then a proof to confirm that if it satisfies the
desired properties is derived. On the other hand, the constructive type
theoretical approach can be viewed as the proof-driven approach in which
a proof to show that the requirement specification is really satisfiable (is a
mathematical theorem) is developed and in the meantime a program can
be extracted as a by- product from the proof.
3. There is a very clear distinction between specifications and implementations in the program derivation approach supported by PowerEpsilon.
However, in many traditional specification techniques such as algebraic
specification techniques, the difference between specifications and implementations is not clear where specifications are often viewed as inefficient
programs and the main aim of an implementation is to improve its performance.
However, the experience of using PowerEpsilon also exhibits some problems
which indicates that in order to obtain a practical software system development
system a lot of improvement for PowerEpsilon system has to be made:
1. As we have seen that the proof development in PowerEpsilon is time
and space consuming task. The derivation of a piece of program whose
length is less than one page often produce several pages of proof. This
shortcoming makes PowerEpsilon impractical to be used as a program
development system for large software development project.
2. Sometimes the users may feel that the approach of programming as mathematical theorem proving is too formal and rigorous. It makes many simple
and easy things complex and difficult. Usually it is easy to construct a
program intuitively but difficult to prove its correctness.
3. To obtain a proof for a theory, we had to start from scratch (Bool, Nat,
etc.). There is a lack of predefined reusable theories for standard mathematical concepts that programmers use or assume. Ideally such library
of theories at least should be general enough for a variety of applications.
They should also be extensible so that users can specialized the general
theories as well as add their own application-specific theories.

12.4.2

Proofs is Evidences of Truth

One of problems associated with computer programming right now is that programmers have had little knowledge of what it means for a program to be

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

148

correct and of how to prove a program correct. The word proof has unpleasant
connotations for many, and it will be helpful to explain what it means.
A proof, according to Webster’s Third New International Dictionary, is “the
cogency of evidence that compels belief by the mind of a truth or fact”. It is an
argument that convinces the reader of the truth of something.
The definition of proof does not imply the need for formalism or mathematics. Indeed, programmers try to prove their programs correct in this sense of
proof, for they certainly try to present evidence that compels their own belief.
Unfortunately, most programmers are not adept at this, as can be seen by looking at how much time is spent debugging. The programmer must indeed fell
frustrated at the lack of mastery of the subject!
Part of the problem has been that only inadequate tools for understanding
have been available. Reasoning has been based solely on how programs are
executed, and arguments about correctness have been based on a number of
test cases that have been run or hand-simulated. The intuition and mental
tools have simply been inadequate. Thus, our development method will center
around the programs derivation from constructive proofs.

12.4.3

Balance between Formality and Common Sense

Our approach to programming is based on program derivation from constructive
proofs. But be assured that complete attention to formalism is neither necessary
nor desirable. Formality alone is inadequate, because it leads to incomprehensible detail; common sense and intuition alone – the programmer’s main tools
till now – are inadequate, because they allow too many errors and bad designs.
What is needed is a fine balance between the two. Obvious facts should be
left implicit, important points should be stressed, and detail should be presented
to allow the reader to understand a program as easily as possible. A notation
must be found that allows less formalism to be used. Where suitable, definitions
in English are okay, but when the going get rough, more formalism is required.
This takes intelligence, taste, knowledge and practice. It is not easy.
Actually, every mathematicians strives for this fine balance. Large gaps will
be left in a proof if it is felt that an educated reader will understand how to fill
them. The most important and difficult points will receive the most attention.
A proof will be organized as a series of lemmas to ease understanding.
This balance between formality and common sense is even more important
for the programmer. Programming requires so much more detail, which must
absolutely correct without relying on the goodwill of the reader. In addition,
some programs are so large that they cannot be comprehended fully by one
person at one time. Thus, there is a continual need to strive for balance, conciseness, and even elegance.
However, a balance cannot be achieved unless one has both common sense
and a facility with theory. The first has been most used by programmers: to

CHAPTER 12. PROGRAM DEVELOPMENT IN POWEREPSILON

149

overcome the current imbalance it is necessary to learn to the formal side for
awhile.

12.4.4

Radical Programming Methodologies

The method discussed in this chapter can certainly benefit almost any programmer. At the same time, it should be made clear that there are other ways to
develop programs. A difficult task like programming requires many different
tools and techniques. Many algorithms require the use of an idea that simply
does not arise from the principles given in this chapter. So this method alone
cannot be used to solve them effectively. Some important ideas, like program
transformation and “abstract data types” may also useful. And, of course,
experience and knowledge can make all the difference in the world.
Secondly, even though the emphasis is on proofs of correctness, errors will
occur. The wise programmer develops a program with the altitude that a correct
program can and will be developed, provide enough care and concentration is
used, and then tests it thoroughly with the altitude that it must have mistake in
it. The frequency of errors in mathematical theorems, proofs, and applications
of theorems is well-recognized and documented, and the area of program-proving
will not be exception. We must simply learn to live with human fallibility and
simplify to reduce it to a minimum.
Nevertheless, the study of Chapter 12 will provide an education in rigorous
thinking, which is essential for good programming. Conscious application of the
methods and techniques discussed will certainly be of benefit.

Chapter 13

Implementation
As a general principle, the use of a computer not only forces the researchers to
be more precise, but also encourages them to be more ambitious. For deeply understanding the semantics of PowerEpsilon, specially its type inference mechanism, we have implemented a prototyping system of PowerEpsilon with the
support of AUTOSTAR system [Zhu 89]. All the examples given in this paper
have been checked out by the system. Another implementation of PowerEpsilon is presently underway using Borland C++ on IBM-PC 386. One of the
main challenges for this work is to find an efficient implementation strategy for
the language.
We hope that this system will be useful for future development of programming environments, where programs will be developed consistently with logical
propositions expressing in one unified formalism – types.
The whole development has followed a rather formal discipline. We start
from an algebraic specification of PowerEpsilon, and then by a series of program transformation, obtain an efficient program. The development has been
supported by a software development system called AUTOSTAR designed and
implemented by author.

13.1

Algebraic Specification of PowerEpsilon

The basic unit of an algebraic specification consists of two parts: a syntactic
specification and a semantic specification. The syntactic specification provides
the syntactic and type checking information: the names, domains and ranges of
the operations associated with the type. The semantic specification is a set of
axioms which defines the meaning of the operations by stating their relationships to one another. The word “algebra” is appreciate because the values and
operations can be regarded as together forming an abstract algebra. Goguen,
Thatcher and Wagner [GogTW 75] and Zilles [Zil 75] have strongly emphasized

150

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

151

the algebraic approach, developing a theory of abstract types as an application
of many-sorted algebra. Implementations are treated under this approach as
other algebras, and the problem showing that an implementation is correct is
treated through showing the existence of a homomorphic mapping from one
algebra to the other.
At the first stage of design and implementation of PowerEpsilon, a complete algebraic specification of PowerEpsilon in terms of a specification language ADT in AUTOSTAR has been written.

13.2

Ada as a Implementation Language

PowerEpsilon has been implemented in programming language Ada on a
VAX-11/785 machine runing VAX/VMS V4.4 operating system. The reason
for choosing Ada as an implementation language is that the Ada language incorporates many of the advances made in programming language design in the
past two decades. It enforces a strict programming discipline with the intention
of making programs more readable, reliable, modular, maintainable and so on –
all qualities of a good program. Especially, the information hiding mechanisms
of Ada, such as private and limited private types, make the programs written
in Ada very easy to modify and extend.

13.3

Program Transformation

PowerEpsilon has been designed and implemented based on “transformatinal
programming” methodology, which is a rigorous process of building, manipulating, and reasoning about programs and their derivations.

13.3.2

Elimination of Recursion

At the first stage of development, the programs developed from the algebraic
specifications are naturally recursive. However, this kind of implementation are
often costly in terms of the execution time and storage space required. This
disadvantage can be minimized by transforming a given recursive program into
an iterative program. Such a method is more efficient than directly constructing
an iterative program, especially when this turns out to be complicated and
therefore difficult to prove correctness. Indeed, if the recursive program has
been proved correct, and if the transformations preserve correctness, then the
iterative program thus obtained is guaranteed to be correct, without a direct

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

153

proof being required.
The complete elimination of recursion in general takes place in two stages:
1. Elimination of formal parameters and local variables.
2. Transformation of a recursive procedure with no parameters and local
variables into an equivalent iterative procedure.
Elimination of Formal Parameters and Local Variables
In this section, we will discuss the methods of eliminating formal parameters
and local variables.
Elimination of Variable Parameters
We shall consider only the case where:
• The actual parameters of recursive calls are the same as the corresponding
formal parameters.
• The actual parameters of the principal call can be accessed from anywhere
in the body of the procedure.
In these two case, for each occurrence of a formal variable parameter, we can
then substitute the corresponding actual parameter of the principal call.
Elimination of Value Parameters
We will eliminate value parameters by saving their values with the help of the
term stack, and then replacing them with global variables.
Let P be a recursive procedure of the form:
procedure P(y : in T) is
begin
......
end P;
We obtain a procedure equivalent to P by replacing each recursive call P(e) by
the sequence:
PUSH(y); y := e; P(y); POP(y);
We can then write P in the form of a procedure without parameters, manipulating global variables, according to the following definition:
yg : T;
procedure P is
begin
......
end P;

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

154

where yg is global variables which replace the value parameters y. The new
body of procedure P is obtained by substituting for each occurrence of a value
parameters y the corresponding global variable yg, and by replacing each recursive call P(e) by the recursive call P. The pricipal call of P, P(u), is replaced by
the sequence yg := u; P.
The parameter of type Env can be naturally globalized.
Elimination of Local Variables
The transformation of local variables into global variables is achieved in the
same way as that of value parameters. A value parameter may be in fact be
considered as an initialized local variable.
Some kinds of optimization can be considered at this stage. If the value a
local variable is never used after any recursive calls, it will be unnecessary to
save its value.
Elimination of Recursion
Having used the results described in the last section, we can now solve the
problem of eliminating recursion in a procedure which has no parameters or
local variables. This can be done using some straightforward rules.
1. At the beginning of the procedure or function, code is inserted which
initialize a term stack and a label stack. The former will be used to hold
the values of parameters and local variables. The later will be used to
save return addresses for each recursive call.
2. The label <<start>> is attached to the first executable statement.
Now, each recursive call is replaced by a set of instructions which do the following:
1. Create the i-th new label, i, and store i in the label stack. The value i
of the label will be used as the return address.
2. Each recursion call will be replaced by a jump to the beginning of the
body of the procedure or function.
3. Add the label to the statement immediately following the unconditional
jump.
These steps are sufficient to remove all recursive calls from the procedure or
function. Finally, we need to precede the last end statement of the procedure
or function by the code to do the following:
1. Insert code for deciding whether the label stack is empty or not, and place
a label <<finish>> before the code.

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

155

2. If the label stack is empty, we simply end the procedure or function.
3. If the stack is not empty, then use the return label from the top of the
label stack and execute a branch to this label.
4. A jump to the label <<finish>> will be generated when end of procedure
or function is reached.
By following these rules carefully one can take any recursive program and produce a program which works in exactly the same way, yet which uses only
iteration to control the flow of the program. On many compilers this resultant
program will be much more efficient than its recursive version. Once the transformation to iterative form has been accomplished, one can often simplify the
program even further thereby producing even more gains in efficiency.
procedure P is
begin
if E1 then
A1; P; B1; P; C1;
elsif E2 then
A2; P; B2; P; C2;
......
elsif En then
An; P; Bn; P; Cn;
else
D
end if;
end P;
procedure P is
begin
<<start>>
while E loop
if E1 then
A1; PUSH(1, 1);
elsif E2 then
A2; PUSH(2, 1);
......
elsif En then
An; PUSH(n, 1);
end if;
end loop;
D;
<<finish>>
if not IS_EMPTY_STACK then

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

156

POP(i, j);
if i = 1 then
if j = 1 then
B1; PUSH(1, 2); goto start;
else
C1; goto finish;
end if;
elsif i = 2 then
if j = 1 then
B2; PUSH(2, 2); goto start;
else
C2; goto finish;
end if;
......
elsif i = n then
if j = 1 then
Bn; PUSH(n, 2); goto start;
else
Cn; goto finish;
end if;
end if;
end if;
end P;
In this general recursion removing scheme, an additional stack for storing
return-addresses (labels) is required. Fortunately, In this implementation of
PowerEpsilon, we may use the term stack deployed for saving parameters and
local variables to eliminate this redundancy. For example, the term pp1 in the
procedure SUBST can be used to distinguish different branches (label i) in an
IF-THEN-ELSE statement, and the subcomponent rank in pp1 can be used for
storing the label j) in each conditional branch, since the rank will never used
in any compound term.
As we can see, the resulting program are no longer easy to read and understand, since its complicated structure.
Procedures with Mutual Recursive Calls
The transformation described in the last section can easily be generalized to the
case of mutual recursively defined procedures. A set of procedures with mutual
recursive calls can be combined into one by the following steps.
• All of the beginning of the body of the procedures are combined together.
The principal procedure call is placed at the beginning.
• Each recursive call, will, as before, be replaced by a jump to the corresponding parts of the beginning of the body of the procedures accordingly.

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

157

• The test t1 , t2 , ..., are set up used to distinguish between the cases: the end
of the principal call, when execution is finished, or, the end of a recursive
call, when a jump is made to the instructions executed immediately after
the recursive call.
Other transformation steps are almost same as before.
Example
Consider two recursively defined procedures f and g of the form:
procedure f is
begin
if condf then
Af;
f;
Bf;
g;
Cf;
else
Ef;
end if;
end f;
procedure g is
begin
if condg then
Ag;
g;
Bg;
f;
Cg;
else
Eg;
end if;
end g;
Assume that f is the principal procedure call. A new procedure fg is defined
with all recursive calls being removed.
procedure fg is
begin
INIT_STACK;
<<Af_start>>
if condf then
Af;

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

158

PUSH(11);
goto Af_start;
end if;
Ef;
goto Final;
<<Ag_start>>

Ag;
PUSH(21);
goto Ag_start;
end if;
Eg;
goto Final;
<<Final>>
if not IS_EMPTY_STACK then
POP(l);
if l = 11 then
Bf;
PUSH(12);
goto Ag_start;
elsif l = 21 then

PUSH(22);

elsif l = 12 then
Cf;
goto Final;
elsif l = 22 then
Cg

end if;
end if;
end fg;

In this iterative procedure, the first two while loops produce sequences of executions of Af and Ag corresponding to the initial parts of the executions of the
recursive procedure. In the same way, the final two loops produces sequences of
executions of Cf and Cg corresponding to the final parts. It can then be written

procedure fg is
begin

<<Af_start>>

Compilation of Type Checking
Another way to improve the efficiency is to apply the technique of compiler to
type checking. For doing so, we need
1. To design and implement a virtual machine for type checking for PowerEpsilon.
2. To develop a compiler which translate each program of PowerEpsilon
into a sequence of codes of the virtual machine.
One way to abstract a virtual machine from a formal semantic definition of
PowerEpsilon (an abstract syntax tree together with a type-checking function) is to use the combinator method developed by Mitchell Wand for generating conventional machine architecture and compilers from interpreters from the
denotational semantics of some programming languages [Wand 82].

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

161

Another way is to use partial evaluation approach. Partial evaluation offers a
unified approach towards compiling and generating compilers from interpreters.
It uses a binding time analyzer determine automatically the static semantics,
and a specializer to execute the static operations so detected. This results in
robust and simple approach to semantic-directed compilation.
We are still in the early stage of development of this approach. More work
need to be done towards this direction.

13.4

Lazy and Eager Evaluation

Since the strongly normalization property of PowerEpsilon, many different
reduction strategies can be deployed.
Eager evaluation (call-by-value) is more efficient than lazy evaluation (callby-name), since each actual parameter is evaluated only once in binding time.
PowerEpsilon’s evaluation mechanism is “lazy”, in the sense that no subexpression is evaluated until its value is known to be required.
Any implementation of lazy evaluation has two ingredients:
1. Arguments to functions should be evaluated only when their value is
needed, not when the function is applied.
2. Arguments should only evaluated once; further uses of the argument
within the function should use the value computed the first time. Since
the language is functional we can be sure that this scheme gives the same
result as reevaluating the argument.
Theorem 13.1 For any term t of PowerEpsilon, LREDUCE(t) (@(EREDUCE,
t)) is in normal form of t.

13.4.1

One-Step Weak Head Normal Form Reduction

In implementation of type inference, we pursue one step weak head normal
form reduction to determine whether two terms t1 and t2 are equal or not.
The reduction will stop at WHNF rather than proceeding all the way to normal
form. This is an essential ingredient of lazy evaluation, since reducing through
to normal form risks performing unnecessary reductions.

13.5

Graph Reduction and Tree Reduction

13.5.1

Abstract Syntax Tree and Graph

The PowerEpsilon programs to be evaluated are held in machine in the form
of its abstract syntax tree. The process of reduction performs successive transformations on the syntax tree. During this process the tree becomes a graph.

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

13.5.2

162

Substituting Pointers to the Arguments

When substituting the argument for the formal parameter, we could just copy
the argument whenever the formal parameter occurred. This kind of reduction is
called tree reduction. But copying the argument may be very wasteful, because
1. The argument might be a very large expression, in which case we are
wasting space by making multiple copies of the same objects.
2. The argument might contain redexes, in which case we are wasting work
by duplicating redexes which may subsequently have to be separately reduced.
Both of these problems can be avoided by substituting pointers to the argument
for the formal parameter. This gives rise to sharing, whereby there may be
many pointers to the same expression, and it is for precisely this reason that
the expression tree becomes a graph.

13.6

Type Checking

Theorem 13.2 For any term t of PowerEpsilon, if t is type-checked, then t
: TEVAL TERM(t, r).
Theorem 13.3 For any term t of PowerEpsilon, if t is type-checked, then
TT EVAL(t, r) ≡ TRUE.
The proof can be obtained by using induction over an arbitrarily given term
t.

13.7

Theory Library Management

13.7.1

An Environment with Global and Local Pointers

An environment is divided into two parts: global part and local part. Two
pointers, global pointer and local pointer, are used for identifying different
parts of an environment. The part between bottom and the location pointed by
global pointer is used to store all the definitions and declarations imported
from other theories. The part between the location pointed by global pointer
and local pointer is used to store all the definitions and declarations in the
theory to be processed. See Figure 13.1

13.7.2

Environment as an External Structure

An unit of library can be treated as the local part of an environment. Since
an environment is a completely processed theory, there will be no need to do
type-checking again.

13.7.3

Environment as an Internal Structure

An environment r can also be treated as an internal structure whic will be
stored in a file. The local base address of the environment is 0. If a term t has
a forward global address chaining a with global pointer as g, then its relative
address o will be g − a = o, where o < 0. To obtain a we can just put a = g − o.

13.7.4

Library Organized as a Tree

Each theory unit of PowerEpsilon is said to belong to a theory library. The
effect of processing a library unit is to define (or redefine) this unit as one
that belongs to the theory library. The designator of a theory unit must be an
identifier. Within a theory library the simple names of all theory units must be
distinct identifiers.
A theory library can be organized as a directory in an operating system such
as VAX/VMS, MS-DOS, and Unix. A library name is treated as a directory
name and a sublibrary is a subdirectory.
The tree manipulating operations include inserting a leaf, delete a leaf,
adding a file to a node, etc.

13.7.5

Library Name Assignment

On VAX/VMS based implementation, for instance, we have the following DCL
command to assign a directory name powerlib to a logical name power$lib.
$assign powerlib power$lib

CHAPTER 13. IMPLEMENTATION

164

In MS-DOS, we may use the MS-DOS command set to define an environment
variable powenv which will be used as a logical name in the system.
set powenv=c:\power\env

Making PowerEpsilon a Real Software Product

To make the PowerEpsilon a real software product, we need to complete the
following important tasks:
• Writing the manual.
• Implementing the on-line help facility.
• Developing the installation program for setting up the system.

13.8.1

Writing Manual

Writing manual is a hard labor intensive job. Most programmers put off writing
the manual until the end of the project – when there is no time to do a proper
job.

13.8.2

Implementing On-Line Help

Implementing the on-line help facility is almost as same as creating user’s manual. However, there is a number of tools distributed on software market to
support the on-line help writing.
ForeHelp
The ForeHelp is help authoring system for Windows in a true WYSIWYG
hypertext environment produced by ForeFront, Inc.

Developing Installation Program

14.1

PowerEpsilon and Literate Programming

The crafting of a program’s source code with an eye towards readability and
facilitating understanding has been a subject discussed in computing circles for
many years. Numerous suggestions have been made in the past regarding program documentation content and layout encompassing such items as naming of
identifiers, use of white space and indentation, program statement layout, commenting conventions, etc. Many of these kinds of guidelines are today commonly
considered good and proper programming practice, though opinions diverge on
specific details.
It has long been recognized that a program, while written only once, will
be read numerous times. Furthermore, the readership extends beyond the program’s authors and the skill level between program author and reader will most
likely differ, perhaps considerably. The inherent complexity of software and
its interaction with a continuously changing environment (new or modified requirements, hardware, personnel, etc.) contribute to the difficulties involved
in software maintenance. Critically important to any successful maintenance
effort is the programmers understanding of the software being modified, for
without this understanding, program alternations are unlikely to achieve the
desired effect. Thus, the question becomes how to promote understandability
and readability in a program and what techniques will facilitate this end.

14.1.1

Literate Programming

One recent attempt at addressing the above problems is that of literate programming, first presented by Donald Knuth [Knuth 84b, Knuth 92]. Central to

165

Interface to Natural Language

166

the concept of literate programming is designed for human audiences [Wyk 87a,
Wyk 87b, Lins 89]. This differs from typical program which is written with the
compiler or machine as the intended audience. The technique of literate programming thus is not only a communication vehicle from the author to reader
but also a communication amplifier allowing the author to highlight or emphasize the most fundamental or important concepts embodied by the program.
The programs are considered to be works of literature.
With the focus on the human reader, the program writing task can take
advantage of the knowledge available on the writing of literary works. For example, the author of a novel, magazine article or play must consider the intended
audience. Obviously, the resulting work will emphasize different aspects of the
subject material based on this one criterion – the audience. Questions such as
their expected knowledge of the topic, experience, etc. must be answered in the
author’s mind before he or she begins to write a single line. This is one example
of how we can take advantage of knowledge outside the domain of computer
science and apply it to our own benefit.
The literate programming philosophy, regardless of implementation mechanisms, is the truly important concept. Briefly put,
Literate Programming = Structured Programming + Structured Documentation.

The problem may become more serious when we use a programming language
like PowerEpsilon to represent mathematics. The daily work of a mathematician is to prove theorems and to write articles. The readability and understandability are more important than in computer science.
As a programming language, most of traditional mathematical symbols such
as Greek letters, superscript and subscript letters are not appeared in PowerEpsilon. This make the representation of mathematical formulas in PowerEpsilon are quite awful. This shortcoming may become a major barrel to
prevent mathematicians from accepting PowerEpsilon as tool for their daily
work.

14.1.2

The WEB System

WEB is a structured documentation software tool supporting literate programming. Essentially, the WEB system allows the program author to organize a
program in a manner suitable for both the human reader as well as the machine.
An overview of the WEB architecture is shown in figure 14.1 using a very
simple data flow diagraming technique. The primary input to WEB system
is written by the programmer and consisting typesetting commands, Pascal
source text, and English text in varying measures. The two main “processes,”
WEAVE and TANGLE, are responsible for dividing the input into separate
formats – one is meant for the human audience while the other is targeted for the
machine (e.g., Pascal compiler). Relatively small examples of WEB programs
are given in [Bent 86, Knuth 86a], while full scale production programs can

be found in [Knuth 86b, Knuth 86c]. As noted by Jon Bentley in [Bent 86],
most published programs, whether in books or magazines, are simply highly
refined subroutines where traditional commenting techniques are adequate for
describing the program. Donald Knuth has demonstrated the effectiveness of
literate programming even when applied to relatively large software programs.
Literate programming is still a relative new concept in computer science.

14.2

From PowerEpsilon to Logic Formulas

The PowerEpsilon theories are written in ASCII codes. We plan eventually
to implement a program to convert the ASCII formulas into input for a text
formatter that will produce a ‘pretty-printed” version. The user will be able to
specify how individual operators are formatted.
Since many special characters are often used in mathematical system, it is
desirable to allow the input of such characters directly, that is, without typesetting or contrived substitutions such as “!” and “?”, respectively.
This conversion program has not yet been designed. However, we believe
that all the transformations from ASCII to printed version can be achieved by
a program that works locally on terms, and needs to know nothing about the
semantics or full syntax of PowerEpsilon. It would be feasible, but probably
a lot of work, to implement the program as TEX macro. The key point here is
how to make the system easy to use.

Syntax of a Subset of LATEX

We have to, as the first step, choose a proper subset of LATEX [Lamp 86] for our
translation experiment. The LATEX document preparation system is a special
version of Donald Knuth’s TEX system [Knuth 84a]. TEX is a sophisticated program designed to produce high-quality typesetting, especially for mathematical
text. LATEX adds to TEX a collection of commands that simplify typesetting
by letting user concentrate on the structure of the text rather than individual
formatting command. The LATEX however, has all the power of TEX hidden
under its hood, and the more adventurous driver can do everything with it that
he can with TEXL̇ATEX presents a balance between functionality and ease of use.

14.2.2

Syntax of Translation Rules

The syntax of translation rules from PowerEpsilon to LATEX is easily formulated by combining the syntax of both languages.
An implementation can be conducted using pattern matching and rewriting techniques. The translation is purely syntax-driven. There is no semantic
information demanded.

14.2.3

Static Interpretation (Partial Evaluation)

The aim of static interpretation is to simplify the λ-terms. A term should be
reduced as simple as possible.

14.3

The output of Translator will be an input of LATEX.

14.4

Discussion

By using PowerEpsilon, mathematician’s working style may be changed. Instead of using merely brain, pencils and papers, they now have computers as
their theorem proving assistants and word processors. Computers can not replace mathematicians. But computer can change their life and help them work
easily and correctly.
If mechanical proof development tools are to see widespread use by the average mathematicians then education of future mathematics becomes important
issue. What new skills, if any, must be taught and whether curriculum changes
are indicated should be examined. Will it suffice that educators place great emphasis on skills, such as computer programming, that typically have not been
considered critical to mathematical structures?
We believe that future mathematics should be standardized in the sense
that all the definitions, axioms, lemmas and theorems should be described in an
uniform way.

Chapter 15

Win-PowerEpsilon: A
Window Based Proof
Development Environment
The logic formulas written in PowerEpsilon is difficult to read, write and
understand. We are trying to develop a window-based proof development environment for providing an user-friendly interface to support formal proof development. The first prototype of system will be implemented on MicroSoft
Windows System 3.1 with Borland C++ 2.0.

15.1

Windows Applications

A Windows application is a special type of program that
• Must be in a special executable (.EXE) file format.
• Runs only under Windows.
• Generally runs in a rectangular window on screen.
• Follows user interface guidelines to appear and perform in a standard way.
• Can run simultaneously with other Windows and non-Windows applications, including other instances of itself.
• Can communicate and share data with other Windows applications.

172

Proof Environment

15.2

173

Benefits of Windows

Windows offers many benefits to both users and developers. Benefits to users
include:
• Standard and preducable operation. If a user know how to use one Windows application, he knows how to use them all.
• No need to set up devices and drivers for each application. Windows
provides drivers to support peripherals.
• Interprocess cooperation and communications.
• Multitasking: the ability to run many applications at once.
• Access to more memory. Windows supports protected mode on the
80286, 80386, and i486; it supports virtual memory on the 80386 and
i486.
Benefits to developers include:
• Device-independent graphics, so graphical applications run on all standard
display adapters.
• Immediate support for a wide range of printers, monitors, and pointing
devices such as mice and trace-balls.
• A rich library of graphics routines.
• More memory for large programs.
• Support for menus, icons, bitmaps, and more.

15.3

Object-Oriented Windowing

Programming for a Windows environment requires familiarity with many new
concepts. Developing even a simple Windows program can be a daunting task.
In Borland C++ 2.0, a package called ObjectWindows is provided to support
users to develop applications for Windows. ObjectWindows simplifies the
process, allowing user to focus on his application’s function, rather than its
form.
ObjectWindows uses the object-oriented features of Borland C++ to
encapsulate parts of the Windows API, insulating user from the details of
Windows programming. As a result, user can write Windows programs with
much less time and effort. Specially, ObjectWindows provides three helpful
features:
• Encapsulation of window information.

Proof Environment

174

• Abstraction of many Windows API functions.
• Automatic message response.

Parent and Child Windows

A full-featured Windows program could have many different types of window
objects (such as windows, controls, and dialogs) associated with it. With the
exception of main window, each window, called a child window, has a single
parent window. A single parent window can have many child windows.
These parental relationships result in a network of related parent and child
windows for each application, with the main window as the ultimate parent. In
this network, most windows serve as a child to one window and as a parent to
others. The group of related child and parent windows for the PowerEpsilon

15.4.3

Dependent and Independent Child Windows

There are two types of child windows. One type is an independent child window.
This type controls its own appearance an location. Messsge boxes and pop-up
windows are independent child windows. The other type is a dependent child
window. This type appears to be stuck to the surface of its parent and moves
around with the parent. Controls, such as the buttons on message boxes, and
some other types of child windows are dependent child windows.

15.6

Porting from VAX/VMS to Windows

Originally, we thought that the porting from Ada implementation in VAX/VMS
to PC based environment in C++ must be relatively easier and straightforward.
However, there have been some difficulties met when porting the system.
• Memory Management: On VAX/VMS, we don’t need to worry about
the memory management problem, since that it is a virtual memory system
totally supported by hardware. However, we don’t have this freedom on
Windows, although it also has a virtual memory system supported purely
in software.
• Debugging: Software debugging is a most difficult task in porting process. Although there have been many good debuggers existed, there is
almost no debugger better than the debugger running on VAX/VMS.

Chapter 16

Testing
According to Webster, a test is “the procedure of submitting a statement to such
conditions or operations as will lead to its proof or disproof or to its acceptance
or rejection.” This classic definition contains two important distinctions.
• First, the tested entity is not presumed to have any validity until the
results of the test are positive.
• Second, the elements of the test – the “conditions or operations” – are
formal and repeatable.
The notion that something isn’t valid until tests prove it so isn’t often applied
to software. In fact, we are often guilty of assuming that the product does
everything it’s supposed to until we are proved wrong. When we rely on users
to be our testers, we are staking our reputations on software that we have been
too busy to test.
That is backwards. The testing stage should be the time we prove what our
application can do. Our application works only if we can prove it, feature by
feature. Deciding to test is an important step.

16.1

Testing Stages

There are two aspects of testing – preparation and execution.

16.1.1

Test Preparation

Preparation is the stage at which we develop the test cases. It takes more
creativity – and therefore more intellectual efforts – than execution. If we are
working from a written set of requirements for the application, most of the test
cases have already been decided, although perhaps in raw form.

177

CHAPTER 16. TESTING

178

The preparation stage of testing permeates the entire development cycle.
Each software requirement generates test cases. The entire test-preparation
phase results in the creation of a test repository. This collection of test cases
is supremely valuable because it represents the set of proofs the developers
will use to prove that their application does what it is supposed to do. The
test repository should be used throughout the life of the application. In fact,
it becomes more valuable over time. Testing a new release becomes merely a
matter of applying the test cases that are still in force and adding those that
are required to test the new features.
For any language systems such as PowerEpsilon, the requirement specification is precisely and formally defined. So it is relatively easier to make
test preparation. The test cases can be classified into three categories: parser
testing, type-checker (static semantics analyzer) testing and dynamic semantics
analyzer testing.

16.1.2

Test Execution

Execution, the second phrase, is the part most often associated with testing.
Test execution is a mind-numbing task of checking everything over and over
until it finally clicks into place as expected.
In most organizations, testing is done during the last few weeks of a project
– or in a few frantic days after the deadline has been passed. The hours are
overtime, and the testing team si most often a motley crew gathered from all
over the organization. Without systematic test cases applied recursively, this is
equivalent to buying a lottery ticket.
At every level, testing requires exact behaviour specifications. The test cases
must not only verify that the program does that it’s supposed to do. They must
also demonstrate that the program doesn’t do what it’s not supposed to do.

16.2

Testing in Windows

Testing in Windows is in some ways easier than testing in a procedural and
character-based environment. The test tool can use the Windows API to query
the various window instances that make up an application and ask what state
they are in. This is true of any GUI, but procedural applications don’t offer this
benefit. But no matter what environment you are testing in, it’s still a matter
of planning, collecting test cases, and applying these test cases systematically.

16.3

Testing Tools

Testing, especially test execution process can benefit tremendously from automation. An automated test tool allows users to apply test cases systematically and efficiently. The test tool never gets tired or bleary-eyed, and, best of

CHAPTER 16. TESTING

179

all, no overtime pay is necessary. As powerful as some test tools are, though –
and some are incredibly powerful – the tool is never more than a tool.
A tool is not a solution. A tool is a means to implement a solution. A
good test tool allows you to build a reusable test repository in a form that can
support three important activities: documentation, execution, and reporting on
the application of test cases.
• Documentation helps to clarify what each test case does and whether it is
sufficient to prove that a requirement has been met.
• Efficient test execution means users can run required test cases easily.
What’s more, it means that they can run the tests hundreds of times to
prove that each release does not violate the integrity of the application’s
features.
• Reporting supplies feedback for the entire organization.
– Development needs to know which test cases failed and where things
went wrong so that they can begin correcting the source code.
– Management needs to know the overall test results so that they can
monitor the performance of the development team and forecast when
the application will be ready for sale or internal use.
– Support needs to know what what was tested so that when customers
call with new situations and problems that weren’t considered by the
original test team, the test suite can be enlarged to cover those issues.
It is best if you can coordinate your test cases with a code-coverage tool that
reports which functions were called (and not called), which code segments (paragraphs) were exercised, and which branches (decision points) were included or
skipped. This provides indisputable evidence of how much of your application
was actually exercised by your collection of test cases, or test suite. Without
this crucial information, even the best test suites typically exercise less than 50
percent of the applications.

Chapter 17

Related Works
The mechanical theorem proving systems can be generally classified into two
categories: the automatic and interactive systems. However, this distinction is
not sharp. Implementers of interactive systems introduce automatic features to
reduce the burden on the user. At the same time, implementers of automatic
systems introduce interactive controls, so the user can assist the system to
discover proofs that are too difficult for it to find automatically. Although
interactive systems are susceptible to gradual automation, most of them are
intended to help the user to check and flesh out a proof he has already outlined
by hand, rather than to discover a new proof. The logical framework embedded
in the automatic systems are more conductive to proof discovery.
We may classify automatic theorem provers according to logical theories they
focus on:
1. Predicative Logic with Equality. Much work has been exploited the resolution [Rob 65] and paramodulation [WosR 69] inference rules for these theories. Recent theorem proving systems in this theory have employed termrewriting systems [KapN 84] and connection methods [Andr 81, Bib 83],
rather than resolution and paramodulation as the primary inference rule.
2. Theories with Induction. A separate body of work focus on proofs requiring the principle of mathematical induction. The Boyer-Moore system
[BoyM 79] is one of the examples of this kind of system.
Most theorem provers based on constructive logics are interactive systems and
have grown out from Martin-Löf’ type theory and LCF. We shall mention some
of them and to compare them with PowerEpsilon system.

180

CHAPTER 17. RELATED WORKS

17.1

181

System F and Its Extensions

In [GirLT 89] Girard defines a powerful extension to Church’s simply typed
lambda-calculus called F and goes on to give a constructive proof of strong
normalization theorem of his system F.

17.2

Second-Order Polymorphic λ-Calculus

A fragment of Girard’s calculus was independently discovered by Reynolds
[Rey 74] who introduced abstraction on type variables and application of functions to types in order to define explicitly polymorphic functions. Reynolds’
calculus is known as the second-order polymorphic λ-calculus.

17.3

Calculus of Constructions and Its Extensions

17.3.1

Calculus of Constructions

The Calculus of Constructions (CC) was introduced by Coquand and Huet
[CoqH 88, Hue 89] as a system for formalizing constructive mathematics. CC
is a higher-order formalism for constructive proofs in natural deduction style.
Every proof is a lambda-expression, typed with propositions of the underlying logic. By removing typed, a pure lambda-expression, expressing its associated algorithm can be obtained. Computing this lambda-expression corresponds
roughly to cut-elimination.
There are many ways to extend CC. We have proposed a possible extension
in PowerEpsilon in which the polymorphic kinds is allowed. Other variations
of CC are also possible.

17.3.2

A Calculus with TYPE : TYPE

One of the possibility is to allow TYPE : TYPE. It may be useful to consider
this calculus as a type system for programming languages. However, Girard has
shown that this calculus does not have normalization property and therefore
the Curry-Howard paradigm seems to be lost forever then, since all propositions
become provable [Coq 86].

17.3.3

A Calculus with a Hierarchy of Universes

Another possible extension is Coquand’s Generalized Calculus of Constructions
[Coq 86] which iterate the idea of a type gathering all the types obtained so
far and form a system with a hierarchy of universes like in Martin-Löf’s system

CHAPTER 17. RELATED WORKS

182

[Mar 82]. In GCC, the ∗ can be thought of as the first universe U0 of MartinLöf. It is then natural to extend the calculus of constructions with a universe
hierarchy U1 such that ∗ is of type U1 , U1 is of type U2 , and so on.
The system GCC is very powerful. It permits to formalize completely the
universe hierarchies in Russel and Whitehead’s Principia Mathematica, including the so-called “typical ambiguity” feature. A complete study of type checking
and typical ambiguity in the context of GCC can be found in [HarpP 89].

17.3.4

An Extended Calculus of Constructions

ECC [Luo 91] integrates Coquand-Huet’s (impredicative) Calculus of Constructions and Martin-Löf’s (predicative) type theory with type universe hierarchies.
ECC is a strong and expressive calculus for formalization of mathematics, structured proof development and program specification.

17.4

Other Works

17.4.1

LCF

Edinburgh LCF [GorMW 79] is a computer system for doing formal proofs
interactively. The acronym LCF stands for “Logic for Computable Functions” –
a logic called PPLAMBDA (Polymorphic Predicate Lambda-Calculus) based
on Scott’s theory for recursively defined functions.
The main aims of designing LCF are as follows:
1. To provide an interactive metalanguage (ML) for conducting proofs, in
which in principle almost any style can be programmed, but which provides the greatest possible security against faulty proofs.
2. To accommodate well a particular proof style usually called “natural deduction” style.
3. To experiment with ML and natural deduction style of proof in the particular calculus PPLAMBDA, in which properties of recursively defined
functions over a wide variety of domains can be quite well formulated and
in which proofs are often found by one of a few good strategies, together
with rather few creative steps supplied by the users.
ML
LCF provide a higher-order functional programming language ML which has
two features:
1. ML has a simple, but flexible, mechanism for raising and handling exceptions; and

CHAPTER 17. RELATED WORKS

183

2. ML has polymorphic type discipline.
ML is designed for programming tactics and tacticals to conduct formal proofs.
PPLAMBDA
PPLAMBDA is the typed lambda-calculus together with fixed point combinators. Each type is interpreted as a domain which is complete partial order.
PPLAMBDA is a family of calculi, each of which is determined by a set of
types, constants and non-logical axioms.

17.4.2

LEAP

LEAP, a “Language with Eval And Polymorphism”, is a strongly-typed functional programming language developed by Pfenning and Lee in Carnegie-Mellon
University [PfeL 89]. The language is an extension of the ω-order polymorphic
lambda-calculus (Fω ) by global definitions of programs and the definition of
versions of REIFY, REFLECT, and EVAL for all of Fω . LEAP is therefore a highly
reflexive language.

17.4.3

Constructor

Constructor [Hel 90] is an interactive proof environment based on the constructive type theory that implements an inference machine which combines the
advantages of Horn clause resolution and higher-order natural deduction style
theorem proving. Both interactive user-guided inference and automatic proof
search are supported by the system.
• In interactive mode, the user may, for a selected goal, choose a clause from
a menu with resolution candidates.
• In automatic mode, the system uses a consecutively bounded depth-first
search strategy. The maximum search depth must be specified interactively. Alternative solutions are generated upon request. The resolution
method itself is used for correctness checking of contexts and local context
introductions. Completed derivations may be added to the global context
as lemmas. To this end, they may be given a name and will be available
for use in subsequent queries. It is possible “freeze” definitions in the
proof development, i.e. to hide their contents and treat them as axioms.
The Constructor system is implemented in Common Lisp running on SUN
3/4 workstations.

CHAPTER 17. RELATED WORKS

17.4.4

184

Nuprl

Nuprl [Const 86] is an interactive proof development environment based on
Martin-Löf’s Type Theory [Mar 75, Mar 82, Mar 84]. It is a significant improvement over LCF proof system. In the system, proofs are constructed top-down
in a sequent calculus, and simultaneously with such construction an associated
algorithm is constructed in the form of a term of the Nuprl language. This term
is known as the extract term of a given proof, and it represents computational
content that may be associated with the statement that has been proved.

17.4.5

ELF

The Edinburgh Logic Framework (ELF) [HarpHP 87] is a formal system for
the representation of logics and formal systems as theories within it, which
was developed by Harper, Honsell and Plotkin. The ELF consists of two components: the λπ-calculus, the λ-calculus with dependent function space (or
product) types; and the principle of judgements as types in which judgements,
the units of inference in logics and other formal systems, are identified with
the types of the λπ-calculus. This work is strongly influenced by the work of
Martin-Löf on judgements in logic. The ELF is suitable for mechanical implementation because the λπ-calculus is decidable. According to [BarH 90], ELF
is a subsystem of CC.

17.4.6

Isabelle

One of the most powerful existing proof systems is Isabelle. [Pau 86, Pau 89,
Pau 88]. The system Isabelle arises from LCF but is a generic theorem prover,
that is, it allows users to describe a new logic and then prove theorems in that
logic.
Isabelle represents its object-logics within a fragment of intuitionistic higher-order logic including implication, universal quantifiers, and equality.
• The implication φ =⇒ ϕ means ‘φ implies ϕ’, and expresses logical entailment.
V
• The quantification x.φ means ‘φ is true for all x’, where x has a fixed
type, and expresses generality in object-rules and axiom schemes.
• The equality a ≡ b means ‘a equals b’, and expresses definitions.
The meta-logic includes the typed λ-calculus, which is convenient for formalizing
the syntax of object-logics, particularly variable binding. Provision of quantifier
rules (of the sort x not free in the assumptions) are enforced by meta-level
quantification.
Like in LCF, backwards proofs are developed using tactics and tacticals,
which are implemented using Standard ML. But an inference rule in LCF is

CHAPTER 17. RELATED WORKS

185

a function from the premises to the conclusion, while in Isabelle it is an axiom
in the meta-logic stating that the premises imply the conclusion.
Since Isabelle axioms are essentially Horn clauses, the proof techniques
draw ideas from PROLOG. Huet’s higher-order unification procedure takes
account of α, β and η-conversions during unification. Higher-order unification
can return multiple or infinitely many results. While the general unification
problem is undecidable, the procedure works well in Isabelle.
Isabelle has been used to formalize various logics, including several firstorder logics, Martin-Löf’s Type Theory, and Zermelo-Fraenkel set theory. Each
new logic is formalized within Isabelle’s meta logic. New types and constants
express the syntax of the logic, while new axioms express its inference rules.

General Relationship of Systems
